can be used by the other parties to infer a next operation, and so on. Indexing the CP maps by the classical transcript
c\in\{0,1\}^{*}
provides a coarse description of
\Lambda
.

2.4
Non-Malleable Codes and Tamper-Detection Codes

We dedicate this section to define classical and quantum non-malleable codes and tamper-detection codes.

2.4.1
Classical Non-Malleable Codes

Consider the following tampering experiment. We are given some message
m
, encode it into some coding scheme
c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(m)
, tamper with it using some function
f:

c^{\prime}=f(c)
, and finally decode it to
\mathsf{Dec}(f(c))=m^{\prime}
. If
\mathsf{Enc}
were an error correcting code robust against
f
, then our goal would be to recover
m^{\prime}=m
. If
\mathsf{Enc}
were an error (or
tamper
) detecting code robust against
f
, we would like either
m^{\prime}=m
or we reject,
m^{\prime}=\bot
. Unfortunately, error correction or detection isn’t possible for every
f
(for instance, constant functions).

Nevertheless, there are cases where the notion of non-malleability can be much more versatile. Dziembowski, Pietrzak, and Wichs formalized a coding-theoretic notion of non-malleability using a simulation paradigm:
m^{\prime}
can be simulated by a distribution that depends only on the adversarial function
f
(and not the message):

Definition 2.12

(Non-Malleable Codes, adapted from
[
DPW18
]
)
.

A pair of (randomized) algorithms
(\mathsf{Enc}:\{0,1\}^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n},\mathsf{Dec}:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{k})
, is an
(\varepsilon,\delta)

non-malleable code
with respect to a family of functions
\mathcal{F}\subset\{f:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}\}
, if the following properties hold:

1.

Correctness:
For every message
m\in\{0,1\}^{k}
,
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\neq m]\leq\delta
.

2.

Non-Malleability:
For every
f\in\mathcal{F}
there is a constant
p_{f}\in[0,1]
and a random variable
D_{f}
on
\{0,1\}^{k}
which is independent of the message, such that

\forall m\in\{0,1\}^{k}:\mathsf{Dec}\circ f\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{f}\cdot m+(1-p_{f})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{f}

(13)

In other words, the outcome of the effective channel on the message is a convex combination of the original message or a fixed distribution.
17
17

17

Non-malleability is originally defined in terms of a simulator which is allowed to output a special symbol
same
∗
which is later replaced by the encoded
m
. The reader may be more accustomed to this notion, nevertheless, it is well-known to be equivalent to the above.
When the code is perfectly correct or its correctness is otherwise clear from context, we simply say non-malleable with error
\varepsilon
. We point out that
[
DKO13
]
showed that NMCs with a single bit message could be defined in an alternative, arguably much simpler way: A classical code is non-malleable if and only if “it is hard to negate the encoded bit with functions from
\mathcal{F}
”. That is,

Theorem 2.3
.

A classical code
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is non-malleable with error
\varepsilon
iff for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\mathbb{P}_{b\leftarrow\{0,1\}}\big[\mathsf{Dec}\circ f\circ\mathsf{Enc}(b)=\neg b\big]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon.

(14)

Note that flipping the encoded bit with probability
\frac{1}{2}
is often trivial: It suffices to swap the message with a copy of an encoding of a random bit. What the above ensures is that
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is non-malleable iff no
f\in\mathcal{F}
can do even marginally better.

Remark 2.2
.

When designing NMCs for classical messages encoded into quantum states, we change the definitions above only slightly. If the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
denotes the codespace, we modify the image and domain of
\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec}
to be
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
, and allow the adversary to tamper with the code using channels
\Lambda:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
.

As previously introduced, the classical
t
-split-state model corresponds to the the collection of individual tampering functions
\{(f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{t})\}
on the shares (for a visual representation of this model with
t=3
, refer to
Figure

3
).

M
M^{\prime}
\mathrm{Enc}
X
X^{\prime}
Y
Y^{\prime}
f_{2}
Z
Z^{\prime}
f_{1}
f_{3}
\mathcal{A}=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
\mathrm{Dec}

Figure 3
:
t
-split-state tampering model for
t=3
.

The notion of an
augmented
non-malleable code naturally extends the split-state model, in that the tampered message
m^{\prime}
is simulatable even
together
with one of the shares of the NMC:

Definition 2.13

(Augmented NMCs)
.

Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a non-malleable code in the
t
-split-state model, and let
X_{i}
denote the random variable corresponding to the
i
th share. Then, we refer to the code as an
augmented non-malleable code
with error
\varepsilon
if there exists
i\in[t]
satisfying: For all
t
-split-state tampering functions
f
, there exists
p_{f}\in[0,1]
and two distributions
\mathcal{X}_{f}
over
\{0,1\}^{|X_{i}|}
and
\mathcal{D}_{f}
over
\{0,1\}^{k}\times\{0,1\}^{|X_{i}|}
, such that

(m^{\prime},X_{i})\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{f}\cdot(m,\mathcal{X}_{f})+(1-p_{f})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{f}.

(15)

Where
p_{f},\mathcal{D}_{f},\mathcal{X}_{f}
only depend on the function
f
.

To instantiate our compilers, we require constructions of classical non-malleable codes in the 2-split-state model (both with and without augmentation). Here we use an existential (inefficient) result by
[
CG13a
]
on capacity-achieving codes, and a recent near-optimal efficient construction by
[
Li23b
]
.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
Li23b
]
)
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an efficient augmented non-malleable code in the
2
-split-state model, which has block length
2n
, rate
k/(2n)=\Omega(1)
, perfect correctness and error
2^{-\Omega(n)}
.

Theorem 2.5

(
[
CG13a
]
, Theorem 3.1)
.

For every sufficiently large block-length
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exist non-malleable codes in the 2-split-state model of rate
1/2-o(1)
, perfect correctness and error
2^{-\Omega(n)}
.

2.4.2
Quantum Secure Non-Malleable Codes and Extractors

To construct our tamper-detection codes in the bounded storage model, we actually require classical split-state non-malleable codes which are
quantum secure
, that is, robust to split-state adversaries who are allowed pre-shared entanglement
\mathsf{LO}_{*}

[
ABJ22
]
. While it is not too conceptually challenging to modify
Definition

2.12
to address quantum adversaries, unfortunately, constructions of high-rate quantum secure non-malleable codes remains an open problem. Instead, in order to optimize the rate of our results, we appeal to fact that we don’t actually require the non-malleability of a worst case message in our codes, just that of a random secret key (see
Section

1.3.2
). For this purpose,
[
BBJ23
,
BGJR23
]
leverage the notion of a
quantum secure, 2-source non-malleable extractor
, and we follow suit.

The connection between 2-source non-malleable extractors and codes was first made explicit by
[
CG13b
]
. Informally, a
2
-source extractor
\mathsf{nmExt}
takes as input two some-what random sources
X
and
Y
, and outputs a near-uniformly random string
R=\mathsf{nmExt}(X,Y)
(which will be our secret key). What makes its non-malleable is that
R
is still near uniformly random even conditioned on any
R^{\prime}=\mathsf{nmExt}(f(X),g(Y))
, which is the outcome of independent tampering functions
f,g
on the sources
X,Y
. In our work we make use of a construction of
quantum secure

2
-source non-malleable extractors by
[
BBJ23
]
:

Fact 2.7

(Quantum Secure
2
-Source Non-malleable Extractor
[
BBJ23
]
)
.

Consider the split-state tampering experiment in
Figure

4
with a split-state tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}})
.
Based on this figure, define
p_{\mathsf{same}}=\mathbb{P}[(X,Y)=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})]_{\hat{\rho}}
and the conditioned quantum states

\rho^{\mathsf{same}}=(\mathsf{nmExt}\otimes\mathsf{nmExt})(\hat{\rho}|(X,Y)=(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}))\text{ and }\rho^{\mathsf{tamp}}=(\mathsf{nmExt}\otimes\mathsf{nmExt})(\hat{\rho}|(X,Y)\neq(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})).

Then, for any
n\geq n_{0}
and constant
\delta>0
, there exists an explicit function
\mathsf{nmExt}:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{\delta n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{r}
with
r=(1/2-\delta)\cdot n
such that for independent sources
X\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
and
Y\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\delta n}
and any such split-state tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}})
,
\mathsf{nmExt}
satisfies

1.

Strong Extraction:
\|\mathsf{nmExt}(X,Y)X-U_{r}\otimes U_{n}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon
and
\|\mathsf{nmExt}(X,Y)Y-U_{r}\otimes U_{\delta n}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon,

2.

Augmented Non-Malleability:
p_{\mathsf{same}}\|\rho^{\mathsf{same}}_{RE_{2}^{\prime}}-U_{r}\otimes\rho^{\mathsf{same}}_{E_{2}^{\prime}}\|_{1}+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\|\rho^{\mathsf{tamp}}_{RR^{\prime}E_{2}^{\prime}}-U_{r}\otimes\rho^{\mathsf{tamp}}_{R^{\prime}E_{2}^{\prime}}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon
,

with
\varepsilon=2^{-n^{\Omega_{\delta}(1)}}
. Furthermore,
\mathsf{nmExt}(x,y)
can be computed in time
{\mathsf{poly}}(n)
.

Item 1 in
2.7
should be the familiar 2-source (strong) extraction guarantee. Item 2 however, captures the
augmented
non-malleability of
\mathsf{nmExt}
. If the tampering attack doesn’t change
X
and
Y
, then
R
should be close to uniformly random even together with any updated side-entanglement
E^{\prime}_{2}
held by one of the adversaries. Conversely, if the tampering attack changed
X
and
Y
, then
R
should be independent of the tampered output
R^{\prime}=\mathsf{nmExt}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
, jointly with
E^{\prime}_{2}
.

We remark that the role of
augmented
non-malleability is more subtle in the quantum secure model. In the classical setting (
Definition

2.13
), the tampering functions are deterministic, and therefore the left share
X
(before tampering) uniquely determines the entire “view” of the left adversary after tampering. In contrast, in the quantum secure model, the entangled state that the split-state adversaries share may apriori collapse and correlate with
both
post-tampered shares
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
- thus not immediately captured by just
X
.

R
E^{\prime}_{2}
\mathsf{nmExt}
\ket{\psi}_{W_{1}W_{2}}
\sigma
\hat{\rho}
\rho
X
X^{\prime}
R^{\prime}
Y
Y^{\prime}
U
V
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,\psi)
E_{1}
E^{\prime}_{1}
E_{2}
E^{\prime}_{2}
{\mathsf{nmExt}}

Figure 4
:
Tampering experiment for quantum secure
2
-source non-malleable extractors.

2.4.3
Quantum Non-Malleable Codes and Augmented Non-Malleable Codes

We now recollect the definition of NMCs from
[
BGJR23
]
for quantum messages, referred to as
quantum non-malleable codes
. For concreteness we state a definition for the
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
adversary model, which can be generalized to any adversary model easily.

\hat{M}
\hat{M}
M
M^{\prime}
\mathrm{Enc}
\sigma
\eta
X
X^{\prime}
Z
Z^{\prime}
Y
Y^{\prime}
V
E^{\prime}_{2}
E_{2}
U
W
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,W,\ket{\psi})
E_{1}
E^{\prime}_{1}
E_{3}
E^{\prime}_{3}
\mathrm{Dec}

Figure 5
:
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{(e_{1},\ldots,e_{t})}:

t
-split tampering model with shared entanglement for
t=3
. The shared entanglement
\psi
is stored in registers
E_{1},E_{2}
and
E_{3}
such that
|E_{i}|\leq e_{i}
for
i\in[3]
.

Let
\sigma_{M}
be an arbitrary state in a message register
M
and
\sigma_{M\hat{M}}
be its canonical purification.
Our coding scheme is given by an encoding Completely Positive Trace-Preserving (CPTP) map

\mathrm{Enc}:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{M})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z})
and a decoding CPTP map
\mathrm{Dec}:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{M})
. Here,
\mathcal{H}_{X},\mathcal{H}_{Y},\mathcal{H}_{Z}
denote the Hilbert spaces for the three shares. The most basic property we require of this coding scheme
(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
is correctness, including preserving entanglement with external systems:

\mathrm{Dec}(\mathrm{Enc}(\sigma_{M\hat{M}}))=\sigma_{M\hat{M}}.

Now, suppose we tamper with the code using a tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
. Recall from
Definition

2.10
that such adversaries are specified by three tampering maps,
U:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E_{1}})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E^{\prime}_{1}})
,
V:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E_{2}})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{Y^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E^{\prime}_{2}})
and
W:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{Z}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E_{3}})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{Z^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E^{\prime}_{3}})
along with a quantum state
\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}}
such that
|E_{i}|\leq e_{i},\ \forall i\in[3]
which captures the pre-shared entanglement between the non-communicating quantum tampering adversaries. After the tampering channel, the decoding procedure
\mathrm{Dec}
is applied to the corrupted codeword
\tau_{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}Z^{\prime}}
and stored in register
M^{\prime}
.

Figure

5
presents a diagram of this tampering model.
Let

\eta=\mathrm{Dec}((U\otimes V\otimes W)\left(\mathrm{Enc}(\sigma_{M\hat{M}})\otimes\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\right))

be the final state after applying the tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}
followed by the decoding procedure. The non-malleability of the coding scheme
(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
is defined as follows.

Definition 2.14

(Quantum Non-Malleable Code against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}

[
BGJR23
]
)
.

See
Figure

5
for the tampering experiment. We say that the coding scheme

(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
is an
\varepsilon
-secure quantum non-malleable code
against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
if for every
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,W,\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}})
and for every quantum message
\sigma_{M}
(with canonical purification
\sigma_{M\hat{M}}
) it holds that

\eta_{M^{\prime}\hat{M}}\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}})\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}},

(16)

where
p_{\mathcal{A}}\in[0,1]
and
\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}}
depend only on the tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,W,\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}})
.

An important restriction to the definition above is known as “average-case” non-malleability:

Remark 2.3

(
[
BGJR23
]
)
.

If
Equation

16
is only guaranteed to hold if
\sigma_{M}
is the maximally mixed state, then
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is referred to as an
average-case
\varepsilon
-secure quantum non-malleable code
.

In our reductions, we require guarantees on the correlations between the recovered message, and the registers of individual split-state adversaries (even after tampering). We introduce the following notion of augmented quantum non-malleable codes, in an analog to its classical counterpart (
Definition

2.13
).

Definition 2.15

(Augmented Quantum Non-Malleable Code against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
)
.

See
Figure

5
for the tampering experiment. We say that the coding scheme

(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
is an
\varepsilon
-secure augmented quantum non-malleable code
against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
if there exists
i\in[3]
such that for every
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,W,\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}})
and message
\sigma_{M}
(with canonical purification
\sigma_{M\hat{M}}
), it holds that

\eta_{M^{\prime}\hat{M}E^{\prime}_{i}}\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}\otimes\zeta^{\mathcal{A}}_{E^{\prime}_{i}}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}})\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}_{i}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}},

(17)

where
p_{\mathcal{A}}\in[0,1]
,
\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\zeta^{\mathcal{A}}_{E^{\prime}_{i}}
depend only on the tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,W,\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}})
.

In other words, the
i
th adversaries “view”
E^{\prime}_{i}
is simulatable jointly with the message.

2.4.4
Quantum Tamper-Detection Codes

Finally, we formally define the notion of quantum tamper-detection codes. Following the previous subsection, for concreteness we present an explicit definition for
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{t})}
tampering adversaries with
t=3
, which can be easily generalized to the different tampering models of
Section

2.3
.

Definition 2.16

(Quantum Tamper-Detection Code against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
)
.

See
Figure

5
for the tampering experiment. We say that the coding scheme

(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
is an
\varepsilon
-secure quantum tamper-detection code
against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
if for every
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,W,\ket{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}E_{3}})
and for every quantum message
\sigma_{M}
(with canonical purification
\sigma_{M\hat{M}}
)
it holds that

\eta_{M^{\prime}\hat{M}}\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}})\bot_{M^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}},

(18)

where
p_{\mathcal{A}}\in[0,1]
depend only on the tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}
and
\bot_{M^{\prime}}
denotes a special abort symbol stored in register
M^{\prime}
to denote tamper detection.

2.5
Secret Sharing Schemes and their Variants

In this subsection we present basic definitions of secret sharing schemes, in addition to known constructions in the literature that we require to instantiate our compilers. We begin in
Section

2.5.1
by introducing threshold secret sharing, proceeded by leakage-resilient secret sharing in
Section

2.5.2
, and non-malleable secret sharing in
Section

2.5.3
. Finally, in
Section

2.5.4
we formally introduce the tamper detecting secret sharing schemes we construct in this work.

2.5.1
Threshold Secret Sharing Schemes

Informally, in a
t
-out-of-
p
secret sharing scheme, any
t
honest shares suffice to reconstruct the secret, but no
t-1
shares offer any information about it.

Definition 2.17

(
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c})
-Secret Sharing Scheme)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a finite set of secrets, where
|\mathcal{M}|\geq 2
. Let
[p]=\{1,2,\cdots,p\}
be a set of identities (indices) of
p
parties. A sharing channel
\mathsf{Enc}
with domain of secrets
\mathcal{M}
is a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c})

threshold secret sharing scheme
if the following two properties hold:

1.

Correctness
: the secret can be reconstructed by any set of parties
T\subset[p],|T|\geq t
. That is, for every such
T
, there exists a reconstruction channel
\mathsf{Dec}_{T}
such that

\forall m\in\mathcal{M}:\text{ }\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)_{T}\neq m]\leq\varepsilon_{c}

(19)

2.

Statistical Privacy
: Any collusion of
|T|\leq t-1
parties has “almost” no information about the underlying secret. That is, for every distinguisher
D
with binary output:

\forall m_{0},m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}:\text{ }|\mathbb{P}[D(\mathsf{Enc}(m_{0})_{T})=1]-\mathbb{P}[D(\mathsf{Enc}(m_{1})_{T})=1]|\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}

(20)

We point out that we use the same syntactic definition of secret sharing for schemes which hide classical messages into classical or quantum ciphers. However, when the cipher is classical, we refer to the encoding/decoding channels as
\mathsf{Share}
and
\mathsf{Rec}
(reconstruction) functions, as opposed to quantum channels
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
. The block-length of the secret sharing scheme is the total qubit
+
bit-length of all the shares, and its rate as the ratio of message length (in bits) to block-length.

To instantiate our constructions, we use Shamir’s standard threshold secret sharing scheme:

Fact 2.8

(
[
Sha79
]
)
.

For any number of parties
p
and threshold
t
such that
t\leq p
, there exists a
t
-out-of-
p
secret sharing scheme
(\mathsf{Share},\mathsf{Rec})
for classical messages of length
b
with share size at most
\max(p,b)
, where both the sharing and reconstruction procedures run in time poly
(p,b)
.

2.5.2
Leakage-Resilient Secret Sharing Schemes

Integral in our constructions are classical secret sharing schemes which offer privacy guarantees even if unauthorized subsets are allowed to leak information about their shares to each other in an attempt to distinguish the message. Moreover, we depart from standard classical leakage models in that the leaked information itself could be a quantum state, even if the shares are classical. The definition of leakage-resilient secret sharing formalizes this idea as follows:

Definition 2.18

(Leakage-Resilient Secret Sharing)
.

Let
(\mathsf{Share},\mathsf{Rec})
be a secret sharing scheme with randomized sharing function
\mathsf{Share}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\{\{0,1\}^{l^{\prime}}\}^{p}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a family of leakage channels. Then
\mathsf{Share}
is said to be
(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon_{lr})
leakage-resilient if, for every channel
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
,

\forall m_{0},m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}:\text{ }\Lambda(\mathsf{Share}(m_{0}))\approx_{\varepsilon_{lr}}\Lambda(\mathsf{Share}(m_{1}))

(21)

As an example, the standard local leakage model in the context of classical secret sharing schemes allows bounded leakage queries
\{f_{i}:\{0,1\}^{l^{\prime}}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\mu}\}_{i\in K}
, on each share corresponding to an arbitrary set of indices
K\subset[n]
, and further allows full share queries corresponding to an unauthorised subset
T\subset[n]
:

\mathsf{Leak}(\mathsf{Share}(m))=\mathsf{Share}(m)_{T},\{f_{i}(\mathsf{Share}(m)_{i})\}_{i\in K}.

(22)

In our constructions, we unfortunately require a slight modification to this setting, where the leakage parties
K
are an unauthorized subset, but are allowed to
jointly
leak a small quantum state (dependent on their shares) to
T
. We formally introduce this model as follows:

Definition 2.19

(Quantum
k
-Local Leakage Model)
.

For any integer sizes
p,t,k
and leakage length (in qubits)
\mu
, we define the
(p,t,k,\mu)
-local leakage model to be the collection of channels specified by

\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{p,t}=\bigg\{(T,K,\Lambda):T,K\subset[p],|T|<t,|K|\leq k,\text{ and }\Lambda:\{0,1\}^{l^{\prime}\cdot|K|}\rightarrow{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H})\bigg\},

(23)

where
\log\dim(\mathcal{H})=\mu
. A leakage query
(T,K,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{p,t}
on a secret
m
is the density matrix:

(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}(m)_{T\cup K})

(24)

In other words, the parties in
K
perform a quantum channel on their (classical) shares and send the
\mu
qubit output state to
T
. To the extent of our knowledge, in the literature we do not know of
\mathsf{LRSS}
constructions in this model (even with classical leakage). However, in
Appendix

C
we show that simple modifications to a construction of
\mathsf{LRSS}
against local leakage by
[
CKOS22
]
provides such guarantees.

Theorem 2.6

(
Theorem

C.1
, restatement)
.

For every
\mu,k,t,p,l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k<t,t+k\leq p,
and
p\leq l
, there exists an
(p,t,0,0)
threshold secret sharing scheme on messages of
l
bits and shares of size
l+\mu+o(l,\mu)
bits, which is perfectly correct and private and
p\cdot 2^{-\tilde{\Omega}(\sqrt[3]{\frac{l+\mu}{p}})}
leakage-resilient against
\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{p,t}
.

2.5.3
Non-Malleable Secret Sharing Schemes

[
GK18a
]
introduced the notion of a non-malleable secret sharing scheme (NMSS). An NMSS is a secret sharing scheme which is robust to certain types of tampering on the shares.

Definition 2.20

(
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c},\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}})
Non-Malleable Secret Sharing Scheme)
.

Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c})
secret sharing scheme, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a family of tampering channels. Then, we refer to
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
as a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c},\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}})

non-malleable secret sharing scheme
against
\mathcal{F}
if, for all
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
and authorized subset
T\subset[p]
of size
|T|\geq t
, there exists
p_{\Lambda}\in[0,1]
and a distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}
such that

\forall m\in\mathcal{M},\quad\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}}p_{\Lambda}\cdot m+(1-p_{\Lambda})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\Lambda}

(25)

In other words, the recovered secret after tampering is a convex combination of the original secret, or a fixed distribution over secrets. We leverage a construction of non-malleable secret sharing schemes against
joint tampering functions
by
[
GK18a
]
.

Definition 2.21

(Joint Tampering Functions
\mathcal{F}_{p,t}^{\text{joint}}
)
.

For any
t
-out-of-
p
secret sharing scheme, the adversary chooses any
t
-out-of-
p
shares to obtain an authorized set
T
, partitions the set
T=A\cup B
into any two non-empty disjoint subsets which have different cardinalities, and jointly tampers with shares in each of the two subsets arbitrarily and independently using deterministic functions
f_{A},f_{B}
.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
GK18a
]
)
.

For every number of parties
p
, threshold
t\geq 3
, and sufficiently large block-length
b
, there exists a
(p,t,0,0,2^{-b^{\Omega(1)}})-\mathsf{NMSS}
against
\mathcal{F}_{p,t}^{\text{joint}}
of rate
p^{-O(1)}
.

2.5.4
Tamper Detecting Secret Sharing Schemes

Below we formalize two tamper detection properties for the secret sharing schemes that we consider in this section. The first of the two is known as “weak” tamper detection, and informally can be understood as the ability to
either
recover the message or reject after tampering, with high probability. However, the probability that either of these two events occurs may depend on
m
:

Definition 2.22

(Weak Tamper-Detecting Secret Sharing Schemes)
.

Let a family of CPTP maps
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c})
secret sharing scheme over
k
bit messages and
\mathcal{F}
a family of tampering channels. We refer to
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
as
(\mathcal{F},r,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{TD}})

weak tamper-detecting
if, for every subset
R\subset[p]
of size
|R|\geq r
and tampering channel
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
,

\forall m\in\{0,1\}^{k},\quad\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{R}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\notin\{m,\bot\}]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{TD}}\text{ }

(26)

Note that in the above, we implicitly consider
ramp
secret sharing schemes, where the threshold for privacy, reconstruction under honest shares, and reconstruction under tampered shares, are different. In a (generic) tamper-detecting secret sharing scheme, we stipulate that the distribution over the recovered message is near convex combination of the original
m
and
\bot
, which doesn’t depend on
m
:

Definition 2.23

(Tamper-Detecting Ramp Secret Sharing Schemes)
.

Let a family of CPTP maps
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c})
secret sharing scheme over
k
bit messages and
\mathcal{F}
a family of tampering channels. We refer to
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
as
(\mathcal{F},r,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{TD}})

tamper-detecting
if, for every subset
R\subset[p]
of size
|R|\geq r
and tampering channel
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
, there exists a constant
p_{\Lambda}\in[0,1]
such that

\forall m\in\{0,1\}^{k},\quad\mathsf{Dec}_{R}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{TD}}}p_{\Lambda}\cdot m+(1-p_{\Lambda})\cdot\bot\text{ }

(27)

2.6
Bipartite and Multipartite
\mathsf{LOCC}
Data Hiding Schemes

In this subsection we present basic definitions of multi-partite quantum measurements restricted to local operations and quantum communication, as well as formal definitions of quantum data hiding schemes.

2.6.1
Bipartite Data Hiding

Definition 2.24
.

Let
\rho,\sigma
be two apriori equiprobable bipartite mixed states, and define the distance
\|\rho-\sigma\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}}=4\cdot(p-\frac{1}{2})
, where
p
is the probability of correctly distinguishing the states, maximised over all decision procedures that can be implemented using only local operations and classical communication (
\mathsf{LOCC}
).

Remark 2.4
.

Notions of distance based on restricted classes of measurements define induced norms on operators (below) only when the set of measurements
\mathbb{M}
is a closed, convex subset of the operator interval
\{M:0\leq M\leq\mathbb{I}\}
containing
\mathbb{I}
and such that
\mathbb{I}-M\in\mathbb{M}
. See
[
MW14
]
for a detailed description.

\|X\|_{\mathbb{M}}=\max_{M\in\mathbb{M}}\Tr(2M-\mathbb{I})X

(28)

The set of
\mathsf{LOCC}
measurements is
not
topologically closed
[
CLM+12
]
, and therefore the distance in
Definition

2.24
is not a norm. Nevertheless, it does satisfy the triangle inequality.

Definition 2.25

(
\mathsf{LOCC}
Data Hiding Schemes
[
TDL00
,
HLSW03
]
)
.

A bipartite
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme is a pair of quantum channels
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
where
\mathsf{Enc}:\{0,1\}^{k}\rightarrow{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
and
\mathsf{Dec}:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{k}
satisfying:

1.

Correctness
:
\forall m\in\{0,1\}^{k}:\mathbb{P}\big[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\neq m\big]\leq\delta
.

2.

Security
:
\forall m_{0},m_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{k}:

\|\mathsf{Enc}(m_{0})-\mathsf{Enc}(m_{1})\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}}\leq\varepsilon

To instantiate our code construction, we use two pre-existing constructions of bipartite data hiding schemes. The first of which is an inefficient, high-rate result by
[
HLSW03
]
:

Theorem 2.8

(
[
HLSW03
]
)
.

For every sufficiently large integer
n
, there exists a bipartite
(2^{-\Omega(\frac{n}{\log n})},2^{-\Omega(\frac{n}{\log n})})
-
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme encoding
n/2-o(n)
bits into
n
qubits.

And the second is an efficient construction by
[
EW02
]
, which furthermore is
separable
.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
EW02
]
)
.

For every
k,\lambda=\Omega(\log k)\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a 2-out-of-2
(2^{-\Omega(\lambda)},2^{-\Omega(\lambda)})

\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme encoding
k
bits into
n=2\cdot k\cdot\lambda^{2}
qubits. Moreover, the data hiding states are separable states.

2.6.2
Data Hiding with an Access Structure

The study of multi-partite data hiding schemes, endowed with some access structure
\mathcal{A}
, can be traced to
[
EW02
,
DHT02
,
HLS04
]
. Recall an access structure
\mathcal{A}
is simply a family of subsets of
[p]
, where
p
denotes the number of parties/registers in the data hiding scheme. A subset of parties
T\in\mathcal{A}
implies that the parties in
T
, using quantum communication between each other, can recover the secret.

However, if
T\notin\mathcal{A}
, then the data hiding scheme offers a notion of privacy which strengthens the standard secret-sharing definition: no unauthorized party
T
can learn information about the secret even if they are allowed arbitrary classical communication with their complement
[p]\setminus T
, in addition to quantum communication within
T
. Here, the parties in
[p]\setminus T
are restricted to
\mathsf{LOCC}
themselves.

Definition 2.26
.

Fix a number
p
of parties and a subset
T\subset[p]
of them. We refer to the set of CPTP channels
\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}^{p}
as the set of operations implementable using local operations and quantum communication within
T
, as well as local operations and classical communication between
T
, and each individual
u\in[p]\setminus T
. When no quantum communication is allowed, we refer to the set of channels as
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
.

Following
Section

7
, given any two apriori equiprobable density matrices
\rho,\sigma
supported on an
p
partite Hilbert space, we denote
\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\sigma\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}^{p}}
as the optimal bias in distinguishing between the two using binary outcome distinguishers
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}^{p}
.

Definition 2.27

(
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\varepsilon_{c})
-
\mathsf{LOCC}
Secret Sharing Scheme)
.

A pair of channels
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is an
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\varepsilon_{c})
-
\mathsf{LOCC}
secret sharing scheme if it satisfies

1.

Correctness
: The secret can be reconstructed by any set of parties
T\subset[p],|T|\geq t
using quantum communication within
T
. That is, for every
T
there exists a reconstruction channel
\mathsf{Dec}_{T}
such that for all messages
m
:

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{T}(m)\neq m]\leq\varepsilon_{c}

(29)

2.

Security
: Any collusion of
|T|\leq t-1
parties has “almost” no information about the underlying secret, even if the remaining
[p]\setminus T
parties can aid them using arbitrary classical communication. That is, for every pair of messages
m_{0},m_{1}
,

\|\mathsf{Enc}(m_{0})-\mathsf{Enc}(m_{1})\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}^{p}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}.

(30)

3
From Non-Malleability to Tamper Detection against
\mathsf{LO}

We dedicate this section to our two black-box reductions that construct tamper-detection codes (TDCs) from non-malleable codes (NMCs) in the split state model. We refer the reader to
Section

2.3
for formal definitions of the relevant tampering models, namely the unentangled
t
-split-state model
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
, and the bounded storage model
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{a}
. Our first result shows that any non-malleable code in the unentangled split-state model implies a tamper-detection code in the unentangled split-state model, but with one extra share.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let the coding scheme
(\mathsf{Enc}^{t},\mathsf{Dec}^{t})
be a quantum NMC against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
, with message length
k
qubits, blocklength
n
, and error
\varepsilon
. Then, for every
0<\lambda<k
, there exists a quantum TDC against tampering adversaries
\mathsf{LO}^{t+1}
of message length
k-\lambda
, blocklength
n+\lambda
, and error
\varepsilon+2^{1-\lambda}
.

Our second result shows the reduction above can be made robust against adversaries with a bounded amount of entanglement, highlighting that tamper detection is not solely an artifact of the unentangled model. The challenge in extending our ideas lies in the fact that quantum NMCs are, a priori, not composable. Their definition offers no guarantees on any entanglement that the adversaries may have with the quantum message, both before or after tampering. Consequently, adversaries with shared entanglement may correlate their attack on the additional
(t+1)
st share, with their attack on the NMC.

To address this challenge, we introduce the concept of an “augmented” quantum NMC, drawing an analogy to their classical counterparts. In a standard split-state NMC, the decoded message is either perfectly recovered or entirely fixed. In an augmented NMC, the decoded message, in conjunction with the left share, is either recovered and remains independent or are jointly fixed. We formalized this notion in the quantum setting, accounting for any pre-shared entanglement, in
Definition

2.15
. We can now state our reduction in the “bounded storage model”, denoted as
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{(e_{1},\cdots,e_{t})}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let the coding scheme
(\mathsf{Enc}^{t},\mathsf{Dec}^{t})
be an quantum NMC against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{t})}
, with message length
k
qubits, blocklength
n
, and error
\varepsilon
, which is
augmented
at the first share.

Then, for every
0\leq\lambda<k
and
0\leq a\leq\min(\lambda,e_{1})
there exists a quantum TDC against
\mathsf{LO}^{(t+1)}_{(e_{1}-a,\ldots,e_{t},e_{t+1}=a)}
, of message length
k-\lambda
, blocklength
n+\lambda
, and error
\varepsilon+2^{1+a-\lambda}
.

In particular, if
(\mathsf{Enc}^{t},\mathsf{Dec}^{t})
is secure against arbitrary pre-shared entanglement
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{*}
, then the result is secure against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{(*,*,\cdots,a)}
. We defer instantiations of the reductions above, as well as explicit constructions of the augmented quantum NMCs to
Appendix

B
.

3.1
Quantum Tamper-Detection Codes against
\mathsf{LO}

Let the coding scheme
(\mathsf{Enc}^{t},\mathsf{Dec}^{t})
be a quantum non-malleable code (NMC) against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
, with message length
k
qubits, blocklength
n
, and error
\varepsilon
. In
Algorithm

5
, for every integer
0<\lambda<k
, we define a quantum tamper-detection code
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
against
\mathsf{LO}^{t+1}
:

Input:

Any input state
\sigma_{M}
(with canonical purification register
\hat{M}
) such that
|M|=k-\lambda
and an integer
\lambda
.

1

2

1:

Prepare
\lambda
EPR pairs,
\Phi^{\otimes\lambda}\equiv\Phi_{E\hat{E}}
, on a bipartite register
E,\hat{E}
.

3

2:

Encode
\sigma_{M}
and the EPR halves in register
E
into the quantum NMC,
\mathsf{Enc}^{t}
.

4

3:

Output the
t
-splits, and the remaining EPR halves in
\hat{E}
in the
(t+1)
st split.

\mathsf{Enc}(\sigma_{M})=\left((\mathsf{Enc}^{t})_{ME}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{\hat{E}}\right)(\sigma_{M}\otimes\Phi_{E\hat{E}})

(31)

Algorithm 1

\mathsf{Enc}
: A quantum TDC defined on
t+1
registers.

Input:

Any code-state
\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\sigma_{M})
on
(t+1)
shares, tampered by a channel
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{t+1}
.

1

2

1:

Decode the quantum NMC in the first
t
shares using
\mathsf{Dec}^{t}
, resulting in registers
M^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}
.

3

2:

Perform an
\mathsf{EPR}
test on registers
E^{\prime}\hat{E}
. This entails a binary outcome measurement with two possible outcomes:
{\Pi=\Phi_{E\hat{E}},\bar{\Pi}=\mathbb{I}_{E\hat{E}}-\Phi_{E\hat{E}}}
.

4

3:

An acceptance decision is made if the outcome is
\Pi=\Phi_{E\hat{E}}
. In this case, the operation
\mathsf{Rep}
(stands for ’Replace’) outputs the register
M^{\prime}
.

5

4:

If the outcome is
\bar{\Pi}
, the operation
\mathsf{Rep}
outputs a special symbol
\bot
in register
M^{\prime}
.

Algorithm 2

\mathsf{Dec}
.

3.1.1
Proof of
Theorem

3.1

Let us denote the tampering adversary
\mathsf{LO}^{(t+1)}
as
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\ldots,\Lambda_{t+1})
, and its restriction to the first
t
shares as
\mathcal{A}^{t}=(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\ldots,\Lambda_{t})
. We depict the tampering process of
\mathcal{A}
on our code
(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
in
Figures

6
and
7
. These two figures describe the same tampering experiment, but only differ in that in
Figure

7
, we have delayed the action of
\Lambda_{t+1}
to introduce an intermediate state
\tau
.
\tau
represents the state of the system after
\mathcal{A}^{t}
has tampered with the first
t
shares and we have decoded the non-malleable code, but before the action of
\Lambda_{t+1}
.

\hat{M}
\hat{M}
M
M^{\prime}
\mathsf{Rep}
M^{\prime}
O
\eta
E
\hat{E}
\mathrm{Enc}^{t}
\sigma
\nu
X
X^{\prime}
E^{\prime}
X_{t}
X_{t}^{\prime}
\Lambda_{1}
\Lambda_{t}
\Lambda_{t+1}
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda_{1},\cdots,\Lambda_{t+1})
\mathrm{Dec}^{t}
\mathsf{EPR}
\hat{E}^{\prime}

Figure 6
:
Quantum TDC against
\mathsf{LO}^{t+1}
.

\hat{M}
\hat{M}
M
M^{\prime}
\mathsf{Rep}
M^{\prime}
O
\eta
E
\hat{E}
\mathrm{Enc}^{t}
\sigma
{\tau}
\nu
X_{1}
X_{1}^{\prime}
E^{\prime}
X_{t}
X_{t}^{\prime}
\Lambda_{1}
\Lambda_{t}
\Lambda_{t+1}
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda_{1},\cdots,\Lambda_{t+1})
\mathrm{Dec}^{t}
\mathsf{EPR}
\hat{E}^{\prime}

Figure 7
:
Analysis of quantum TDC against
\mathsf{LO}^{t+1}
.

Since the state
\tau
is derived after tampering against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
, the security of the quantum NMC against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
guarantees:

\tau_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}E^{\prime}\hat{E}}\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}\otimes\Phi_{E\hat{E}}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}})\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\Phi_{\hat{E}},

where
(p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}},\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}})
depend only on tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}^{t}
. The data processing inequality
2.1
now tells us that the Bell basis measurement in
\mathsf{EPR}
is either completely independent of the the message
M
, or acts on a product state:

\nu_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}O}\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}\sigma_{\hat{M}M}\otimes\mathsf{EPR}(\Lambda_{t+1}(\Phi_{E\hat{E}}))+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}})\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\mathsf{EPR}(\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}}\otimes\Lambda_{t+1}(\Phi_{\hat{E}})).

(32)

To proceed, we state two claims,
3.3
and
3.4
, which enable us to analyze the two terms in the convex combination above. In
3.3
, we point out the (trivial) observation that if the non-malleable code recovers the message
\sigma_{\hat{M}M}
, then the Bell basis measurement is independent of
\sigma
:

Claim 3.3
.

\mathsf{EPR}(\Lambda_{t+1}(\Phi_{E\hat{E}}))=p_{\Lambda_{t+1}}\outerproduct{1}{1}+(1-p_{\Lambda_{t+1}})\outerproduct{0}{0}
, where
p_{\Lambda_{t+1}}
, depends only on
\Lambda_{t+1}
.

In
3.4
, we formalize the fact that the Bell basis measurement rejects product states with high probability. We state its proof in
Section

3.1.2
.

Claim 3.4
.

\mathsf{EPR}(\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}}\otimes\Lambda_{t+1}(\Phi_{\hat{E}}))\approx_{2\cdot 2^{-\lambda}}\zeta_{M^{\prime}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}
, for some density matrix
\zeta_{M^{\prime}}
.

By combining
3.3
,
3.4
and
Equation

32
, we are guaranteed the decoder outputs a convex combination of the original message
\sigma_{\hat{M}M}
or the Bell basis measurement rejects:

\nu_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}O}\approx_{\varepsilon+2\cdot 2^{-\lambda}}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{\Lambda_{t+1}}\sigma_{\hat{M}M}\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1}+p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}(1-p_{\Lambda_{t+1}})\sigma_{\hat{M}M}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}})\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\zeta_{M^{\prime}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}.

We can now conclude using the data processing
2.1
:

\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\sigma_{\hat{M}M})=\eta_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}}\approx_{\varepsilon+2\cdot 2^{-\lambda}}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{\Lambda_{t+1}}\sigma_{\hat{M}M}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{\Lambda_{t+1}})\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\bot_{M^{\prime}},

which implies
Theorem

3.1
, since the probability
p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{\Lambda_{t+1}}
depends only on the tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

3.1.2
Proof of
3.4

We point out that
\phi_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}\hat{E}}=\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}}\otimes\Lambda_{t+1}(\Phi_{\hat{E}})
is a product state, and therefore can be written as a convex combination of pure states of rank
1
across the cut
(M^{\prime}E^{\prime})/\hat{E}
. Using
3.5
below, we prove
\mathsf{EPR}(\phi_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}\hat{E}})
outputs outcome
0
with probability at least
1-2^{-\lambda}
.

Claim 3.5
.

Let

\Phi_{E\hat{E}}\equiv\Phi^{\otimes\lambda}
denote

\lambda
EPR pairs on a bipartite register
E,\hat{E}
. Let
\tau_{E\hat{E}}
be a pure state of Schmidt rank
R
. Then, measuring
\tau
with the binary measurement
\{\Phi_{E\hat{E}},\mathbb{I}_{E\hat{E}}-\Phi_{E\hat{E}}\}
outputs the
\Phi_{E\hat{E}}
with negligible probability, i.e.,

\Tr[\Phi^{\otimes\lambda}\tau]\leq R\cdot 2^{-\lambda}.

Similarly, if
\tau_{E\hat{E}}
were a mixed state with Schmidt number
R
, then
\Tr[\Phi^{\otimes\lambda}\tau]\leq R\cdot 2^{-\lambda}.

Recall that the Schmidt rank/number (
Definition

2.7
) is non-increasing under local operations, and so if
\phi_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}\hat{E}}
has rank 1 across the cut
(M^{\prime}E^{\prime})/\hat{E}
, the RDM
\phi_{E^{\prime}\hat{E}}
has Schmidt number
1
.
3.4
follows.

Proof.

[of
3.5
] Let us first consider a pure state
\ket{\phi}
of rank
R
, and let
\ket{\phi}=\sum_{i=1}^{R}\alpha_{i}\ket{u_i}_{E}\otimes\ket{v_i}_{\hat{E}}
,
\Phi_{E\hat{E}}\equiv\ket{\Phi}^{\otimes\lambda}=2^{-\lambda/2}\sum_{j}\ket{j}_{E}\otimes\ket{j}_{\hat{E}}
define Schmidt decompositions. Then, by the triangle and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequalities:

\displaystyle\Tr[\Phi^{\otimes\lambda}\phi]^{1/2}=|\bra{\Phi}^{\otimes\lambda}\ket{\phi}|\leq\sum_{i}^{R}|\alpha_{i}|\cdot|\bra{\Phi}^{\otimes\lambda}\ket{u_i}\otimes\ket{v_i}|\leq

(33)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\ket{u}\otimes\ket{v}}|\bra{\Phi}^{\otimes\lambda}\ket{u}\otimes\ket{v}|\cdot R^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{i}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq R^{1/2}\cdot\max_{\ket{u}\otimes\ket{v}}|\bra{\Phi}^{\otimes\lambda}\ket{u}\otimes\ket{v}|

(34)

In turn,

\displaystyle|\bra{\Phi}^{\otimes\lambda}\ket{u}\otimes\ket{v}|=2^{-\lambda/2}\cdot\bigg|\sum_{j}\bra{j}\ket{u}\cdot\bra{j}\ket{v}\bigg|\leq 2^{-\lambda/2}\bigg(\sum_{j}\cdot|\bra{j}\ket{u}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{j}|\bra{j}\ket{v}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\leq 2^{-\lambda/2},

(35)

which gives us the desired bound. If
\tau
is a mixed state of Schmidt number
R
, then it can be written as a convex combination of pure states of Schmidt rank
\leq R
, which concludes the claim.
∎

augmented
non-malleable code
(\mathsf{Enc}_{t},\mathsf{Dec}_{t})
against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots e_{t})}
tampering adversaries.

.

\mathsf{LO}^{(t+1)}_{(e_{1}-a,e_{2},\ldots,e_{t+1}=a)}
as
\mathcal{A}=(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\ldots,\Lambda_{t+1},\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{E_{1}E_{2}\ldots E_{t+1}})
, and its “truncation” to the first

3.2.1

3.2

. Note that they only differ in that in

\Lambda_{t+1}

. Informally

\Lambda_{t+1}
.

E_{1}

Figure 9
:

.

By bundling together the (augmented) first register
E_{1}
with the entanglement in the new register
E_{t+1}
, note that
|E_{1}E_{t+1}|\leq a+(e_{1}-a)=e_{1}
. The security of the augmented quantum non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}_{(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{t})}
ensures the following about state
\tau
:

\tau_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}E^{\prime}\hat{E}E_{t+1}E^{\prime}_{1}}\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}\otimes\Phi_{E\hat{E}}\otimes\zeta_{E_{t+1}E^{\prime}_{1}}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}})\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}E^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\Phi_{\hat{E}},

where
p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}},\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}E^{\prime}_{1}}
and
\zeta_{E_{t+1}E^{\prime}_{1}}
depend only on adversary
\mathcal{A}^{t}
. Using data processing
2.1
, we have

\nu_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}O}\approx_{\varepsilon}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}\sigma_{\hat{M}M}\otimes\mathsf{EPR}(\Tr_{E^{\prime}_{t+1}}\left(\Lambda_{t+1}(\Phi_{E\hat{E}}\otimes\zeta_{E_{t+1}})\right))+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}})\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\mathsf{EPR}(\Tr_{E^{\prime}_{t+1}}\left(\Lambda_{t+1}(\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}}\otimes\Phi_{\hat{E}})\right)).

(36)

To proceed, we state two claims,
3.6
and
3.7
, which enable us to analyze the two terms in the convex combination above. In
3.6
, we point out the (trivial) observation that if the non-malleable code recovers the message
\sigma_{\hat{M}M}
, then the Bell basis measurement is independent of
\sigma
:

Claim 3.6
.

\mathsf{EPR}(\Tr_{E^{\prime}_{t+1}}\left(\Lambda_{t+1}(\Phi_{E\hat{E}}\otimes\zeta_{E_{t+1}})\right))=p_{{t+1}}\outerproduct{1}{1}+(1-p_{{t+1}})\outerproduct{0}{0}
, where
p_{{t+1}}
, depends only on
(\Lambda_{t+1},\zeta_{E_{t+1}})
.

In turn,
3.7
formalizes that the Bell basis measurement rejects states of low Schmidt rank.

Claim 3.7
.

\mathsf{EPR}(\Tr_{E^{\prime}_{t+1}}\left(\Lambda_{t+1}(\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}}\otimes\Phi_{\hat{E}})\right))\approx_{2^{1+a-\lambda}}\kappa_{M^{\prime}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}
, where
\kappa_{M^{\prime}}
is some density matrix, so long as the size of the
(t+1)
st register is
|E_{t+1}|\leq a.

Once again by combining
3.6
,
3.7
and
Equation

36
, we are guaranteed the decoder outputs a convex combination of the original message
\sigma_{\hat{M}M}
or the Bell basis measurement rejects:

\nu_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}O}\approx_{\varepsilon+2^{1+a-\lambda}}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{{t+1}}\sigma_{\hat{M}M}\otimes\outerproduct{1}{1}+p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}(1-p_{{t+1}})\sigma_{\hat{M}M}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}})\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\kappa_{M^{\prime}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}.

Again by the data processing
2.1
,

\eta_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}}\approx_{\varepsilon+2^{1+a-\lambda}}p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{{t+1}}\sigma_{\hat{M}M}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{{t+1}})\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\bot_{M^{\prime}}.

Which implies the desired theorem if we note that
p_{\mathcal{A}^{t}}p_{{t+1}}
depends only on tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}
. This follows since
p_{{t+1}}
depends only on
(\Lambda_{t+1},\zeta_{E_{t+1}})
and
\zeta_{E_{t+1}}
depends only on
\mathcal{A}^{t}
. It only remains to prove
3.7
:

Proof.

[of
3.7
] Our goal is to prove the Bell basis measurement on the state
\Tr_{E^{\prime}_{t+1}}\left(\Lambda_{t+1}(\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}}\otimes\Phi_{\hat{E}})\right)
rejects with high probability. Note that the Schmidt number of this state across the
(E^{\prime},E_{t+1})
cut is at most the Schmidt number of
\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}}\otimes\Phi_{\hat{E}}
across the
(M^{\prime}E^{\prime},E_{t+1}\hat{E})
, since the partial trace and
\Lambda_{t+1}
are local operations, and local operations do not increase Schmidt number
Proposition

2.1
.

However, the Schmidt number of
\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}}\otimes\Phi_{\hat{E}}
across the cut
(M^{\prime}E^{\prime},E_{t+1}\hat{E})
is at most the Schmidt number of
\gamma_{M^{\prime}E^{\prime}E_{t+1}}
across the
(M^{\prime}E^{\prime},E_{t+1})
cut, which is in turn
\leq 2^{a}
since the size of
|E_{t+1}|\leq a
. Applying
3.5
gives us the desired bound.
∎

4
Tamper Detection in the Bounded Storage Model

In this section, we present and analyze our quantum tamper detection code in the
3
-split-state model
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})}
, where the adversaries are allowed a finite pre-shared quantum state to assist in tampering with their shares of the code (see
Definition

2.10
). In particular, we show security even when
e_{1},e_{2}
are both unbounded, that is, only the entanglement of one of the parties is restricted.

Theorem 4.1

(
Theorem

1.2
, restatement)
.

For every integer
k
,
a\leq\lambda
, there exists an efficient tamper-detection code against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(a,*,*)}
for
k
qubit messages with error
2^{-(k+\lambda)^{\Omega(1)}}+6\cdot 2^{a-\lambda}
and rate
(11+12\frac{\lambda}{k})^{-1}
.

Our construction combines quantum secure non-malleable extractors and families of unitary 2 designs. We refer the reader to
Section

2.4.2
and
Section

2.2
respectively for formal definitions of the ingredients. We dedicate
Section

4.1
for a description of our construction, and
Section

4.2
for its analysis.

4.1
Code Construction

4.1.1
Ingredients

Let
\delta,\delta^{\prime}>0
be constants,
k=(1/2-\delta-5\delta^{\prime})n/5
, and
\lambda=\delta^{\prime}\cdot n
.
We combine

1.

\mathsf{nmExt}:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{\delta\cdot n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{(1/2-\delta)n}
, a quantum secure two source non-malleable extractor with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}=2^{-n^{\Omega_{\delta}(1)}}
from
2.7
.

2.

The family of
2
-design unitaries
C_{R}
from
2.6
.

4.1.2
Our candidate TDC

Our candidate construction of quantum TDC against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1}=\infty,e_{2}=\infty,e_{3})}
is given in
Figure

10
along with the tampering process. We describe it explicitly in the following
Algorithm

3
. The decoding scheme, denoted as
\mathrm{Dec}
, for the quantum TDC operates as in
Algorithm

8
.

Input:

A
k
qubit quantum message
\sigma_{M}
(with canonical purification
\hat{M}
).

1

2

1:

Sample classical registers
X,Y
uniformly and independently of size
n,\delta n
respectively. Evaluate
R=\mathsf{nmExt}(X,Y)
.

3

Prepare
\lambda=\delta^{\prime}n
EPR pairs,
\Phi^{\otimes\lambda}
, on a bipartite register
E,\hat{E}
.

4

3:

Consider
X
as the first share and
(Y,E)
as the second share.

5

Let
C_{R}
be the Clifford unitary picked using sampling process
\mathsf{Samp}
in
2.6
. Apply
C_{R}
on registers
(\hat{E},M)
to generate
Z
. This is possible since
|R|=(1/2-\delta)n=5(k+\delta^{\prime}n)
from our choice of parameters. Consider
Z
as the third share.

6

5:

Output shares
X,(Y,{E}),Z
.

Algorithm 3

\mathsf{Enc}
: Quantum TDC against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(e_{1}=\infty,e_{2}=\infty,e_{3})}
(see
Figure

10
).

Input:

Any tampered code-state
\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\sigma_{M})
on 3 shares
(X,YE,Z)
.

1

2

1:

The decoder first computes
R^{\prime}=\mathsf{nmExt}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
.

3

2:

Subsequently

is applied on register

to obtain
\hat{E}^{\prime},M^{\prime}
.

4

3:

Perform an
\mathsf{EPR}
test on registers
E^{\prime}\hat{E}^{\prime}
. This entails a binary outcome projective measurement
({\Pi=\Phi_{E\hat{E}},\bar{\Pi}=\mathbb{I}_{E\hat{E}}-\Phi_{E\hat{E}}})
.

5

4:

An acceptance decision is made if the outcome is
\Pi=\Phi_{E\hat{E}}
. In this case, the “replace” operation
\mathsf{Rep}
outputs the register
M^{\prime}
.

6

If the outcome is not
\Pi
, the operation
\mathsf{Rep}
outputs a special symbol
\bot
in register
M^{\prime}
.

7

\mathsf{Dec}
:

In this section, we prove two key results about our code construction. Our main result is that the code described above is a secure tamper-detection code in the bounded storage model:

Theorem 4.2

(Tamper Detection)
.

Consider
Figure

10
.
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
as shown in
Figure

10
is a quantum TDC against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(\infty,\infty,e_{3})}
with error
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq 2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}+6\cdot 2^{e_{3}-\lambda}
.

The proof of which we defer to
Section

4.2.1
. By instantiating
Theorem

4.2
with the ingredients above, we obtain the following explicit construction.

Corollary 4.3
.

For any constant
0<\gamma<1/12
, there exists an efficient quantum TDC of blocklength
n
and rate
\frac{1-12\cdot\gamma}{11}
secure against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(\infty,\infty,\gamma\cdot n)}
with error
2^{-n^{\Omega(1)}}
.

We further prove that our construction inherits quite strong secret sharing properties, which we extensively leverage in our future applications to ramp secret sharing.

Theorem 4.4

(3-out-of-3 Secret Sharing)
.

(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
from
Theorem

4.2
is also a
3
-out-of-
3
secret sharing scheme with error
\varepsilon^{\prime}
. In fact, any two shares of the quantum TDC are
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-close to the maximally mixed state.

Proof.

Note that
\mathrm{Enc}(.)
in
Figure

10
first samples an independent
(X,Y)
and then generates
R=\mathsf{nmExt}(X,Y)
. It also independently prepares
\lambda=\delta^{\prime}n
EPR pairs
\Phi_{E\hat{E}}
. It follows from the strong-extraction property of
\mathsf{nmExt}
(see
2.7
) that

RX\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}}U_{R}\otimes U_{X}\quad;\quad RY\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}}U_{R}\otimes U_{Y}.

Recall that the three shares are
(X,YE,Z=C_{R}(\hat{E}M)C^{\dagger}_{R})
. Thus for every message
{\sigma_{M\hat{M}}}
, using the fact that Cliffords are 1-Designs (
2.5
), and the approximate sampler
\mathsf{Samp}
from
2.6
, we have

(\mathrm{Enc}(\sigma))_{\hat{M}ZX}\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}+2^{1-\lambda}}\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes U_{Z}\otimes U_{X}\quad;\quad(\mathrm{Enc}(\sigma))_{\hat{M}ZYE}\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}+2^{1-\lambda}}\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes U_{Z}\otimes U_{Y}\otimes U_{E}.

(37)

Since
|Z|>\lambda=\delta^{\prime}n
. Moreover, since
(X,Y)
are sampled independently, we also have

(\mathrm{Enc}(\sigma))_{\hat{M}XYE}=\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes U_{X}\otimes U_{Y}\otimes U_{E}.

(38)

∎

4.2.1
Proof of
Theorem

4.2

To show that
(\mathrm{Enc},\mathrm{Dec})
is an
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-secure quantum TDC, it suffices to show that for every
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,T,\psi_{W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}})
it holds that (in

)

\eta_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}}\approx_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}p_{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\hat{M}M}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}})(\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes{\bot}_{M^{\prime}}),

(39)

where
p_{\mathcal{A}}
depends only on the tampering adversary
\mathcal{A}
. To do so, we proceed similarly to

, where we “delayed” the tampering channel on the extra
(t+1)
st share. In

below, we represent the same tampering experiment as

, except for the the delayed action of the tampering map

. We show that

holds in

, which completes the proof.

.

Proof.

Consider the state
{\tau}
in
Figure

. Note
{\tau}_{\hat{M}M}\equiv\sigma_{\hat{M}M}
is a pure state (thus independent of other registers in
{\tau}
) and

{\tau}=(\mathsf{nmExt}_{X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}}\otimes\mathsf{nmExt}_{XY})\left((U\otimes V)(\sigma\otimes\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}})\right).

Our analysis will proceed by cases, depending on whether the
X
and

registers are modified by the tampering experiment in
Figure

(i.e.,
XY\neq X^{\prime}Y^{\prime}
) or not.
To this end, we consider two different conditionings of

based on these two cases. Let
\tau^{1}
be the state if the tampering adversary ensures
(XY=X^{\prime}Y^{\prime})
and
\tau^{0}
be the state conditioned on
(XY\neq X^{\prime}Y^{\prime})
.

Using
2.7
, state
\tau
can be written as convex combination of two states

and
\tau^{0}
such that:

(\tau)_{{R}R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}}=p_{\mathsf{same}}(\tau^{1})_{{R}R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}}+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})(\tau^{0})_{{R}R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}},

(40)

where
p_{\mathsf{same}}
depends only on the adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=(U,V,\psi_{W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}})
. In case
(XY=X^{\prime}Y^{\prime})
, the key is recovered
\Pr({R} =R')_{\tau^{1}}=1
.
2.7
guarantees the non-malleability of the secret key
R
:

p_{\mathsf{same}}\|(\tau^{1})_{{R}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}}-U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{1})_{W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}}\|_{1}+\\
(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\|(\tau^{0})_{{R}R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}}-U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{0})_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}.

(41)

Suppose
\Upsilon
denotes the CPTP map from registers
{R}R^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}
to
M^{\prime}\hat{M}
(i.e.
\Upsilon
maps state
\tau
to
\eta
) in
Figure

11
. We present two claims (proved in the next subsections) which allow us to conclude the proof. The first one stipulates that if the key is not recovered, the Bell basis measurement rejects with high probability:

Claim 4.5

(Key
not
recovered)
.

\Upsilon(U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{0})_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})\approx_{2\cdot 2^{|W_{3}|-|E|}}\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\bot_{M^{\prime}}
.

In the second, if the key is recovered, then the 2-design functions as an authentication code. In this manner, we either recover the original message, or reject:

Claim 4.6

(Key recovered)
.

\Upsilon(U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{1})_{W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})\approx_{\frac{2}{(4^{|E|+|M|}-1)}+\frac{2}{2^{|E|}}}p\sigma_{\hat{M}M}+(1-p)\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\bot_{M^{\prime}}
. Furthermore,
p
depends only on adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,T,\ket{\psi}_{W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}})
.

We are now in a position to conclude the proof. First, leveraging
Equation

41

\displaystyle\eta_{M^{\prime}\hat{M}}=\Upsilon((\tau)_{{R}R^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})=p_{\mathsf{same}}\Upsilon((\tau^{1})_{{R}R^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\Upsilon((\tau^{0})_{{R}R^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})

\displaystyle\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}}p_{\mathsf{same}}\Upsilon(U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{1})_{W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\Upsilon(U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{0})_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})

(42)

Next, by applying
4.6
, proceeded by
4.5
:

\displaystyle\approx_{\frac{2}{(4^{|E|+|M|}-1)}+\frac{2}{2^{|E|}}}p_{\mathsf{same}}\cdot(p\cdot\sigma_{M\hat{M}}+(1-p)(\bot_{{M^{\prime}}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}}))+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\Upsilon(U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{0})_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})

(43)

\displaystyle\approx_{2\cdot\frac{2^{|W_{3}|}}{2^{|E|}}}p_{\mathsf{same}}\cdot p\cdot\sigma_{M\hat{M}}+p_{\mathsf{same}}(1-p)(\bot_{{M^{\prime}}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}})+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\bot_{M^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}}

(44)

The total error is thus
\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{nmExt}}+6\cdot 2^{|W_{3}|-|E|}
. The observation that
p_{\mathsf{same}}
and
p
depend only on the adversary
\mathcal{A}=(U,V,T,\ket{\psi}_{W_{1}W_{2}W_{3}})
completes the proof.

∎

4.2.2
Proof of
4.5

Proof.

Let
\nu^{0},\chi^{0},\mu^{0}
be the intermediate states and
\eta^{0}
be the final state when we run the CPTP map
\Upsilon
on
U_{|R|}\otimes\tau^{0}_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}}
(see
Figure

11
). Since,
\tau^{0}_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}}=\tau^{0}_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}}\otimes\sigma_{M\hat{M}}
, using
2.5
(Cliffords are 1-Designs) it follows that in the state
\nu^{0}
the two registers
E
and
\hat{E}
are decoupled:

\nu^{0}_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{M}\hat{E}M}=\tau^{0}_{R^{\prime}W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes U_{\hat{E}M}

(45)

We fix
R^{\prime}=r^{\prime}
and argue that we output
\bot_{M^{\prime}}
with high probability for every such fixing. Let

\tau^{0,r^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\tau^{0}|(R^{\prime}=r^{\prime})
and similarly define
\nu^{0,r^{\prime}},\chi^{0,r^{\prime}}
,
\mu^{0,r^{\prime}}
and
\eta^{0,r^{\prime}}
. Note
\nu^{0,r^{\prime}}_{W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{M}\hat{E}M}=\tau^{0,r^{\prime}}_{W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}}\otimes\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes U_{\hat{E}M}
. This implies that the Schmidt number of the state
\nu^{0,r^{\prime}}
across the bipartition
(W_{2}^{\prime}E^{\prime},W_{3}\hat{E}M\hat{M})
is
\leq 2^{|W_{3}|}
.

The states
\chi^{0,r^{\prime}},\mu^{0,r^{\prime}}
can be prepared from
\nu^{0,r^{\prime}}
using just local operations on each side of the cut
(W_{2}^{\prime}E^{\prime},W_{3}\hat{E}M\hat{M})
. From
Proposition

2.1
, their Schmidt numbers are at most
2^{|W_{3}|}
. Moreover, again by
Proposition

2.1
, the reduced density matrix
\mu^{0,r^{\prime}}_{E^{\prime}\hat{E}}
also has Schmidt number at most
2^{|W_{3}|}
.
3.5
then ensures that the
\mathsf{EPR}
test on state
\mu^{0,r^{\prime}}
fails with probability at least
1-2^{|W_{3}|-|E|}
. We conclude
\eta^{0,r^{\prime}}_{\hat{M}M^{\prime}}\approx_{2\cdot 2^{|W_{3}|-|E|}}\sigma_{\hat{M}}\otimes\bot_{M^{\prime}}
.

Since the above argument works for every fixing of
R^{\prime}=r^{\prime}
and
\eta^{0}=\mathbb{E}_{r^{\prime}}\eta^{0,r^{\prime}}
, we have the desired.

∎

Proof.

\mu^{1}\approx_{2/(4^{|M|+|\hat{E}|}-1)}p_{1}\mu^{1,0}+(1-p_{1})\mu^{1,1}
. Let the final states be

\eta^{1}\approx_{2/(4^{|M|+|\hat{E}|}-1)}p_{1}\eta^{1,0}+(1-p_{1})\eta^{1,1}.

(46)

using

as

\displaystyle\Upsilon(U_{|R|}\otimes(\tau^{1})_{W_{2}^{\prime}W_{3}E^{\prime}\hat{E}M\hat{M}})

Considering

, we have the desired. Through
p_{1},p_{2}
,

∎

5
Tamper-Detecting Secret Sharing Schemes

In this section, we prove our main result on the construction of secret sharing schemes which detect local tampering, restated below. We refer the reader to
Section

2.5.4
for the definition of tamper-detecting secret sharing schemes.

Theorem 5.1

(
Theorem

1.4
, restatement)
.

For every
p,t
s.t.
4\leq t\leq p-2
, there exists a
(p,t,0,0)
secret sharing scheme for
k
bit messages which is
(\mathsf{LO}^{p},t+2,2^{-\max(p,k)^{\Omega(1)}})
-tamper detecting.

In other words, no
t-1
shares reveal any information about the message, any
t
honest shares uniquely determine the message, and one can detect
\mathsf{LO}
tampering on any
(t+2)
shares.

We organize this section as follows. We begin in
Section

5.1
by introducing the relevant code components, overviewing the construction, as well as presenting the construction and analysis of the “triangle gadgets” discussed in
Section

1.3.3
. Next, in
Section

5.2
we present our secret sharing scheme, and in the subsequent
Section

5.3
we present its analysis.

5.1
Code Components and the Triangle Gadget

In
Section

5.1.1
, we describe the ingredients in our secret sharing scheme, in
Section

5.1.2
we introduce the “triangle gadget”, and in
Section

5.1.3
we prove that it inherits two basic properties: (weak) tamper detection, and 3-out-of-3 secret sharing.

5.1.1
Ingredients and Overview

We combine:

1.

(\mathsf{Share}_{\mathsf{LRSS}},\mathsf{Rec}_{\mathsf{LRSS}})
: A classical
(p,t,0,0)
-secret sharing scheme which is
\varepsilon_{lr}
-leakage resilient to
\mu
qubits of the
3
-local leakage model
\mathcal{F}_{3,\mu}^{p,t}
, such as that of
Theorem

2.6
.

For basic definitions of leakage-resilient secret sharing, see
Section

2.5.2
.

2.

(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}^{\lambda},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{TD}}^{\lambda})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
: The 3-split-state quantum tamper-detection code in the bounded storage model of
Theorem

4.2
with
\lambda
EPR pairs, comprised of:

(a)

\mathsf{nmExt}:\{0,1\}^{q}\times\{0,1\}^{\delta\cdot q}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{r}
, a quantum secure two source non-malleable extractor, with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}=2^{-q^{\Omega_{\delta}(1)}}
and output size
r=(1/2-\delta)q
from
2.7
.

(b)

The family of
2
-design unitaries
C_{R}
from
2.6
on
\frac{1}{5}\cdot r
qubits.

From
Theorem

4.2
,
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}^{\lambda},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{TD}}^{\lambda})
has message length
\frac{r}{5}-\lambda
and is
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{TD}}\leq 2(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{a-\lambda})
secure against
\mathsf{LO}_{(a,*,*)}
. Definitions of these code components can be found in
Section

4
and
Section

2
.

Our approach to secret sharing attempts to extend the compiler by
[
ADN+19
]
to a tripartite setting. First, the classical message
m
is shared into the secret sharing scheme, resulting in classical shares
(M_{1},\cdots,M_{p})\leftarrow\mathsf{Share}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}(m)
. Then every triplet of parties
a<b<c
jointly encodes their shares
M_{a},M_{b},M_{c}
into a code
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
supported on a tripartite register, the shares of which are redistributed among
a,b,c
.

In this subsection, we present our gadget code
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
and prove two of its relevant properties: a strong form of 3-out-of-3 secret sharing, as well as a weak form of tamper detection. In the next subsections, we show how to leverage these gadget properties together with that of the underlying leakage-resilient secret sharing scheme to achieve tamper detection, globally.

5.1.2
The Triangle Gadget

Input:

Three parties
p_{0}<p_{1}<p_{2}\in[p]
, three messages
M_{0},M_{1},M_{2}
, and an integer parameter
\lambda
.

Output:

A quantum state
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}^{\lambda}(M_{0},M_{1},M_{2})
on a tripartite register
A,B,C
.

1

2

1:

Each party
i\in\{0,1,2\}
encrypts their message
M_{i}
into the tamper-detection code of
Algorithm

3
, into a tripartite register
(Q_{i},Y_{i}\hat{E}_{i},X_{i})
. Explicitly, party
i
:

1.

Samples uniformly random classical registers
X_{i},Y_{i}
and evaluates the key
R_{i}=\mathsf{nmExt}(X_{i},Y_{i})
.

2.

Prepares
\lambda_{i}=(i+1)\cdot\lambda
EPR pairs, on a bipartite register
E_{i}\hat{E}_{i}
.

3.

Samples the Clifford unitary
C_{R_{i}}
using the sampling process
\mathsf{Samp}
in
2.6
. Applies
C_{R_{i}}
on registers
M_{i},E_{i}
to generate
Q_{i}
.

4.

Hands register
X_{i}
to party
i-1
(mod
3
), registers
Y_{i},\hat{E}_{i}
to party
i+1
(mod
3
), and keeps the authenticated register
Q_{i}
.

3

2:

Output registers
A,B,C
for parties
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2}
respectively, where

A=(Q_{0},Y_{2}\hat{E}_{2},X_{1}),B=(Q_{1},Y_{0}\hat{E}_{0},X_{2}),C=(Q_{2},Y_{1}\hat{E}_{1},X_{0})

(49)

Algorithm 5

\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
: The “triangle gadget”

\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle}
: To decode, we simply run the decoder
\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{TD}}
for the tamper-detection code on the registers
(Q_{i},Y_{i}\hat{E}_{i},X_{i})
corresponding to
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}(M_{i})
. If any of the three are
\bot
, output
\bot
. If otherwise, output the resulting messages
({M}^{\prime}_{0},{M}^{\prime}_{1},{M}^{\prime}_{2})
. Note that if no tampering is present, then from the perfect correctness of the tamper-detection code
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle},\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle})
is also perfectly correct.

5.1.3
Analysis of the Triangle Gadget

Before moving on to our global construction, we prove two important properties of the gadget
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle},\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle})
. The first is that not only is
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}

3
-out-of-
3
secret sharing, but in fact any two shares
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
are near maximally mixed. Note that 3-out-of-3 secret sharing only implies that the two-party reduced density matrices are independent of the message, but apriori they could still be entangled.

Claim 5.2

(Pairwise Independence)
.

For every share
W\in\{A,B,C\}
and fixed strings
m_{0},m_{1},m_{2}
, the two party marginal of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{0},m_{1},m_{2})
without
W
is
\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}
-close to maximally mixed, where
\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}\leq 6\cdot(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{-\lambda})
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}^{e}
is pairwise independent with error
\leq 2(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{-e})
from
Theorem

4.4
. The result follows from a triangle inequality.
∎

The second property concerns the resilience of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
to tampering in
\mathsf{LO}^{3}
. We prove that two of the three inputs, namely
m_{1},m_{2}
, are individually tamper-detected:

Claim 5.3

(Share-wise Tamper Detection)
.

Fix
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{3}
, three strings
m_{0},m_{1},m_{2}
, and an integer
\lambda
. Let
({M}^{\prime}_{0},{M}^{\prime}_{1},{M}^{\prime}_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{0},m_{1},m_{2})
denote the distribution over the recovered shares. Then for both
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\mathbb{P}[{M}^{\prime}_{i}\notin\{m_{i},\bot\}]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}},

(50)

where
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{4-\lambda}
.

By a union bound, we have
\mathbb{P}[({M}^{\prime}_{1},{M}^{\prime}_{2})\notin\{(m_{1},m_{2}),\bot\}]\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}
. However, here we already exhibit the selective bot problem. The event
{M}^{\prime}_{1}=\bot
may correlate with
{M}^{\prime}_{2}
, breaking the tamper detection guarantee.

Proof.

[of
5.3
]
Fix
m_{0},m_{1},m_{2}
, the tampering channel
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{3}
, and
i\in\{1,2\}
. In
Algorithm

5
, note that the
i
th share of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
is comprised of registers
(Q_{i},Y_{i-1}\hat{E}_{i-1},X_{i+1})
, where

1.

Q_{i}
is a share of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}^{\lambda_{i}}(m_{i})

2.

The only other quantum register contains
|\hat{E}_{i-1}|\leq\lambda_{(i-1)\text{mod}3}
qubits.

We can now consider the marginal distribution over the
i
th message
m_{i}
after the tampering channel. The marginal distribution over the recovered share
{m}^{\prime}_{i}
in the gadget
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
can be simulated using a channel
\Lambda^{\prime}
directly on
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}(m_{i})
:

\Tr_{\neq i}\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})=\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{TD}}\circ\Lambda^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}(m_{i}),

(51)

where
\Lambda^{\prime}
consists of a local tampering channel on
(Q_{i},Y_{i}\hat{E}_{i},X_{i})
aided by pre-shared entanglement in a particular form: the adversary who receives share
Q_{i}
holds at most
a=\lambda_{i-1}
qubits of pre-shared entanglement (but the other two may hold unbounded-size registers). This is precisely the model
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(a,*,*)}
of
Definition

2.10
, and we recall by
Theorem

4.2
that
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{TD}}^{e}
detects tampering against
\mathsf{LO}^{3}_{(a,*,*)}
with error
2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{4+a-e}
. Since parties
i\in\{1,2\}
hold more EPR pairs in their tamper-detection codes than the corresponding adversaries pre-shared, i.e
\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i-1}=\lambda>0
when
i\in\{1,2\}
, we conclude their shares are tamper-detected:

i\in\{1,2\}:\mathbb{P}[{M}^{\prime}_{i}\notin\{m_{i},\bot\}]\leq 2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{4-\lambda}.

(52)

∎

5.2
Code Construction

We describe our encoding algorithm in
Algorithm

5
below:

Input:

A
k
bit message
m
.

1

2

1:

Encode
m
into the
\mathsf{LRSS}
,
(M_{1},\cdots,M_{p})\leftarrow\mathsf{Share}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}(m)
.

3

2:

For every triplet of parties
a<b<c\in[p]
,

1.

Encode the shares
(M_{a},M_{b},M_{c})
into the “triangle gadget” described in
Algorithm

5
supported on triplets of quantum registers

A_{(a,b,c)},B_{(a,b,c)},C_{(a,b,c)}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(M_{a},M_{b},M_{c})

(53)

2.

Hand the
A_{(a,b,c)}
register to party
a
,
B_{(a,b,c)}
to
b
, and
C_{(a,b,c)}
to
c
.

4

3:

Let the resulting
i
th share be the collection of quantum registers

S_{i}=\{A_{(i,b,c)}:\forall b,c\text{ s.t. }i<b<c\}\bigcup\{B_{(a,i,c)}:\forall a,c\text{ s.t. }a<i<c\}\bigcup\{C_{(a,b,i)}:\forall a,b\text{ s.t. }a<b<i\}

(54)

Algorithm 6

\mathsf{Enc}
: A Tamper-Detecting Ramp Secret Sharing Scheme

We are now in a position to describe our decoding algorithm. Upon receiving the locally tampered shares of any authorized subset
T
of size equal to
t+2
, our decoder partitions
T
into two un-authorized subsets, and decodes the gadgets only within each partition.

Input:

An (authorized) subset
T
of size
t+2
, and a collection of tampered quantum registers
{S}^{\prime}_{i}:i\in T
.

Output:

A
k
bit message
M^{\prime}

1

2

1:

Partition
T
into a subset
U
of the three smallest indexed shares, and
T\setminus U
.

3

2:

For every triplet of parties
a<b<c
contained entirely in
T\setminus U
or entirely in
U
:

4

1.

Apply the triangle gadget decoding algorithm
\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle}
on registers
A_{(a,b,c)},B_{(a,b,c)},C_{(a,b,c)}
.

2.

Output
\bot
if so does the decoder. Otherwise, let
M_{(a,bc)}^{\prime},M_{(b,ac)}^{\prime},M_{(c,ab)}^{\prime}
be the recovered shares.

5

3:

If
M_{(a,bc)}^{\prime}\neq M_{(a,de)}^{\prime}
for any two triangles
(a,b,c),(a,d,e)
on the same side of the partition, output
\bot
. Otherwise, let
M_{U}^{\prime}
,
M^{\prime}_{T\setminus U}
be the recovered shares from either side of the partition.

6

4:

Remove the lowest index share of
U
to obtain
V\subset U
and
T\setminus U
to obtain
W\subset T\setminus U
. Note
|V\cup W|\geq t
.

7

5:

Run the decoder
M^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Rec}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})
on the classical shares of
V\cup W
.

Algorithm 7

\mathsf{Dec}
: A Bipartite Decoding Algorithm.

The main result of this section proves that the secret sharing scheme described in
Algorithm

5
and
Algorithm

8
above detects unentangled tampering, when handed at least
t+2
shares.

Lemma 5.4
.

Assuming the
\mathsf{LRSS}
is
\varepsilon_{lr}
-resilient to
\mu\geq 10\cdot p^{2}\cdot\lambda
qubits of leakage, then
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
described above is a
(p,t,0,0)
-secret sharing scheme which is
(\mathsf{LO}^{p},t+2,\varepsilon)
-tamper-detecting with error
\varepsilon=O(\varepsilon_{lr}+p^{4}\cdot(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}^{1/2}+2^{-\lambda/2}))
.

We dedicate the next subsection to its proof. By instantiating
Lemma

5.4
above,

Theorem 5.5
.

There exists an efficient
(p,t,0,0)
secret sharing scheme for
k
bit messages which is
(\mathsf{LO}^{p},t+3,2^{-\max(k,p)^{\Omega(1)}})
-tamper-detecting.

Proof.

We use the construction of the
(p,t,0,0)

\mathsf{LRSS}
from
Theorem

2.6
with message length
k
bits, shares of length
s=k+p^{2}\cdot\lambda
bits, and error
2^{-\Omega(\lambda)}
; together with the tamper-detection codes
\mathsf{Enc}^{\lambda}
from
Theorem

4.2
with message size
s
, error
2^{-\lambda}+2^{-s^{\Omega(1)}}
, and selecting
\lambda=\max(k,p)
, we conclude the corollary.
∎

5.3
Analysis

Our proof is comprised of two key claims, which we state and use to prove
Lemma

5.4
and then analyze in the subsequent sections. We begin by arguing that our secret sharing scheme inherits a weak form of tamper detection from the “outer” split-state tamper-detection codes.

Claim 5.6

(Weak Tamper Detection)
.

For every tampering channel
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{p}
, authorized subset
T\subset[p]
of size
\geq t+2
and message
m
, the distribution over the recovered message
M^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)
satisfies

\mathbb{P}[M^{\prime}\notin\{m,\bot\}]\leq\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}=O(p^{3}\cdot(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}+\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}\cdot p))=O(p^{4}(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{-\lambda}))

(55)

In fact, all the shares of
V\cup W
are recovered whp:
\mathbb{P}[\exists i\in V\cup W:M^{\prime}_{i}\notin\{m_{i},\bot\}]\leq\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}

In other words,
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is a secret sharing scheme which is weak tamper-detecting against
\mathsf{LO}^{p}
, so long as the decoder receives at least
t+2
shares. As previously discussed, the reason this is only
weak
tamper detecting is due to the selective bot problem - the probability with which we reject may apriori depend on
m
. We leverage the leakage resilience of the underlying secret sharing scheme to ensure this cannot occur:

Claim 5.7

(The Selective Bot Problem)
.

For every tampering channel
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{p}
and authorized subset
T\subset[p]
of size
\geq t+2
, the probability the decoding algorithm
\mathsf{Dec}
in
Algorithm

8
rejects is near independent of the message. That is,

\forall\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{p},m_{0},m_{1}:\big|\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m_{0})=\bot]-\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m_{1})=\bot]\big|\leq\eta_{lr},

(56)

where
\eta_{lr}=O(\varepsilon_{lr}+p^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}})=O(\varepsilon_{lr}+p^{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}^{1/2}+2^{-\lambda/2}))
.

Put together, we now conclude the proof:

Proof.

[of
Lemma

5.4
] Let the random variable
M
denote the uniform distribution over messages, and let
m
be any fixed value of
M
. From
5.6
, for a fixed
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{p}
and
T\subset[p]
the distribution over the recovered message
M^{\prime}
is near a convex combination of
M
and rejection, with bias
\gamma_{m}
(dependent on
m
):

M^{\prime}|_{M=m}\approx_{\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}}\gamma_{m}\cdot m+(1-\gamma_{m})\cdot\bot.

(57)

However, by
5.7
, the probability of
\bot
can barely depend on
M
:

1-\gamma_{m}\approx_{\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}}\mathbb{P}[M^{\prime}=\bot|M=m]\approx_{\eta_{lr}}\mathbb{P}[M^{\prime}=\bot]\equiv p_{\mathsf{Adv}}

(58)

By the triangle inequality,
M^{\prime}|_{M=m}\approx_{3\cdot\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}+2\eta_{lr}}(1-p_{\mathsf{Adv}})\cdot m+p_{\mathsf{Adv}}\cdot\bot
, for some fixed
p_{\mathsf{Adv}}
dependent on
\Lambda,T
. With
\eta_{\mathsf{TD}},\eta_{lr}
as in the claims above we conclude the theorem.
∎

5.3.1
Proof of Weak Tamper Detection (
5.6
)

Proof.

[of
5.6
] Consider any triangle
a<b<c\in[p]
contained on the same side of the partition
(U,T\setminus U)
, and let
(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c})
be any fixing of their classical shares. Note that the reduced density matrix on their quantum registers
(S_{a},S_{b},S_{c})|((M_{a},M_{b},M_{c})=(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c}))
contains the triangle
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c})
, as well as the registers of all the triangles
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
which intersect
(a,b,c)
. However, since each individual register of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
is maximally mixed, and all pairs of registers of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
are
\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}
approximately pairwise independent (
5.2
), we have

(S_{a},S_{b},S_{c})|_{((M_{a},M_{b},M_{c})=(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c}))}\approx_{3\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}\cdot p}\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c})\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}}{\Tr\mathbb{I}}

(59)

The copies
\sigma=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{\Tr\mathbb{I}}
of the maximally mixed state are separable across the shares
a,b,c
, and independent of
m
, and act as ancillas. Therefore, any channel
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LO}^{3}
which tampers with the registers
(S_{a},S_{b},S_{c})
using
\sigma
can be simulated in
\mathsf{LO}^{3}
without them:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle}\circ\Lambda\circ(S_{a},S_{b},S_{c})|_{((M_{a},M_{b},M_{c})=(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c}))}\approx_{3\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}\cdot p}\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle}\circ\Lambda(\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c})\otimes\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Dec}_{\triangle}\circ\Lambda^{\prime}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c})

By
5.3
and the triangle inequality, this implies that two of the three shares in each triangle
(a,b,c)
are weak tamper detected:

\mathbb{P}[({M}^{\prime}_{a},{M}^{\prime}_{b})\notin\{(m_{a},m_{b}),\bot\}]\leq 2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}+3\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}\cdot p

(60)
