By a union bound over all the triangles, either all the shares in
V\subset U,W\subset(T\setminus U)
are recovered, or we reject with probability

\mathbb{P}[\forall i\in V\cup W:{M}^{\prime}_{i}\notin\{m_{i},\bot\}]\leq t^{3}\cdot(2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}+3\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}\cdot p).

(61)

Since
|V\cup W|
contains
t
(honest) shares,
\mathbb{P}[M^{\prime}\notin\{m,\bot\}]\leq t^{3}\cdot(2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}+3\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}\cdot p)
.
∎

5.3.2
Proof of Leakage-Resilience (
5.7
)

The decoder
\mathsf{Dec}_{T}
of
Algorithm

8
rejects a tampered message
\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)
if either subset
U
or
(T\setminus U)
reject (up to step 3), or if the decoder for the
\mathsf{LRSS}
rejects.

We begin by analyzing the first of these two possibilities. In
5.8
, we use the leakage resilience of the underlying secret sharing scheme to argue that the event that either subset
U
or
(T\setminus U)
rejects cannot depend on the message
m
. To do so, we show that the measurement of whether either
U
or
(T\setminus U)
reject can be simulated by a leakage channel from
U
to
(T\setminus U)
on the shares of the classical
\mathsf{LRSS}
.

Claim 5.8
.

Fix a tampering channel
\Lambda
and subsets
T\subset[n]
,
|T|\geq t+2
and
U\subset T
of the smallest 3 shares in
T
. The probability
\mathsf{Dec}_{T}

Algorithm

8
has rejected before step 4, does not depend on the message:
\forall m_{0},m_{1}:

|\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m_{0}]-\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m_{1}]|\leq p^{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}+\varepsilon_{lr}.

(62)

Proof.

Fix a message
m
, and let us condition on the classical
\mathsf{LRSS}
shares
M_{T}
of
m
in
T\subset[p]
being a fixed value
M_{T}=m_{T}=\{m_{i}:i\in T\}
. Now, consider the reduced density matrix
(\mathsf{Enc}(m)_{T}|_{M_{T}=m_{T}})
on the shares of
T\subset[p]
conditioned on the classical shares. It is comprised of multiple shares of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
, for every triangle
a<b<c
which intersects
T
. However, similarly to the proof of
5.6
, the pairwise independence of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}
(
5.2
) ensures any less than three shares are near maximally mixed. It suffices to then consider the triangles contained entirely in
T
:

\bigg\|(\mathsf{Enc}(m)_{T}|_{M_{T}=m_{T}})-\bigg(\bigotimes_{a,b,c\in T}\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}(m_{a},m_{b},m_{c})\bigg)\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}}{\Tr\mathbb{I}}\bigg\|_{1}\leq p^{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}

(63)

Now, recall that
\mathsf{Enc}_{\triangle}^{\lambda}
can be implemented using local operations between the three parties and at most
O(\lambda)
qubits of shared entanglement. This implies that the parties in
U
and
T\setminus U
could prepare the reduced density matrix
T
,
(\mathsf{Enc}(m)_{T}|_{M_{T}=m_{T}})
, using only their own shares
m_{U},m_{T\setminus U}
and

1.

Joint operations within the subsets
U,T\setminus U
.

2.

At most
\mu=O(\lambda\cdot p^{2})
qubits of one-way communication from
U
to
T\setminus U
.

If we denote
L
as the
\mu
qubit “leakage” register, then one can formalize the above by defining two channels
\mathcal{E}_{L,U}
and
\mathcal{E}_{T\setminus U,L}
.
\mathcal{E}_{L,U}
acts on the shares of
U
producing the leakage register
L
, and
\mathcal{E}_{T\setminus U,L}
acts on
L,T\setminus U
respectively:

(\mathsf{Enc}(m)_{T}|_{M_{T}=m_{T}})\approx_{p^{3}\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}}(\mathcal{E}_{T\setminus U,L}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{U})\circ(\mathbb{I}_{T\setminus U}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L,U})\circ(m_{T\setminus U},m_{U})

(64)

Moreover, before step
4
in
Algorithm

8
the decoding channel
\mathsf{Dec}_{T}
factorizes as a tensor product of channels
\mathsf{Dec}_{U}\otimes\mathsf{Dec}_{T\setminus U}
, as does the tampering channel
\Lambda_{U}\otimes\Lambda_{T\setminus U}
. The output of the decoder (up to step
4
), conditioned on the message
m
and its shares
M_{T}=m_{T}
can thereby be simulated using

\displaystyle(\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\circ\Lambda_{T}\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)_{T}\big|_{M_{T}=m_{T}})\approx_{p^{3}\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{T\setminus U,L}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{U})\circ(\mathbb{I}_{T\setminus U}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{L,U})\circ(m_{T\setminus U},m_{U})

(65)

\displaystyle\text{ where }\mathcal{N}_{U,L}=\mathsf{Dec}_{U}\circ\Lambda_{U}\circ\mathcal{E}_{U,L}\text{ and }\mathcal{N}_{L,T\setminus U}=\mathsf{Dec}_{T\setminus U}\circ\Lambda_{T\setminus U}\circ\mathcal{E}_{{T\setminus U},L}

(66)

Let
\mathcal{N}_{U,L}^{\prime}
denote the channel which applies
\mathcal{N}_{U,L}
on
m_{U}
and then checks whether the decoder rejected (a bit
b
), leaks
b
and
L
to
T\setminus U
, and traces out
U
. Note that
\mathcal{N}_{U,L}^{\prime}
is in the leakage family
\mathcal{F}_{3,\mu+1}^{p,t}
, since
|U|=3
leaks
\mu+1
qubits to a subset of size
|T\setminus U|<t
. We conclude that for any two messages
m_{0},m_{1}
, by monotonicity of trace distance under CPTP maps:

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m_{0}]-\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m_{1}]\big|\leq

(67)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot p^{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}+\|(\mathcal{N}_{U,L}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{T\setminus U})\circ\mathsf{Share}(m_{0})_{T}-(\mathcal{N}_{U,L}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{T\setminus U})\circ\mathsf{Share}(m_{1})_{T}\|_{1}\leq

(68)

\leq 2\cdot p^{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}+\varepsilon_{lr}
, where last we leverage the leakage resilience of the
\mathsf{LRSS}
against
\mathcal{F}_{3,\mu}^{p,t}
.
∎

Observe that in
Algorithm

8
the
\mathsf{LRSS}
can only reject if the subsets
U
or
(T\setminus U)
did not already reject. However, from
5.6
, we know that if
U
or
(T\setminus U)
didn’t reject, then (roughly) with high probability we must have recovered honest shares from the subsets
V\subset U,W\subset(T\setminus U)
- which the
\mathsf{LRSS}
must accept. We can now conclude the proof of
5.7
:

Proof.

[of
5.7
] The decoder
\mathsf{Dec}_{T}
rejects a tampered message
\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)
with probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(M)=\bot|M=m]=\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m]+

(69)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4 }\text{ and }\mathsf{Rec}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})=\bot|M=m].

(70)

From
5.8
, the probability either half of the partition
U\text{ or }T\setminus U
rejects is
\gamma=2p^{3}\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}+\varepsilon_{lr}
close to independent of the message:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m_{0}]\approx_{\gamma}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m_{1}]

(71)

Fix a real parameter
\delta
. We divide into two cases on
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ aborts before Step 4 }|M=m_{0}]
:

1.

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4 }|M=m_{0}]\leq\delta
, then
\forall m
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4 }\text{ and }\mathsf{Rec}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})=\bot|M=m]\leq

(72)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4 }|M=m]\leq\delta+\gamma.

(73)

By the triangle inequality,

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m_{0})=\bot]\approx_{\delta+\gamma}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m_{1})=\bot].

(74)

2.

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4 }|M=m_{0}]\geq\delta
. From the weak tamper detection guarantee in
5.6
,
\forall m
:

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4, and}(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})\neq(m_{V},m_{W})|M=m]\leq\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}

(75)

Since under message
m_{0}
,
\mathsf{Dec}_{T}
doesn’t abort before Step 4 with non-negligible probability, then conditioned on that event, the shares of
V,W
are untampered with high probability:

\mathbb{P}[(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})=(m_{V},m_{W})|\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4},M=m_{0}]\geq 1-\frac{\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}}{\delta}.

(76)

A similar statement holds for
m_{1}
since
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4 }|M=m_{1}]\geq\delta-\gamma
, and therefore

\mathbb{P}[(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})=(m_{V},m_{W})|\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4},M=m_{1}]\geq 1-\frac{\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}}{\delta-\gamma}.

(77)

Finally, since
\mathsf{Rec}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}
has perfect correctness, if we condition on
(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})=(m_{V},m_{W})
,
\mathsf{Rec}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}
recovers the message with probability
1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\forall m:\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4, and }\mathsf{Rec}_{\mathsf{LRSS}}(M^{\prime}_{V},M^{\prime}_{W})\neq\bot|M=m]

(78)

\displaystyle\approx_{\frac{\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}}{\delta-\gamma}}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}_{T}\text{ doesn't abort before Step 4}|M=m].

(79)

We conclude
|\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m_{0})=\bot]-\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m_{1})=\bot]|\leq\eta_{lr}
, where by the triangle inequality and optimizing over
\delta:

\eta_{lr}\leq\gamma+\max(\delta,\frac{\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}}{\delta-\gamma})\leq 2\gamma+\sqrt{\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}}\leq O(\varepsilon_{lr}+p^{2}\cdot\sqrt{\delta_{\mathsf{PI}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Share}}}),

(80)

where we used the values of
\gamma
and
\eta_{\mathsf{TD}}
from
5.8
and
5.6
.
∎

6
A Single-Bit Non-Malleable Code against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{2}

In this section, our main result is a 2-split-state non-malleable code for a single bit message, secure against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{2}
. Please refer to
Definition

2.11
for a description of
\mathsf{LOCC}
channels.

Theorem 6.1
.

For every
n,e\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LOCC}_{e}^{2}
(with
e
qubits of shared entanglement) for single-bit messages of blocklength
2n
qubits and error
2^{1+e-n}
.

Our construction is simple and based on the LOCC data hiding scheme
[
TDL00
]
. However, whereas our code is non-malleable against
\mathsf{LOCC}
, curiously it is not data-hiding. That is, using
\mathsf{LOCC}
the adversaries could distinguish the message, but even in doing so they wouldn’t be able to tamper with it.

Input:

A bit
m\in\{0,1\}
, and an integer
n
.

1

2

1:

If
m=0
, then output
n
EPR pairs
\ket{\Phi^{\otimes n}}
on registers
AB
.

3

2:

If
m=1
,

[(a)]

1.

Sample two vectors
a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
uniformly at random, conditioned on either
a
or
b\neq 0^{n}

2.

Apply and output
(X^{a}\otimes Z^{b})\ket{\Phi^{\otimes n}}
.
18
18

18

We remark that for
a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
, we refer to the
n
-qubit Pauli operator
X^{a}=\otimes_{i\in[n]}X^{a_{i}}
(respectively
Z^{a}
). See
Definition

A.1
.

Algorithm 8

\mathsf{Enc}
: An NMC against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{2}
encoding a single bit.

If we didn’t desire perfect correctness, we could have chosen
\mathsf{Enc}(1)
to be the maximally mixed state. The decoding algorithm measures each maximally entangled pair in the Bell basis, resulting in
a,b\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and outputs
m=0
if
a,b=0^{n}
and
1
otherwise.

6.1
Analysis

Put together,
6.2
and
6.3
argue that no LOCC tampering channel can change an encoding of
1
to one of
0
with non-negligible bias. Following a result by
[
DKO13
]
(see
Theorem

2.3
) on NMCs for single-bit messages, this will imply the non-malleability of our construction.

Claim 6.2
.

The encoding of
m=1
is statistically close to the maximally mixed state,
\mathsf{Enc}(1)\approx_{4^{1-n}}4^{-n}\cdot\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{I}
.

Proof.

If
\mathsf{Enc}(1)
were a uniformly random Bell basis state, it would be equal to the maximally mixed state. Instead, we sample
a,b
from a distribution which is
2\cdot 4^{-n}
close to the uniform distribution over
\{0,1\}^{2n}
, which implies the desired bound.

∎

Claim 6.3
.

For every
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LOCC}^{2}
,
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(1)=0]\leq 2^{1+e-n}
.

Together with
Theorem

2.3
, we obtain
Theorem

6.1
.

Proof.

If
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LOCC}^{2}
, from
6.2
and
Proposition

2.1
,
\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(1)
remains
4^{1-n}
close to a separable state
\sigma
. If
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LOCC}^{2}_{e}
, then
\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(1)
is
4^{1-n}
close to a state of Schmidt number
2^{e}
.

However, from
3.5
, any state of Schmidt number
R
separable state has fidelity at most
R\cdot 2^{-n}
with any maximally entangled state on
n
pairs of qubits. We conclude

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(1)=0]\approx_{4^{1-n}}\Tr[\Phi^{\otimes n}\sigma]\leq 2^{e-n}.

(81)

∎

7
Non-Malleable Codes against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{4}

The main result of this section is a construction of non-malleable codes for classical messages in the 4-split-state model which is secure against arbitrary local operations and classical communication between the parties:

Theorem 7.1
.

For every sufficiently large blocklength
n,\lambda=\Omega(\log n)\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an efficient split-state non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{4}
with rate
\Omega(\lambda^{-2})
, security and correctness error
2^{-\Omega(\min(\lambda,n))}
.

Our code is based on a compiler which combines (bipartite)
\mathsf{LOCC}
data-hiding schemes and non-malleable codes in the 2-split-state model. We refer the reader to
Section

2.6
for formal definitions of data hiding schemes, and to
Section

2.4
for basic definitions of non-malleable codes.

In
Section

7.1
we present our construction and parameter choices. In
Section

7.2
we present our proof of security.

7.1
Code Construction

We combine:

1.

(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{NM}})
, a classical
2
-split-state non-malleable code with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
and rate
r_{\mathsf{NM}}
.

2.

(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}})
, a family of bipartite
(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}})
-
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding schemes of rate
r_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
.

Input:

A
k
bit message
m\in\{0,1\}^{k}

1

2

1:

Encode
m
into shares of the classical non-malleable code
(L,R)\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)
.

3

2:

Encode
L
into two shares
L_{1},L_{2}
of the
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme,
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(L)
.

4

3:

Encode
R
into two shares
R_{1},R_{2}
of the
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme,
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(R)
.

4:

Output
(L_{1},L_{2},R_{1},R_{2})
as
4
Shares respectively.

\mathsf{Enc}(m)=\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}^{\otimes 2}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)

(82)

Algorithm 9

\mathsf{Enc}
: A non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{4}
channels.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}})
be a 2-out-of-2
(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}})
-
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme and let
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{NM}})
be a classical non-malleable code against 2-split-state adversaries with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
. Then
\mathsf{Enc}=\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}^{\otimes 2}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}},\mathsf{Dec}=\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{NM}}\circ\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}^{\otimes 2}
is a
(2\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}},2\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}})
4-split-state non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LOCC}
channels.

Moreover, the rate of
\mathsf{Enc}
is the product of the rates of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
and
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}
.

7.1.1
Parameters

We present two instantiations of
Lemma

7.2
, the first to obtain an efficient code, and the second using existential constructions of the component codes to optimize rate. First, leveraging the constant rate, inverse-exponential error 2 split-state non-malleable code by
[
Li23b
]
, together with the
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme in
Theorem

2.9
by
[
EW02
]
, we obtain:

Theorem 7.3
.

For every sufficiently large blocklength
n,\lambda=\Omega(\log n)\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an efficient split-state non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{4}
with rate
\Omega(\lambda^{-2})
, security and correctness error
2^{-\Omega(\min(\lambda,n))}
.

Using the (existential) capacity achieving 2-split-state non-malleable codes
Theorem

2.5
by
[
CG13a
]
, together with the rate
1/2-o(1)

\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding schemes in
Theorem

2.8

[
HLSW03
]
,

Theorem 7.4
.

For every sufficiently large blocklength
n
, there exists a split-state non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{4}
with rate
1/4-o(1)
, security and correctness error
2^{-\Omega(n/\log n)}
.

7.2
Analysis

Note that for any
k
bit message
m\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, the encoding of
m
is given by a separable state

\mathsf{Enc}(m)=\mathbb{E}_{(L,R)\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)}\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(L)\otimes\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(R)

(83)

where
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)
indicates the joint distribution over left and right shares
L,R
of the non-malleable code.

Now, suppose we fix
L=l,R=r
and consider a fixed
\mathsf{LOCC}
“tampering” channel
\Lambda
. The classical communication transcript
c\in\{0,1\}^{*}
of the protocol is some classical random variable, which is drawn from some conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}_{c|L=l,R=r}
. We begin by claiming that if the distribution over the transcript was correlated with
l,r
, then that would break the data-hiding guarantee:

Claim 7.5
.

The distribution over the transcript is independent of
l,r
:
\|\mathbb{P}_{c|L=l,R=r}-\mathbb{P}_{c}\|_{1}\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
.

Moreover, since the tampering is performed using a separable channel, the post-tampered density matrices on the left and right splits remain separable and can only depend on the transcript:

Claim 7.6
.

For every value of
c\in\{0,1\}^{*}
and shares
l,r
, there exists a pair of density matrices
\sigma^{c,l},\sigma^{c,r}
such that the post-tampered state is given by the mixture

\Lambda(\mathsf{Enc}(m))=\mathbb{E}_{L,R\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)}\mathbb{E}_{c\leftarrow\mathbb{P}_{c|L,R}}\bigg(\sigma^{c,L}\otimes\sigma^{c,R}\bigg)

(84)

Put together, we conclude our “non-malleable reduction” from 4-split-state
\mathsf{LOCC}
tampering to 2-split tampering:

Proof.

[of
Lemma

7.2
] From
7.6
, we have that after running the decoding channel
\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
, the distribution over tampered shares
l^{\prime},r^{\prime}
forms a Markov Chain, i.e.
\forall l^{\prime},r^{\prime},l,r,c:

\text{ }\mathbb{P}[(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})=(l^{\prime},r^{\prime})|L=l,R=r,C=c]=\mathbb{P}[L^{\prime}=l^{\prime}|L=l,C=c]\cdot\mathbb{P}[R^{\prime}=r^{\prime}|R=r,C=c]

(85)

Moreover, from
7.5
, we have the distribution over the transcript is independent of
l,r
:
\|\mathbb{P}_{c|L=l,R=r}-\mathbb{P}_{c}\|_{1}\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
. Therefore, the distribution over
l^{\prime},r^{\prime}
conditioned on
l,r
is approximately the same as that of split state tampering with the transcript acting as shared randomness:

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime},r^{\prime}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}[(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})=(l^{\prime},r^{\prime})|L=l,R=r]-\sum_{c}\mathbb{P}[L^{\prime}=l^{\prime}|L=l,C=c]\cdot\mathbb{P}[R^{\prime}=r^{\prime}|R=r,C=c]\cdot\mathbb{P}[C=c]\bigg|\leq

(86)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l^{\prime},r^{\prime},c}\mathbb{P}[L^{\prime}=l^{\prime}|L=l,C=c]\cdot\mathbb{P}[R^{\prime}=r^{\prime}|R=r,C=c]\cdot\bigg|\mathbb{P}[C=c|L=l,R=r]-\mathbb{P}[C=c]\bigg|\leq

(87)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c}\bigg|\mathbb{P}[C=c|L=l,R=r]-\mathbb{P}[C=c]\bigg|\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}

(88)

Since the non-malleable code is secure against split state tampering, from
Definition

2.12
we have that the distribution over the recovered message
M^{\prime}
is approximately a convex combination of the original
m
with probability
p
, and an uncorrelated message distribution
\mathcal{D}
:

\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\approx_{2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}}\mathbb{E}_{c\leftarrow\mathbb{P}_{c}}\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{NM}}\circ(\Lambda^{c}_{1}\otimes\Lambda^{c}_{2})\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}}p\cdot m+(1-p)\cdot\mathcal{D}

(89)

with error
2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
.
∎

7.2.1
Proofs of Deferred Claims

Proof.

[of
7.5
] Suppose for the purposes of contradiction, there exists
(x,y)
such that
\|\mathbb{P}_{c|L=x,R=y}-\mathbb{P}_{c}\|_{1}>2\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
. Since
\mathbb{P}_{c}=\mathbb{E}_{L,R\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)}\mathbb{P}_{c|L,R}
, by an averaging argument, there exists
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
such that

\|\mathbb{P}_{c|L=x,R=y}-\mathbb{P}_{c|L=x^{\prime},R=y^{\prime}}\|_{1}>2\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}

(90)

by the triangle inequality, one of
\|\mathbb{P}_{c|x,y}-\mathbb{P}_{c|x^{\prime},y}\|_{1}
or
\|\mathbb{P}_{c|x^{\prime},y}-\mathbb{P}_{c|x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\|_{1}
is
\geq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
, WLOG the first of the two. Now, we argue that this violates the indistinguishability of the data hiding scheme. Crucially, since
R=y
is fixed, one of the two parties holding the encoding of
L
could generate the encoding of
R
by themselves, and simulate the
\mathsf{LOCC}
protocol which discriminates
(x,y)
from
(x^{\prime},y)
. Thereby,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}<\|\mathbb{P}_{c|x,y}-\mathbb{P}_{c|x^{\prime},y}\|_{1}\leq\|(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x^{\prime}))\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}},

(91)

which by definition violates the data hiding property.
∎

Proof.

[of
7.6
] Recall the density matrix describing the encoding of a message
m
is

\mathsf{Enc}(m)=\mathbb{E}_{L,R\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)}\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(L)\otimes\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(R)

(92)

Fix
L,R
and a communication transcript
c\in\{0,1\}^{*}
. Since the tampering is performed using only
\mathsf{LOCC}
operations, the post-tampered states must still be separable across the left-right partition. Thereby, WLOG, the post-tampered states are described by CP maps
\mathcal{E}^{L,c},\mathcal{E}^{R,c}
,

\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(m)=\sum_{c}\mathbb{E}_{L,R\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{NM}}(m)}\bigg(\mathcal{E}^{L,c}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(L)\bigg)\otimes\bigg(\mathcal{E}^{R,c}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(R)\bigg).

(93)

If we denote the real numbers
q_{L,c}=\Tr\mathcal{E}^{L,c}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(L)
,
q_{R,c}=\Tr\mathcal{E}^{R,c}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(R)
, and corresponding density matrices

\sigma^{L,c}=\mathcal{E}^{L,c}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(L)/q_{L,c}\text{ and }\sigma^{R,c}=\mathcal{E}^{R,c}\circ\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(R)/q_{R,c},

(94)

Then the probability the communication transcript is
c
is
\mathbb{P}_{c|L,R}=q_{L,c}\cdot q_{R,c}
, and the resulting post-tampered state is precisely as in the statement of
7.6
.
∎

8
Non-Malleable Secret Sharing Schemes against
\mathsf{LOCC}

The main result of this section is a construction of a threshold secret sharing scheme for classical messages which is both data hiding and non-malleable against local operations and classical communication between the parties. For basic definitions of data hiding and non-malleable secret sharing, we refer the reader to
Section

2.6
and
Section

2.5.3
respectively.

Theorem 8.1

(
Theorem

1.3
, restatement)
.

For every number of parties
p
, threshold
t\geq 3
and message length
k=\Omega(\log p)
, there exists a
t
-out-of-
p
threshold secret sharing scheme which is non-malleable against
\mathsf{LOCC}
with reconstruction, privacy and non-malleability error
2^{-(kp)^{\Omega(1)}}
, as well as inverse-polynomial rate
p^{-O(1)}
.

Our construction is inspired by a compiler from
[
ADN+19
]
, who constructed leakage-resilient and non-malleable secret sharing schemes from generic secret sharing schemes. In this section, we show how to combine bipartite
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding schemes with non-malleable secret sharing schemes within their compiler, to construct secret sharing schemes which are non-malleable against
\mathsf{LOCC}
.

We organize the rest of this section as follows. In
Section

8.1
we present the ingredients and overview our code construction. In
Section

8.2.1
we prove its data hiding property, and in
Section

8.2.2
we present the proof of non-malleability.

8.1
Code Construction

8.1.1
Ingredients

We refer the reader to
Section

2.6
and
Section

2.5.3
for basic definitions of the following ingredients:

1.

An
(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}})
-2-out-of-2 data hiding scheme
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}})
with separable state encodings, such as that of
Theorem

2.9
from
[
EW02
]
.

2.

A
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},\varepsilon_{c},\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}})
-non-malleable secret sharing scheme
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{SS}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{SS}})
which is non-malleable against
\mathcal{F}_{p,t}^{\text{joint}}
, such as that of
Theorem

2.7
from
[
GK18a
]
.

As previously discussed in the introduction, we require non-malleable secret sharing schemes which are secure against a (relatively mild) joint tampering model. However, we appeal to constructions secure against
\mathcal{F}_{p,t}^{\text{joint}}
(
Definition

2.21
) for simplicity, as it is the most well studied joint tampering model.

8.1.2
Overview

We formally present the encoding channel
\mathsf{Enc}
in
Algorithm

10
and decoding channel
Algorithm

5
. An outline follows: First, a message
m\in\{0,1\}^{k}
is encoded into classical shares
(M_{1},\cdots,M_{p})
, using a classical non-malleable secret sharing scheme. Then, each party
i\in[p]
creates
(p-1)
copies of their share
M_{i}
and encodes each of them into the
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding scheme
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{i})
. This results in
p-1
bipartite registers
(X_{i,j},Y_{i,j})_{j\in[p]\setminus\{i\}}
for each
i
, and the register
Y_{i,j}
is handed to the
j
th party (while
i
keeps all of the
X_{i,j}
).

Input:

A
k
bit message
m\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, and an integer
\lambda
.

Output:

A code-state defined on
p
registers
S_{1},\cdots,S_{p}

1

2

1:

Encode
m
into a secret sharing scheme
(M_{1},\cdots,M_{p})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{SS}}(m)
.

3

2:

For each
i\in[p]
, produce
p-1
copies
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{i})
of a 2-out-of-2 data hiding scheme encoding of the
i
th share. For
i\in[p],j\in[p]\setminus\{i\}
, let
X_{i,j},Y_{i,j}
be the corresponding bipartite register.

4

3:

For each
i\in[p]
, define the
i
th share
S_{i}
to be the concatenation of quantum registers

S_{i}=\bigg\{X_{i,j}\text{ and }Y_{j,i}:j\in[p]\setminus\{i\}\bigg\}

(95)

Algorithm 10

\mathsf{Enc}
:

Input:

A subset
T\subset[p]
,
|T|\geq t
and a density matrix
\rho_{T}
supported on
S_{T}
.

Output:

A classical
k
bit message
m^{\prime}
.

1

2

1:

Partition
T=T_{1}\cup T_{2}\cup\cdots\cup T_{\lfloor\frac{|T|}{2}\rfloor}
into pairs or triples of vertices.

3

2:

For each subset
T_{j}
, decode the data hiding schemes within
T_{j}
by applying
\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
on all registers
X_{u,v},Y_{u,v}
for pairs
u,v\in T_{j}
until every share in
T_{j}
has been decoded. Let
M^{\prime}_{u}
for
u\in T
be the recovered classical shares.

4

3:

Decode shares
\{M^{\prime}_{u}\}_{u\in T}
using
\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{SS}}
.

Algorithm 11

\mathsf{Dec}
:

Due to the redundancy in the encoding, if any two parties
u,v
had access to quantum communication, then they could jointly learn their shares
M_{u},M_{v}
. From the threshold secrecy of the secret sharing scheme, if only
T\subset[p],|T|\leq t-1
parties has access to quantum communication, then naturally they would learn a negligible amount of information about the message. We begin by arguing that
\mathsf{Enc}
above actually has an even stronger data hiding property: it is a data hiding scheme with a threshold access structure (
Definition

2.27
). In
Lemma

8.2
, we prove that even if the parties in
([p]\setminus T)
could aid
T
by means of local operations and unbounded classical communication,
T
would still not be able to distinguish the message.

Lemma 8.2
.

\mathsf{Enc}
defined in
Algorithm

10
defines a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{c}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime})
-secret sharing scheme which is
\mathsf{LOCC}
data hiding, with reconstruction error
\varepsilon_{c}^{\prime}=p\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{c}
and privacy error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime}=O(p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}})
. Moreover, if the share size of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
is
s_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
, then the share size of the resulting construction is
2\cdot s_{\mathsf{LOCC}}\cdot p
.

Note that this “data hiding” or leakage resilience (against classical communication) follows directly from the privacy property of the underlying classical secret sharing scheme, and doesn’t require any non-malleability.

Lemma 8.3
.

(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
defined in
Algorithm

10
,
Algorithm

5
are non-malleable with respect to
\mathsf{LOCC}_{p}
with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}^{\prime}=O(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}\cdot p^{2})
.

8.1.3
Parameters

By instantiating our compiler with

1.

The efficient, separable-state
(2^{-\Omega(\lambda)},2^{-\Omega(\lambda)})
bipartite data hiding scheme
Theorem

2.9

[
EW02
]
message length
b
and blocklength
2b\cdot\lambda^{2}
.

2.

The
(p,t,0,0,2^{-b^{\Omega(1)}})-\mathsf{NMSS}
against
\mathcal{F}_{p,t}^{\text{joint}}
of share size
b
and rate
p^{-O(1)}
guaranteed by
Theorem

2.7

[
GK18a
]
.

If we fix a message length
k
, then
b=k\cdot p^{O(1)}
, and we can further fix
\lambda=b^{\Theta(1)}
. We obtain

Theorem 8.4
.

For every number of parties
p
, threshold
t\geq 3
and message length
k=\Omega(\log p)
, there exists a
t
-out-of-
p
threshold secret sharing scheme which is non-malleable against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
with reconstruction, privacy and non-malleability error
2^{-(kp)^{\Omega(1)}}
, as well as inverse-polynomial rate
p^{-O(1)}
.

8.2
Analysis

8.2.1
Correctness and Privacy

In the below, we refer to
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
as the set of measurements performed by LOCC on
p
parties.

Claim 8.5

(Correctness)
.

Suppose
\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{SS}}
have reconstruction error
\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}},\varepsilon_{c}
respectively. Then the reconstruction error for
\mathsf{Dec}
is
\leq p\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{c}
.

Proof.

For a fixed access structure and a valid subset of parties
T\subset[p]
, the parties in
T
first attempt to recover their classical shares, and then use these shares to decode the classical secret sharing scheme. The reconstruction succeeds if the
p

\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
protocols and the
\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{SS}}
both succeed, which occurs with probability
\geq 1-p\cdot\delta_{\mathsf{LOCC}}-\varepsilon_{c}
.
∎

Fix a subset
T\subset[p]
, and let
\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}
be the tampering channels corresponding to arbitrary quantum communication within
T
while the parties in
[p]\setminus T
are restricted to
\mathsf{LOCC}
operations. Suppose now we fix two messages
m\neq\tilde{m}
. We prove privacy against measurements in
\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}
,
|T|\leq t-1
by considering the following hybrids:

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The message
m
is encoded into the code,
\mathsf{Enc}(m)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
: The message
m
is encoded into the code, however, all registers
(X_{i,j},Y_{i,j})
when either of
i,j\notin T
, are replaced by encodings of a fixed string
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}
: The message
\tilde{m}
is encoded into the code, however, all registers
(X_{i,j},Y_{i,j})
when either of
i,j\notin T
, are replaced by encodings of a fixed string
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
: The message
\tilde{m}
is encoded into the code,
\mathsf{Enc}(\tilde{m})
.

Let
\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{i}}
be the density matrix on the
p
shares during Hybrid
i
. Our first claim argues that using
\mathsf{LOCC}
between
T,[p]\setminus T
cannot help to distinguish messages:

Claim 8.6
.

\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}},\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}\leq p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
.

Together with the statistical privacy of the secret sharing scheme, we prove statistical privacy against
\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}
:

Claim 8.7

(Privacy)
.

For any two messages
m\neq\tilde{m}
and every
T\subset[p],|T|\leq t-1
, the privacy error

\|\mathsf{Enc}(m)-\mathsf{Enc}(\tilde{m})\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}\leq 2\cdot p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}

Proof.

[of
8.7
] Fix
|T|\leq t-1
. Since
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
is separable, both
\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}},\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}}
can be written as tensor products
\rho^{m}_{T}\otimes\sigma,\rho^{\tilde{m}}_{T}\otimes\sigma
for some fixed density matrix
\sigma
, and where
\rho^{m}_{T}
only depends of the shares in
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{SS}}(m)_{T}
(similarly for
\tilde{m}
). Thus, by the statistical privacy of the secret sharing scheme,

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}=\|(\rho^{m}_{T}-\rho^{\tilde{m}}_{T})\otimes\sigma\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}\leq\|\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{SS}}(m)_{T}-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{SS}}(\tilde{m})_{T}\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}.

(96)

From
8.6
and the triangle inequality,

\|\mathsf{Enc}(m_{a})-\mathsf{Enc}(m_{b})\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}\leq\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}+\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}+\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}\leq

(97)

\leq 2p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}
.
∎

Proof.

[of
8.6
] It suffices to prove the hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0},\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}
are indistinguishable for all messages
m
, as the case
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2},\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}
is analogous. To do so, we introduce another sequence of hybrids, which replaces each data hiding scheme
(X_{u,v},Y_{u,v})
where either
u,v\notin T
one by one.

Suppose we define an ordering
1,\cdots,p
to the vertices in
[p]
, and an ordering
1,\cdots,p-|T|
to the vertices of
[p]\setminus T
. For every
a\in\{1,\cdots,p\},b\in\{1,\cdots,p-|T|\}
, we define:

\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b}
: The message
m
is encoded into the code, however, if
i
is in the first
(a-1)
vertices of
[p]
then we replace the registers
(X_{i,j},Y_{i,j})
with the fixed state
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
for each
j\in[p]\setminus T
. Additionally, for
j\in\{1,\cdots,b\}
the registers
(X_{a,j},Y_{a,j})
are replaced with
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
.

We denote
\mathsf{Hyb}_{a+1,0}
by the hybrid which has already replaced all the data hiding schemes encoding
s_{i}
(where
i\leq a
) held by parties outside
T
by encodings of
x
. Note that
\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,p-|T|}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{a+1,0}
. By a triangle inequality,

\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}\leq p^{2}\cdot\max_{a\in\{1,\cdots p\}}\max_{b\in\{0,1,\cdots p-|T|-1\}}\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b+1}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}

(98)

However,
\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b}},\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b+1}}
differ only in the bipartite register
(X_{a,u_{b+1}},Y_{a,u_{b+1}})
, where
a
is the
a
th vertex of
[p]
and
u_{b+1}
is the
(b+1)
st neighbor of
a
outside
T
. Thus, there is some separable state
\sigma
such that
\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b}}=\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{a})\otimes\sigma
,
\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b+1}}=\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)\otimes\sigma
. By an averaging argument,

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b}}-\rho_{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a,b+1}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}=\|(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{a})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x))\otimes\sigma\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}\leq

(99)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\otimes_{u}^{p}\psi_{u}}\|(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{a})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x))\otimes_{u}^{p}\psi_{u}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}}=\|\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{a})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},

(100)

where finally we used that vertices
a,u_{b+1}
can distinguish between
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{a}),\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
with a bipartite
\mathsf{LOCC}
protocol with the same bias as any
\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}
protocol for
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{a})\otimes\sigma,\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)\otimes_{u}^{p}\psi_{u}
, by preparing
\otimes_{u}^{p}\psi_{u}
using
\mathsf{LOCC}
and simulating the
\mathsf{LOCC}_{T}
protocol.
∎

8.2.2
Non-Malleability

Our proof proceeds via a non-malleable reduction, from individual tampering on the quantum secret sharing scheme
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
to joint tampering
\mathcal{F}^{joint}_{p,t}
on a classical non-malleable secret sharing scheme. To do so, we reason that the distribution over the recovered shares
M^{\prime}_{T}=(M^{\prime}_{u},u\in T)
, during the decoding algorithm in
Algorithm

5
and conditioned on the shares before tampering
M_{T}=(M_{u},u\in T)
, is near a convex combination over joint tampering functions
\mathcal{F}^{joint}_{p,t}
on
S_{T}
. We prove:

Claim 8.8

(Non-Malleability)
.

If
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
has privacy error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
and
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{SS}},\mathsf{Dec}_{\mathsf{SS}})
is non-malleable against
\mathcal{F}^{joint}_{p,t\geq 5}
with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
, then
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is non-malleable against
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
with error
\leq 3\cdot p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
.

To prove
8.8
, we begin similarly to the proof of privacy and establish a sequence of hybrids. Our goal will be to start with the encoding of a fixed message
\mathsf{Enc}(m)
, and then to (gradually) replace all the data hiding schemes which are between parties not in the same partition in
\mathcal{P}
, by encodings of a fixed string
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
. Let
N_{\text{pairs}}=\sum_{i\neq j\in\mathcal{P}}|P_{i}||P_{j}|+|T|(p-|T|)
denote the number of bipartite data hiding schemes between different partitions of
[p]
. We define

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
: The message
m
is encoded into the code,
\mathsf{Enc}(m)
.

For every integer
0\leq a\leq N_{\text{pairs}}
,

\mathsf{Hyb}_{a}
: The message
m
is encoded into the code, however, the first
a
data hiding schemes
(X_{i,j},Y_{i,j})
where
i,j
are in different sides of the
(T,[p]\setminus T)
cut or in different partitions of
T
in
\mathcal{P}
, are replaced by encodings of a fixed string
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}
: The message
m
is encoded into the code, however, all registers
(X_{i,j},Y_{i,j})
where either of
i,j
are in different partitions of
T
in
\mathcal{P}
, are replaced by encodings of a fixed string
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
.

Recall that the random variable
M^{\prime}_{T}
is the collection of recovered classical shares of the subset
T
(after tampering). If we denote
\mathbb{P}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}
the distribution over
S_{T}^{\prime}
conditioned on the shares
S_{T}
before tampering (and the message), then clearly it is captured by the
0
th Hybrid
\mathbb{P}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}=\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}
. Since Hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}
differ only in separable state data hiding schemes, then following the proof of privacy in
8.6
, we reason that their distributions over recovered shares are near indistinguishable. As otherwise, their distinguishability could be turned into a bipartite
\mathsf{LOCC}
protocol which distinguishes a data-hidden share from the fixed string
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
:

Claim 8.9
.

For all
m,m_{T}
,
\|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}-\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}\|_{1}\leq p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}

Now, note that once one conditions on the shares
m_{T}
, the density matrix in the final Hybrid
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}
can be expressed as a product state across the partitions of
\mathcal{P}
(which depend on the shares
s_{T}
), and a global separable state:

\rho^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}|_{M_{T}=m_{T}}=\gamma_{m_{T}}\otimes\sigma=\otimes_{j\in\mathcal{P}}\gamma_{m_{P_{j}}}\otimes\sigma.

(101)

\gamma_{m_{T}}
is supported on the bipartite registers
(X_{u,v},Y_{u,v})
where
u,v
both are in the same partition of
\mathcal{P}
, and thereby factorizes into a product state across different partitions.
\sigma=\mathbb{E}_{z\leftarrow Z}\psi^{z}
, where the
\psi^{z}
are product states, represents all the copies of the fixed state
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
.

Suppose we fix
m_{T},Z=z
, and the
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
tampering channel
\Lambda
. If the random variable
C\in\{0,1\}^{*}
denotes the classical transcript of the protocol, then let
\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z}^{\Lambda}
be the conditional distribution over the transcript. We begin by claiming that, conditioned on
M_{T},Z
and the transcript
C
, the post-tampered density matrix remains a tensor product state across the partition
\mathcal{P}
:

Claim 8.10
.

For all
M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z,C=c
, there exist density matrices
\sigma_{m_{P}}^{c,z}
for each partition
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
\sigma_{u}^{c,z}
for
u\in[p]\setminus T
such that the post-tampered state

\Lambda(\rho^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}|_{M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z})=\mathbb{E}_{c\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z}}\big(\bigotimes_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sigma_{m_{P}}^{c,z}\bigotimes_{u\in[p]\setminus T}\sigma_{u}^{c,z}\big)

(102)

Moreover, we claim the distribution over the transcript
\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z}
is almost independent of the shares, as otherwise one could break the bipartite security of the data hiding scheme:

Claim 8.11
.

For all
\Lambda\in\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
and for every fixed value
Z=z,M_{T}=m_{T}
:
\|\mathcal{D}^{\Lambda}_{|M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z}-\mathcal{D}_{Z=z}^{\Lambda}\|_{1}\leq(p+1)\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
.

We can now put everything together and prove the main claim of this subsection:

Proof.

[of
8.8
] From
8.11
and
8.10

\bigg\|\Lambda(\rho^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}|_{M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z})-\mathbb{E}_{z}\mathbb{E}_{c\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\Lambda}_{z}}\big(\bigotimes_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sigma_{m_{P}}^{c,z}\big)\otimes\sigma_{[p]\setminus T}^{c,z}\bigg\|_{1}\leq(p+1)\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}

(103)

By monotonicity of trace distance, the fact our decoding algorithm only acts only within the same partition of
\mathcal{P}
,

\|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}-\mathbb{E}_{z}\mathbb{E}_{c\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\Lambda}_{z}}\prod_{j\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}_{M^{\prime}_{P}|M_{P}=m_{P},c,z}\|_{1}\leq 2\cdot p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}

(104)

Thereby, the distribution over the tampered shares in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}
is near that of a convex combination of functions in
\mathcal{F}_{p,t}^{joint}
. However, via
8.9
, the distribution over the tampered shares in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
must also be near a convex combination
\mathcal{F}_{p,t}^{joint}
. By the non-malleability of the classical secret sharing scheme and the triangle inequality, we conclude the distribution over recovered messages
M^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}
must be
\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+3\cdot p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}}
close in statistical distance to a convex combination of the original message or an uncorrelated message.
∎

8.2.3
Deferred Proofs

Proof.

[of
8.9
] Consider the hybrids
\mathsf{Hyb}_{a}:a\in[N_{\text{pairs}}]
, which replace all the data hiding schemes outside
T
by encodings of the fixed message
x
, one by one. By the triangle inequality, there exists
a^{*}
such that

\|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}-\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}\|_{1}\leq p^{2}\cdot\|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}-\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}+1}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}\|_{1}

(105)

However,
\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}+1}
differ only in a single bipartite register
(X_{u,v},Y_{u,v})
, and all the remaining registers in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}+1}
are fixed to a separable density matrix
\sigma_{a^{*}}
. Thereby, since
u,v
are in different partitions of
T
, parties
u,v
are able to distinguish between encodings of the share and the fixed string
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(m_{u}),\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
using a bipartite
\mathsf{LOCC}
protocol which simply simulates the decoding algorithm in
Algorithm

5
to obtain the post-tampered shares
M^{\prime}_{T}
. If the conditional distribution on
M^{\prime}_{T}
is distinguishable, then so is
\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(M_{u}),\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)
:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}-\mathbb{P}^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{a^{*}+1}}_{M^{\prime}_{T}|M_{T}=m_{T},m}\|_{1}\leq\|(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(m_{u})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x))_{u,v}\otimes\sigma_{a^{*}}\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}_{\mathcal{P}}}=

(106)

\displaystyle=\|\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(m_{u})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(x)\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},

(107)

where in the above we referred to
\mathsf{LOCC}_{\mathcal{P}}
as the class of channels implementable via quantum communication within partitions of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathsf{LOCC}
between partitions and out of
T
.
∎

Proof.

[of
8.10
] Recall that
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
tampering channels act as collections of CP maps
\Lambda_{u}^{c}
on each
u\in[p]
, which depend only on
\Lambda
and the
transcript

c\in\{0,1\}^{*}
of the classical communication in the protocol. The post-tampered state can thereby be expressed as a mixture over CP maps on
\gamma_{m_{T}}
:

\displaystyle\Lambda(\rho^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}|_{S_{T}=s_{t},Z=z})=\sum_{c}(\bigotimes_{u\in[p]}\Lambda_{u}^{c})\circ(\rho^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}|_{M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z})=

(108)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c}(\bigotimes_{u\in[p]}\Lambda_{u}^{c})\circ(\gamma_{m_{T}}\otimes\psi_{z})=\sum_{c}\bigg(\bigotimes_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\tilde{\sigma}_{m_{P}}^{c,z}\bigotimes_{u\in[p]\setminus T}\tilde{\sigma}_{u}^{c,z}\bigg).

(109)

Where each
\tilde{\sigma}
is a PSD matrix (or an unnormalized density matrix), and note we used the fact that
\gamma_{m_{T}}
factorizes across
\mathcal{P}
. Morever, on average over
c
,
\Lambda^{c}
must be trace preserving:
\sum_{c}\Tr\big(\otimes_{u\in[p]} \Lambda^{c}_u\big)(A)=\Tr A
. By normalizing each
\tilde{\sigma}
by its trace, we can treat the transcript
c
as a random variable with conditional distribution
\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z}(c)=\prod_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\Tr\tilde{\sigma}_{m_{P}}^{c,z}\prod_{u\in[p]\setminus T}\Tr\tilde{\sigma}_{u}^{c,z}
, such that:

\displaystyle\Lambda(\rho^{\mathsf{Hyb}_{\text{Final}}}|_{M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z})=\mathbb{E}_{c\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=m_{T},Z=z}}\big(\bigotimes_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sigma_{m_{P}}^{c,z}\bigotimes_{u\in[p]\setminus T}\sigma_{u}^{c,z}\big)

(110)

∎

Proof.

[of
8.11
] Following the now standard hybrid argument and triangle inequality, suppose two conditional distributions over transcripts
\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=a,Z=z},\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=b,Z=z}
for two different sets of shares
M_{T}=a,b
were distinguishable. Then, there is a pair of parties
u,v\in T
within the same subset in the partition of
T
and a fixed separable state
\sigma
, which is distinguishable with comparable bias using
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
:

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=a,Z=z}-\mathcal{D}_{|M_{T}=b,Z=z}\|_{1}\leq(p+1)\cdot\|(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(a_{u})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(b_{u}))_{u,v}\otimes\sigma\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}}=

(111)

\displaystyle=(p+1)\cdot\|\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(a_{u})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(b_{u})\|_{\mathsf{LOCC}}\leq(p+1)\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{LOCC}},

(112)

where we note the
\mathsf{LOCC}^{p}
protocol distinguishing
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(a_{u})-\mathsf{Enc}_{\mathsf{LOCC}}(b_{u}))_{u,v}\otimes\sigma
can be simulated by a bipartite protocol between
u,v
.
∎

9
Connections to Quantum Encryption

A remarkable property of quantum authentication schemes
[
BCG+01
]
is that they encrypt the quantum message
19
19

19

That is, any adversary which is oblivious to the internal randomness shared by the encoder & decoder, can learn nothing about the encoded state. This naturally is in contrast to classical authentication, where oftentimes a short authentication tag suffices, leaving the message “in the clear”.
. Since quantum non-malleable and tamper-detection codes are relaxations of quantum authentication codes, it may not seem too surprising that they inherit similar properties. In this section, we show they satisfy a related notion of encryption:

Definition 9.1
.

We refer to a quantum code
\mathsf{Enc}
on
t
registers as
single share encrypting
with error
\delta
if the reduced density matrix on each register is independent of the message:
\forall\psi_{0},\psi_{1}
and
i\in[t]
,

\|\Tr_{\neg i}\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{0})-\Tr_{\neg i}\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{1})\|_{1}\leq\delta

(113)

We prove that each share of a split-state quantum tamper-detection code must be encrypted:

Theorem 9.1

(
Theorem

1.5
, restatement)
.

Any
t
-split quantum tamper-detection code against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
with error
\varepsilon
must be single share encrypting with error
\Delta\leq 4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/2}
.

As previously discussed, classical non-malleable codes with
3
or more shares do
not
satisfy single share encryption. However, due to limitations in our proof approach in
Theorem

9.1
, it still remains open whether quantum non-malleable codes do. Nevertheless, by combining
Theorem

9.1
with our reduction from
Section

3
, we show that one can always easily turn a quantum non-malleable code into one which encrypts its shares, with arbitrarily small changes to its rate and error:

Corollary 9.2
.

Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a
t
-split quantum non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
with error
\varepsilon
and message length
k
. For
0<\lambda<k
, consider the quantum code
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Dec}_{\lambda})
of message length
k-\lambda
defined by encoding
\psi
together with
\lambda
random bits into
\mathsf{Enc}
. Then,
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Dec}_{\lambda})
is a quantum non-malleable code with error
\varepsilon
, and is single share encrypting with error
4\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon+2^{1-\lambda}}
.

In a nutshell,
\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda}
simply “pads” the message state using
\lambda
random bits, which are ignored during decoding. To conclude this section, our last connection to encryption is an analog to a well known classical result by
[
ADKO15
]
for 2-split-state non-malleable codes. We prove that quantum non-malleable codes against entangled
2
split-state adversaries must always be
2
-out-of-
2
secret sharing:

Lemma 9.3
.

Fix
\varepsilon\leq 1/16
. Any
\varepsilon
-secure quantum non-malleable code against entangled 2-split-state adversaries
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is single share encrypting with privacy error
\delta\leq 32\cdot\varepsilon
.

We emphasize that this is not implied by
Corollary

9.2
, which requires padding the input. Our proof approach is similar to that of
[
ADKO15
]
. Informally, we reason that if two code-states
\ket{a},\ket{b}
are distinguishable by acting only on a subset of the qubits of the code-state, then we will be able to swap between encodings of
\ket{a},\ket{b}
with some non-trivial bias. We dedicate
Section

9.2
to its proof.

9.1
t
-Split Tamper-Detection Codes Encrypt their Shares

To prove
Theorem

9.1
, we follow a proof approach is analogous to that of
[
BCG+01
]
. At a high level, we leverage an equivalence between distinguishing two states
\ket{a},\ket{b}
(even with some tiny bias) and mapping
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{a}+\ket{b})
to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{a}-\ket{b})
. We make fundamental use of a lemma by
[
GJMZ22
]
(and shown similarly in
[
AAS20
]
):

Lemma 9.4

(
[
GJMZ22
]
, Lemma 6.6.ii)
.

Suppose a distinguisher
D
implemented using a binary projective measurement
(\Pi,\mathbb{I}-\Pi)
distinguishes
\ket{x},\ket{y}
with bias
\Delta
, i.e.
|\bra{x}\Pi\ket{x}-\bra{y}\Pi\ket{y}|\geq\Delta
. Then, the unitary (reflection)
U=\mathbb{I}-2\Pi
maps the between the following superpositions with fidelity bias:

\bigg|\bigg(\frac{\bra{x}-\bra{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)U\bigg(\frac{\ket{x}+\ket{y}}{\sqrt{2}}\bigg)\bigg|\geq\Delta

(114)

As a consequence, we reason that if one of the
t
adversaries is able to distinguish information about the message using just
their share
, then they can also map between messages with some non-negligible advantage.

Proof.

[of
Theorem

9.1
] For the purpose of contradiction, suppose two messages
\ket{a'},\ket{b'}
are distinguishable on one of their shares, with statistical distance
\geq 4\Delta
. Then, by the triangle inequality and an averaging argument, there exists a pair of orthogonal states
\ket{a},\ket{b}
with statistical distance
\geq 2\Delta
on that share. We thus restrict our attention to the distiguishability of orthogonal states.

If
\|\Tr_{\neg i}\mathsf{Enc}(\ket{a})-\Tr_{\neg i}\mathsf{Enc}(\ket{b})\|_{1}\geq 2\Delta
for some
i\in[t]
, then let
(\Pi_{D},\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{D})
be the measurement which optimally distinguishes the two reduced density matrices on the
i
th share with bias
\geq\Delta
. By
Lemma

9.4
, that implies the
i
th adversary can map between
\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{+}),\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{-})
with fidelity
\geq\Delta
using
U_{i}=\mathbb{I}-2\Pi
, where
\ket{\psi_\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{a}\pm\ket{b})
. That is,

\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{-}),(\mathbb{I}_{\neg i}\otimes U_{i})\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{+})))\geq\Delta

(115)

By monotonicity of fidelity, we conclude that after applying the decoding channel,
i
is able to swap the messages with non-trivial bias:

\mathsf{F}(\psi_{-},\mathsf{Dec}\circ(\mathbb{I}_{\neg i}\otimes U_{i})\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{+})))=\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{Dec}\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{-}),\mathsf{Dec}\circ(\mathbb{I}_{\neg i}\otimes U_{i})\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{+})))\geq\Delta.

(116)

By the the tamper detection definition, there must exist
p\in[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Dec}\circ(\mathbb{I}_{\neg i}\otimes U_{i})\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{+}))\approx_{\varepsilon}p\cdot\psi_{+}+(1-p)\cdot\bot

(117)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}^{2}(\psi_{-},\mathsf{Dec}\circ(\mathbb{I}_{\neg i}\otimes U_{i})\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{+})))=\bra{\psi_-}\mathsf{Dec}\circ(\mathbb{I}_{\neg i}\otimes U_{i})\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{+})\ket{\psi_-}\leq\varepsilon

(118)

which violates the tamper detection definition unless
\Delta\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}
.
∎

Leveraging the secrecy of tamper-detection codes, we now prove our result on non-malleable secret sharing.

Proof.

[of
Corollary

9.2
] Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a
t
-split-state non-malleable code with error
\varepsilon
, and let
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Dec}_{\lambda})
be the
(t+1)
-split-state tamper-detection code guaranteed by
Theorem

3.1
with
\lambda<k
EPR pairs and error
\varepsilon+2^{1-\lambda}
. By
Theorem

9.1
,
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Dec}_{\lambda})
is single share encrypting with error
4\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon+2^{1-\lambda}}
. However, note that the reduced density matrix of
\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda}(\psi)
on any single share
i\in[t]
, for any
k-\lambda
qubit message state
\psi
, is the same as that of the non-malleable encoding of
\psi
together with
\lambda
random bits:
\Tr_{\neg i}\mathsf{Enc}(\psi\otimes\mathbb{I}/2^{\lambda})=\Tr_{\neg i}\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda}(\psi)
. Since
\|\Tr_{\neg i}\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda}(\psi)-\rho_{i}\|_{1}\leq 4\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon+2^{1-\lambda}}
for some fixed state
\rho_{i}
by
Theorem

9.1
, we conclude that
\mathsf{Enc}
must be single share encrypting with error
4\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon+2^{1-\lambda}}
if
\psi
is padded with
\lambda
random bits.
∎

9.2
2-Split Non-Malleable Codes are 2-out-of-2 Secret Sharing

In this subsection we prove
Lemma

9.3
, that 2-split-state non-malleable codes against entangled adversaries are secret sharing. For the purpose of constradiction, suppose two states
\ket{\psi_0'},\ket{\psi_1'}
are distinguishable on their left halves, with statistical distance
\geq\delta
. Then, by the triangle inequality and an averaging argument, there exists a pair of orthogonal states
\ket{\psi_0},\ket{\psi_1}
with statistical distance
\geq\delta/2
on their left shares, and thereby we restrict our attention to the distiguishability of orthogonal states.

Our proof proceeds by constructing channels which leverage this distinguishability to break the non-malleability guarantee. To do so, first, in
9.5
we show that it suffices to construct a CPTP map
\Lambda_{LR}
20
20

20

Which can be implemented by local operations of the left and right adversaries with access to pre-shared copies of
\rho^{b}=\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{b})
for
b\in\{0,1\}
, which converts the encoding
\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{b})=\rho^{b}_{LR}
of
\psi_{b}
into that of
\psi_{\neg b}
(the opposite bit) with some fidelity bias to show a contradiction:

Claim 9.5
.

Let
\rho^{b}_{out}=\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{b})
denote the the state output by the decoder. If
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is an
\varepsilon
non-malleable code against
\Lambda
, then the fidelity of
\rho^{\neg b}_{out}
with
\psi_{b}
can never be much larger than that of
\rho^{b}_{out}
with
\psi_{b}
:

\mathsf{F}^{2}(\rho^{\neg b}_{out},\psi_{b})-\mathsf{F}^{2}(\rho^{b}_{out},\psi_{b})=\bra{\psi_b}\rho^{\neg b}_{out}-\rho^{b}_{out}\ket{\psi_b}\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon

(119)

Now, assume there exists a pair of orthogonal states
\ket{\psi_0},\ket{\psi_1}
whose split-state shares are distinguishable, i.e.

\|\Tr_{R}\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{0})-\Tr_{R}\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{1})\|_{1}=\delta_{L}\quad;\quad\|\Tr_{L}\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{0})-\Tr_{L}\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{1})\|_{1}=\delta_{R}.

(120)

and WLOG
\delta_{L}=\max\{\delta_{L},\delta_{R}\}
. Recall the distinguishability implies the existance of a pair of POVMs
(M_{L},\mathbb{I}-M_{L})
(and
(M_{R},\mathbb{I}-M_{R})
) supported only on
L
(and
R
), which distinguish the encodings of the pair with certain bias:

\mathbb{P}[\text{outcome }M_{L}=0|b=0]=\Tr[\bigg(M_{L}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{R}\bigg)\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{0})]=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4},

(121)

and analogously for
b=1
and
R
. In
Algorithm

5
and
Algorithm

4
, we use the measurements
M_{L},M_{R}
to construct two channels
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
with large fidelity bias (in terms of on
\delta_{L},\delta_{R}
). Together with
9.5
, we obtain upper bounds on
\delta_{L},\delta_{R}
.

Input:

The bipartite state
\rho^{b}_{LR}=\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{b})
for some
b\in\{0,1\}

Output:

Some bipartite state
\Lambda_{1}(\rho^{b}_{LR})
.

1

2

1:

The split state adversaries share valid encodings
\rho^{0}_{LR},\rho^{1}_{LR}
of both
\ket{\psi_0},\ket{\psi_1}
in advance.

3

2:

Upon receiving
\rho^{b}_{LR}
, the left adversary measures
(M_{L},\mathbb{I}-M_{L})
, obtaining a bit
m\in\{0,1\}
.

4

3:

The left outputs their pre-shared copy of the encoding of
\neg b
, and the right their pre-shared copy of the encoding of a random bit
r\in\{0,1\}
.

Algorithm 12

\Lambda_{1}
: Right’s output is entangled

Informally,
\Lambda_{1}
attempts to distinguish
b
, and is able to output the encoding of
\neg b
with some non-trivial bias:

Claim 9.6
.

\forall b\in\{0,1\}
, the state
\rho_{out}^{b}=\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda_{1}\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{b})
output by the receiver can be written as

\rho_{out}^{b}=\big(1-\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\big)\cdot\sigma+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\cdot\psi_{\neg b}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\cdot\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{\neg b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{b}_{R})

(122)

where we used WLOG
\delta_{L}=\max\{\delta_{L},\delta_{R}\}
, and
\sigma
is some fixed density matrix independent of
b
.

Unfortunately, this intuition is just slightly not enough to prove our result, as it is technically possible that the unentangled state
\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{\neg b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{b}_{R})\approx\psi_{b}
, “spoiling” our advantage in breaking the non-malleability. However, this can only happen if, coincidentally, an unentangled copy of
\rho^{b}_{R}
somehow determines the message. Leveraging this intuition, we formulate a set of channels which ensure that if the code-states are near separable, then the channels certainly break the non-malleability guarantee:

Input:

The bipartite state
\rho^{b}_{LR}=\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{b})
for some
b\in\{0,1\}
, and two bits
r,l\in\{0,1\}

Output:

A product state
\rho^{m}_{L}\otimes\rho^{r}_{R}
for some
m\in\{0,1\}
correlated with
b
and some fixed
r\in\{0,1\}

1

2

1:

The right adversary outputs the
R
half of a fresh encoding of
\rho_{R}^{r}
.

3

2:

The left adversary measures
(M_{L},\mathbb{I}-M_{L})
obtaining outcome
m\in\{0,1\}
, and outputs
\rho_{L}^{m\oplus l}
.

Algorithm 13

\Lambda_{2}^{r,l}
: Right’s output is fixed (and unentangled).

Claim 9.7
.

\forall b,r,l\in\{0,1\}
, the state
\rho^{b,r,l}=\mathsf{Dec}\circ\Lambda_{2}^{r,l}\circ\mathsf{Enc}(\psi_{b})
output by the receiver satisfies

\rho^{b,r,l}=\bigg(1-\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\bigg)\cdot\sigma_{r,l}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\cdot\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{L}^{b\oplus l}\otimes\rho_{R}^{r}),

(123)

where
\sigma_{r,l}
doesn’t depend on
b
.

We now put these claims together to prove the lemma:

Proof.

[of
Lemma

9.3
] Applying
9.5
to the channel
\Lambda_{2}^{r,l=0}
we obtain (from
9.7
) that for all
b,r\in\{0,1\}
:

2\varepsilon\geq\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\cdot\bra{\psi_b}\mathsf{Dec}\bigg((\rho_{L}^{\neg b}-\rho_{L}^{b})\otimes\rho_{R}^{r}\bigg)\ket{\psi_b}

(124)

And analogously, with
l=1
, for all
b,r\in\{0,1\}
:

\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\cdot\bra{\psi_b}\mathsf{Dec}\bigg((\rho_{L}^{\neg b}-\rho_{L}^{b})\otimes\rho_{R}^{r}\bigg)\ket{\psi_b}\geq-2\varepsilon.

(125)

By combining the above (with
r=b
) with Holder’s inequality, we observe

|\bra{\psi_b}\mathsf{Dec}\bigg((\rho_{L}^{\neg b}-\rho_{L}^{b})\otimes\rho_{R}^{b}\bigg)\ket{\psi_b}|\leq\min\left\{\frac{4\varepsilon}{\delta_{L}},\delta_{L}\right\}\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}

(126)

Analogously, we obtain for the RHS:

\forall b:|\bra{\psi_b}\mathsf{Dec}\bigg(\rho_{L}^{b}\otimes(\rho_{R}^{b}-\rho_{R}^{\neg b})\bigg)\ket{\psi_b}|\leq\min\left\{\frac{4\varepsilon}{\delta_{R}},\delta_{R}\right\}\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}

(127)

Put together with
9.6
, and again applying
9.5
:

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon\geq\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\cdot\bra{\psi_b}\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{\neg b}_{R}-\rho^{\neg b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{b}_{R})\ket{\psi_b}\geq\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}-\delta_{L}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/2}

(128)

Which implies
\delta_{L}=\max\{\delta_{L},\delta_{R}\}\leq 16\varepsilon
assuming
\varepsilon\leq 1/16
.

∎

9.2.1
Proofs of deferred claims

Proof.

[of
9.5
] Since
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
is
\varepsilon
-non-malleable, there exists input-independent
p=p_{same},\sigma
such that

\displaystyle\max_{b}\|\rho_{out}^{b}-(p\psi_{b}+(1-p)\sigma)\|_{1}\leq\varepsilon

(129)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bra{\psi_{b}}\rho_{out}^{\neg b}-\rho_{out}^{b}\ket{\psi_b}\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon+p\cdot\bra{\psi_{b}}\psi_{\neg b}-\psi_{b}\ket{\psi_b}=2\varepsilon-p\cdot\big(1-\mathsf{F}^{2}(\psi_{b},\psi_{\neg b})\big)\leq 2\varepsilon

(130)

∎

Proof.

[of
9.6
] Let
\mathbb{P}[m_{L}|b]
denote the distribution over left measurement outcomes
m_{L}
, where
\mathbb{P}[m_{L}=0|b=0]=\mathbb{P}[m_{L}=1|b=1]=\mathbb{P}[m_{L}=b|b]=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}
. One can then re-express the state
\rho_{out}^{b}
output by the receiver as

\displaystyle\rho_{out}^{b}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}[b=m_{L}|b]\cdot\bigg(\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{\neg b})+\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{\neg b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{b}_{R})\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}[b\neq m_{L}|b]\cdot\bigg(\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{b})+\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{\neg b}_{R})\bigg)

(131)

\displaystyle=\big(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\delta_{L}}{8}\big)\cdot\mathsf{Dec}\bigg(\rho^{\neg b}+\rho^{b}+\rho^{\neg b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{b}_{R}+\rho^{b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{\neg b}_{R}\bigg)+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\cdot\mathsf{Dec}\big(\rho^{\neg b}+\rho^{\neg b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{b}_{R}\big)

(132)

Note that the first term in the line above is independent of
b
, and thereby we denote their sum as the fixed density matrix
(1-\frac{\delta_{L}}{2})\cdot\sigma
of trace
1-\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}
. Also,
\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{\neg b})=\psi_{\neg b}
. Thus,

\rho_{out}^{b}=(1-\frac{\delta_{L}}{2})\cdot\sigma+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\cdot\psi^{\neg b}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\cdot\mathsf{Dec}(\rho^{\neg b}_{L}\otimes\rho^{b}_{R})

(133)

which gives the claim.
∎

Proof.

[of
9.7
] The decoded state is given by

\displaystyle\rho^{b,r,l}=\bigg(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\bigg)\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{L}^{b\oplus l}\otimes\rho_{R}^{r})+\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\bigg)\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{L}^{b\oplus l\oplus 1}\otimes\rho_{R}^{r})=

(134)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\cdot\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{L}^{b\oplus l}\otimes\rho_{R}^{r})+\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\delta_{L}}{4}\bigg)\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{L}^{\neg b}\otimes\rho_{R}^{r}+\rho_{L}^{b}\otimes\rho_{R}^{r})=

(135)

\displaystyle=\bigg(1-\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\bigg)\cdot\sigma_{r}+\frac{\delta_{L}}{2}\cdot\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{L}^{b\oplus l}\otimes\rho_{R}^{r})

(136)

for some fixed density matrix
\sigma_{r}
which doesn’t depend on
b
.
∎

10
On the Capacity of Separable Non-Malleable Codes

In this section, we prove an upper bound for the rate of split-state non-malleable codes where the code-states are separable states. Recall that by separable, we mean the code-states are unentangled across one or more of the shares (see
Definition

2.6
). Although imposing separability may seem like a strong constraint on the family of codes we consider, we emphasize that prior to this work the only known constructions of non-malleable codes were separable
[
BGJR23
,
BBJ23
]
.

Our main result in this section is that quantum non-malleable codes which have separable state encodings, inherit their capacity from that of classical non-malleable codes. Our proof revisits and simplifies a result of Cheraghchi and Guruswami
[
CG13a
]
, who proved a similar bound for the classical
t
-split-state model.

Theorem 10.1

(
Theorem

1.6
, restatement)
.

Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a quantum non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
and blocklength
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and assume that
\mathsf{Enc}(\psi)
is a separable state (across each share) for every message
\psi
. If the rate of
\mathsf{Enc}
exceeds
1-\frac{1}{t}+\delta
for some
\delta\geq 4\cdot\log n/n
, then the error must exceed
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}=\Omega(\delta^{2})
.

At a high level,
[
CG13a
]
’s proof leverages information-theoretic techniques to argue that if the rate of the code is too high (exceeding
1-\alpha
), then any large enough share (of size
\geq\alpha\cdot n
) must correlate with the message. They show how an adversary holding said share, could then leverage the correlations with the message to tamper with their share and break non-malleability.

In contrast, we appeal directly to the distinguishability of the message.
[
CG13a
]
’s arguments are used to show that if the rate of the code is too high, then the code is not single-share encrypting. That is, an adversary holding said share could guess the message by themself. However, by appealing to our results in
Section

9
, we know that quantum codes in the split-state model must encrypt their shares, leading to a contradiction.

10.1
Preliminaries

We dedicate this subsection to a background on quantum entropies, as well as the necessary statements from prior sections on breaking non-malleability and tamper detection. Refer to
[
Wil11
]
for a review.

10.1.1
Quantum Entropies

We use the following standard notions of quantum entropy, conditional quantum entropy, mutual information, and conditional quantum mutual information.

Definition 10.1

(von Neumann Entropy)
.

The von Neumann entropy of a quantum state
\rho_{A}
is defined as,

{\mathsf{S}}(A)_{\rho}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}-\Tr(\rho_A\log\rho_A).

Definition 10.2

(Conditional Quantum Entropy)
.

The conditional quantum entropy of a state
\rho_{AB}
is defined as,

{\mathsf{S}}(A|B)_{\rho}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathsf{S}}(AB)_{\rho}-{\mathsf{S}}(B)_{\rho},\quad\textnormal{and}\quad-|A|\leq{\mathsf{S}}(A|B)_{\rho}\leq|A|,

where we recall
|A|=\log\dim(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.

Definition 10.3

(Mutual Information)
.

Let
\rho_{AB}
be a quantum state. We define the mutual information as follows.

\mathsf{I}(A:B)_{\rho}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathsf{S}}(A)_{\rho}+{\mathsf{S}}(B)_{\rho}-{\mathsf{S}}({AB})_{\rho}={\mathsf{S}}(A)_{\rho}-{\mathsf{S}}(A|B)_{\rho}.

Furthermore,
0\leq\mathsf{I}(A:B)_{\rho}\leq 2\min\{|A|,|B|\}
.

Definition 10.4

(Conditional Mutual Information)
.

Let
\rho_{ABC}
be a quantum state. We define the conditional mutual information as follows.

\mathsf{I}(A:B|C)_{\rho}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{I}(A:BC)_{\rho}-\mathsf{I}(A:C)_{\rho}={\mathsf{S}}(A|C)_{\rho}-{\mathsf{S}}(A|BC)_{\rho}.

Furthermore,
0\leq\mathsf{I}(A:B|C)_{\rho}\leq 2\min\{|A|,|B|\}
.

The following three facts correspond to important properties of the conditional quantum entropy. In particular, concavity, non-decrease under local operations, and non-negativity when one of the registers is classical:

Fact 10.1

(Concavity of the Conditional Quantum Entropy)
.

Let
\rho_{AB}
be a state such that

\rho_{AB}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\sigma^{i}_{AB},\quad\forall i\quad p_{i}\geq 0,\quad\sum_{i}p_{i}=1.

Then,

{\mathsf{S}}(A|B)_{\rho}\geq\sum_{i}p_{i}{\mathsf{S}}(A|B)_{\sigma^{i}}.

Fact 10.2

(Non-negativity of the Conditional Quantum Entropy of Separable States)
.

Let
\rho_{XB}
be a separable mixed state, i.e.,
\rho_{XB}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\sigma_{X}^{i}\otimes\sigma_{B}^{i}
. Then,
{\mathsf{S}}(X|B)_{\rho}\geq 0
.

Fact 10.3

(Data-Processing)
.

Let
\rho_{AB}
be a state and
\mathcal{E}:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{B})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{C})
be a CPTP map acting on register
B
. Let

\sigma_{AC}=(\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathcal{E})(\rho_{AB})
.
Then,
{\mathsf{S}}(A|B)_{\rho}\leq{\mathsf{S}}(A|C)_{\sigma}
.

Finally, we make use of continuity of the von Neumann entropy:

Fact 10.4

(The Fannes Inequality)
.

Let
\rho_{A},\sigma_{A}
be two states such that
\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{A}-\sigma_{A}\|_{1}\leq\delta
. Then,

|{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{A})-{\mathsf{S}}(\rho_{A})|\leq\delta\cdot|A|+\frac{1}{e\ln 2}.

(137)

10.1.2
Tamper-resilient Codes Encrypt their Shares

At a high level, our proof proceeds by arguing that if the rate of the non-malleable code is too high, then one of the split-state adversaries (holding only one of the shares) would locally be able to distinguish the message. In some cases (see
Section

9
)
21
21

21

But not all - recall the 3 split-state counter-example for classical codes.
, this can be shown to break the non-malleability guarantee. In this subsection we state two useful claims on breaking non-malleability by violating single-share encryption.

Aggarwal et. al
[
ADKO15
]
proved that non-malleable codes in the split-state model are 2-out-of-2 secret sharing. That is, the marginal distribution on either right or left shares is nearly independent of the message.

Lemma 10.2

(
[
ADKO15
]
)
.

Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a non-malleable code in the 2-split state model with error
\varepsilon<1/2
. For any pair of messages
m_{0},m_{1}
, let
(X_{i},Y_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(m_{i})
and let
p_{X_{i}}
denote the marginal distribution over the right share
X_{i}
. Then,

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\|p_{X_{0}}-p_{X_{1}}\|_{1}\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon

(138)

In
Section

9
, we showed that a similar behavior was present in tamper-detection codes, regardless of the number
t
of splits, following a connection between quantum authentication codes and quantum encryption scheme. In
Corollary

9.2
(restated below), we extended a limited version of this connection to non-malleable codes.

Claim 10.3

(
Corollary

9.2
, restatement)
.

Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a non-malleable code in the
t
-split state model with error
\varepsilon<1/2
, and let
(\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda},\mathsf{Dec}_{\lambda})
denote the non-malleable code in the
t
-split state model which pads the message with
\lambda
random bits and encodes it into
\mathsf{Enc}
. Then, for any pair of messages
m_{0},m_{1}
and share
j\in[t]
:

\frac{1}{2}\|\Tr_{\neg j}\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda}(m_{0})-\Tr_{\neg j}\mathsf{Enc}_{\lambda}(m_{1})\|_{1}\leq 2(\varepsilon^{1/2}+2^{1-\lambda/2}).

(139)

10.2
Proof of
Theorem

10.1

Let
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
be a quantum non-malleable code with error
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
and rate
r=\frac{k}{n}
. Consider the cq state
\rho_{\hat{X}Q}
defined by encoding half of a maximally correlated mixed state
\sigma_{\hat{X}X}
into the code:

\sigma_{\hat{X}X}=2^{-k}\sum_{x}\outerproduct{x}{x}_{\hat{X}}\otimes\outerproduct{x}{x}_{X}\rightarrow\rho_{\hat{X}Q}=2^{-k}\sum_{x}\outerproduct{x}{x}_{\hat{X}}\otimes\mathsf{Enc}_{X\rightarrow Q}(\outerproduct{x}{x})

(140)

We begin by observing that
Q
has full information about
\hat{X}
.

Claim 10.4
.

The conditional entropy
{\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}|Q)_{\rho}={\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}Q)_{\rho}-{\mathsf{S}}(Q)_{\rho}=0
.

Proof.

Note that
{\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}|X)=0
. By the data-processing inequality in
10.3
,
{\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}|X)_{\sigma}\leq{\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}|Q)_{\rho}
. Moreover, if the non-malleable code is perfectly correct, we must have

\sigma_{\hat{X}X}=[\mathbb{I}_{\hat{X}}\otimes(\mathsf{Dec}\circ\mathsf{Enc})_{X}](\sigma_{\hat{X}X}).

(141)

Once again by the data-processing inequality,
{\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}|Q)\leq{\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}|X)
.
∎

Based on these properties we prove a separable state analog of a statement by
[
CG13a
]
, that there must exist at least one message whose left share has significantly less entropy than that of the encoding of a random message.

Claim 10.5
.

Let the first share have size
|Q_{1}|=\alpha\cdot n
, and assume that
\rho_{\hat{X},Q_{1},Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}}
is separable across the
\hat{X}Q_{1}:Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}
cut. Then,

\mathsf{I}(\hat{X}:Q_{1})\geq k-(1-\alpha)\cdot n.

(142)

Proof.

[of
10.5
] From the chain rule of the mutual information,
\mathsf{I}(Q_{1}:\hat{X})_{\rho}=\mathsf{I}(Q:\hat{X})_{\rho}-\mathsf{I}(Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}:\hat{X}|Q_{1})_{\rho}
. From
10.4
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}(Q:\hat{X})_{\rho}={\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X})-{\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X}|Q)={\mathsf{S}}(\hat{X})=k

(143)

If we assume
\rho
is separable across the
Q_{1},Q\setminus Q_{1}
cut, then the conditional entropy is non-negative
{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}|Q_{1},\hat{X})\geq 0
from
10.2
. Then, by subadditivity,

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}(Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}:\hat{X}|Q_{1})_{\rho}={\mathsf{S}}(Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}|Q_{1})-{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}|Q_{1},\hat{X})\leq

(144)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}|Q_{1})\leq\sum_{i\neq 1}|Q_{i}|=(1-\alpha)\cdot n

(145)

∎

We can now conclude the proof of the theorem.

Proof.

[of
Theorem

10.1
] By an averaging argument, at least one of the
t
shares (WLOG the first one) has size
|Q_{1}|=\alpha\cdot n\geq\frac{1}{t}\cdot n
. We begin by leveraging
Corollary

9.2
. By our compiler, any quantum non-malleable code
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
of rate
r>\delta
and error
\varepsilon
can be converted into another quantum non-malleable code
(\mathsf{Enc}^{\prime},\mathsf{Dec}^{\prime})
of rate
r^{\prime}=r-\delta
, which is single-share encrypting. That is, if
\rho_{Q_{1}}^{x}=\Tr_{Q_{2}\cdots Q_{t}}\mathsf{Enc}_{Q}^{\prime}(\outerproduct{x}{x})
denotes the reduced density matrix on
Q_{1}
for a fixed message
x
, then

\forall x,y:\|\rho_{Q_{1}}^{x}-\rho_{Q_{1}}^{y}\|_{1}\leq 4(\varepsilon^{1/2}+2^{1-\delta\cdot n/2}).

(146)

We now prove that since
(\mathsf{Enc}^{\prime},\mathsf{Dec}^{\prime})
is single-share encrypting, we must have an upper bound on its rate
r^{\prime}
. Indeed, assume for the purposes of contradiction that the rate exceeds
r^{\prime}\geq(1-t^{-1})+\delta
. From
10.5
, we must then have
\mathsf{I}(\hat{X}:Q_{1})\geq\delta\cdot n
. From the concavity of conditional entropy
10.1
, there exists an
x\in\{0,1\}^{k}
such that

{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{1}|\hat{X}=x)_{\rho}\leq{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{1}|\hat{X})_{\rho}={\mathsf{S}}(Q_{1})_{\rho}-\mathsf{I}(Q_{1}:\hat{X})_{\rho}\leq{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{1})_{\rho}-\delta\cdot n.

(147)

However, by the the Fannes inequality
10.4
, the entropy difference gives us a lower bound on the TV distance between the marginals:

{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{1})_{\rho}-{\mathsf{S}}(Q_{1})_{\mathsf{Enc}_{Q}(\outerproduct{x}{x})}\leq\|\rho_{Q_{1}}-\rho_{Q_{1}}^{x}\|_{1}\cdot\alpha\cdot n+\frac{2}{3}.

(148)

and by an averaging argument, this tells us there must exist two strings
x,y
whose marginals are statistically close:

\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{Q_{1}}^{x}-\rho_{Q_{1}}^{y}\|_{1}\geq\frac{\delta}{6\alpha}\text{ (Assuming }\delta\geq 1/n)

(149)

However, if we assume
\delta\geq 2\log n/n
, this will contradict the assumption that
\mathsf{Enc}^{\prime}
is single-share encrypting, unless the error
\varepsilon
satisfies

\frac{\delta}{6\alpha}\leq 4(\varepsilon^{1/2}+2^{1-\delta\cdot n/2})\leq 4(\varepsilon^{1/2}+\frac{2}{n})\Rightarrow\varepsilon=\Omega(\delta^{2}).

(150)

In this manner, we conclude that any quantum NMC of rate
r\geq 1-t^{-1}+\gamma
for some
\gamma\geq 4\log n/n
must have error
\Omega(\gamma^{2})
.
∎

Appendix A
Background on Pauli and Clifford Operators

Here we review Pauli operators and the associated Pauli and Clifford groups.

Definition A.1

(Pauli Operators)
.

The single-qubit
Pauli operators
are given by

I=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad X=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\quad Y=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}\quad Z=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

An
n
-qubit Pauli operator is given by the
n
-fold tensor product of single-qubit Pauli operators.
We denote the set of all
|A|
-qubit Pauli operators on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
by
{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
, where
|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|=4^{|A|}
. Any linear operator
L\in{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
can be written as a linear combination of
|A|
-qubit Pauli operators with complex coefficients as
L=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}\alpha_{P}P
. This is called the
Pauli decomposition
of a linear operator.

We remark that for
a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
, we refer to the
n
-qubit Pauli operator
X^{a}=\otimes_{i\in[n]}X^{a_{i}}
(respectively
Z^{a}
).

Definition A.2

(Pauli Group)
.

The single-qubit
Pauli group
is given by

\{+P,-P,\ iP,\ -iP:P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}\}.

The Pauli group on
|A|
-qubits is the group generated by the operators described above applied to each of
|A|
-qubits in the tensor product. We denote the
|A|
-qubit Pauli group on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
by
\tilde{{\mathsf{P}}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.

Definition A.3

(Clifford Group)
.

The
Clifford group

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
is defined as the group of unitaries that normalize the Pauli group
\tilde{{\mathsf{P}}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
, i.e.,

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}_{A})=\{V\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}_{A}):V\tilde{{\mathsf{P}}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})V^{\dagger}=\tilde{{\mathsf{P}}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\}.

The
Clifford unitaries
are the elements of the Clifford group.

We will also need to work with subgroups of the Clifford group with certain special properties.
The following fact describes these properties and guarantees the existence of such subgroups.

Fact A.1

(Restatement of
2.6

[
CLLW16
]
)
.

There exists a subgroup
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
of the Clifford group
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
such that given any non-identity Pauli operators
P,Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
we have that

|\{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|C^{\dagger}PC=Q\}|=\frac{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|=2^{5|A|}-2^{3|A|}.

Informally, applying a random Clifford operator from
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
