(by conjugation) maps
P
to a Pauli operator chosen uniformly at random over all non-identity Pauli operators.
Furthermore, we have that
{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\subset\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.

Additionally, there exists a procedure
\mathsf{Samp}
which given as input a uniformly random string
R\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{5|A|}
outputs in time
{\mathsf{poly}}(|A|)
a Clifford operator
C_{R}\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
such that

C_{R}\approx_{2^{-2|A|}}U_{\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})},

(151)

where
U_{\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}
denotes the uniform distribution over
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.

Twirling and related facts.

The analysis of our construction will require the use of several facts related to Pauli and Clifford twirling. We collect them below, beginning with the usual version of the Pauli twirl.

Fact A.2

(Pauli
1
-Design)
.

Let
\rho_{AB}
be a state. Then,

\frac{1}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(Q\otimes\mathbb{I})\rho_{AB}(Q^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{I})=U_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}.

Fact A.3

(Pauli Twirl
[
DCEL09
]
)
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be a state and
P,P^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be Pauli operators such that
P\neq P^{\prime}
. Then,

\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}Q^{\dagger}PQ\rho Q^{\dagger}P^{\prime\dagger}Q=0.

Fact A.4

(
1
-Design)
.

Let
\rho_{AB}
be a state. Let
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be the subgroup of Clifford group as defined in
A.1
. Then,

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C\otimes\mathbb{I})\rho_{AB}(C^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{I})=U_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}.

Fact A.5

(Clifford Subgroup Twirl, Lemma 1 in
[
BBJ23
]
)
.

Let state
\rho_{AB}
be a state. Let
P,Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be any two Pauli operators. Let
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be the sub-group of Clifford group as defined in
A.1
.

1.

If
P\neq Q
, then

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(\mathbb{I}\otimes C^{\dagger}PC)\rho_{BA}(\mathbb{I}\otimes C^{\dagger}Q^{\dagger}C)=0.

(152)

2.

If
P=Q\neq\mathbb{I}_{A}
, then

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(\mathbb{I}\otimes C^{\dagger}PC)\rho_{BA}(\mathbb{I}\otimes C^{\dagger}Q^{\dagger}C)=\frac{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|(\rho_{B}\otimes U_{A})-\rho_{BA}}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}.

(153)

Lemma A.1

(Restatement of
Lemma

2.2
)
.

Consider
Figure

. Let
\psi_{R}=U_{R}
be a state independent of
{\psi}_{A\hat{A}E}
and
|R|=5|A|
. Let
\Lambda:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E})
be any CPTP map. Let
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be the sub-group of Clifford group as defined in
A.1
. Let

\rho_{\hat{A}AE}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}\Lambda(C(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})C^{\dagger})C).

Then,

\rho_{\hat{A}AE}\approx_{\frac{2}{2^{2|A|}-1}}\Phi_{1}(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})+(\Phi_{2}(\psi_{\hat{A}E})\otimes U_{A}),

where
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{E})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{E})
are CP (completely positive) maps acting only on register
E
, depending only on
\Lambda
, and
\Phi_{1}(.)+\Phi_{2}(.)
is a CPTP map.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda:{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E})\to{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E})
be the CPTP map. Let
\{M_{i}\}_{i}
be the set of Kraus operators corresponding to
\Lambda
, and its Pauli basis decomposition
M_{i}=\sum_{j}\alpha_{ij}P^{ij}
for
P^{ij}\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{AE})
. We denote
P^{ij}_{A}
to use Pauli operator corresponding to register
A
of
P^{ij}
.
Let
M^{Q}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{j:P^{ij}_{A}=Q}\alpha_{ij}P^{ij}_{E}
for every
Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
. Note that since
\Lambda
is CPTP, we have
\sum_{i}M_{i}^{\dagger}M_{i}=\mathbb{I}_{AE}
.

We begin by showing that if we restrict the
M_{i}
to their operation on the register
E
, they still form a CPTP map. For this we need to show
\sum_{i,Q}(M^{Q}_{i})^{\dagger}M^{Q}_{i}=\mathbb{I}_{E}
. Consider,

\displaystyle 2^{|A|}\mathbb{I}_{E}=\Tr_{A}(\mathbb{I}_{AE})

\displaystyle=\Tr_{A}\left(\sum_{i}M_{i}^{\dagger}M_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr_{A}\left(\sum_{i}\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}P^{ij^{\prime}}\right)\left(\sum_{j}\alpha_{ij}P^{ij}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\Tr_{A}\left(\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}}\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}(P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}P^{ij}_{A})\otimes(P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}P^{ij}_{E})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}}\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\Tr(\id_A)\otimes P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}P^{ij}_{E}+\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}\neq P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}}\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\Tr( P^{ij'}_A P^{ij}_A )\otimes P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}P^{ij}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{|A|}\left(\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}}\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}P^{ij}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{|A|}\sum_{i,Q}\left(\sum_{j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}=Q}\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}P^{ij}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{|A|}\sum_{i,Q}\left(\sum_{j^{\prime}:P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}=Q}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)\left(\sum_{j:P^{ij}_{A}=Q}\alpha_{ij}P^{ij}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{|A|}\sum_{i,Q}(M^{Q}_{i})^{\dagger}(M^{Q}_{i})

We can now turn our attention to
\rho_{\hat{A}AE}
. Consider,

\displaystyle\rho_{\hat{A}AE}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}C^{\dagger}\Lambda(C\psi_{\hat{A}AE}C^{\dagger})C

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}M_{i}C)(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})(C^{\dagger}M_{i}^{\dagger}C)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}P^{ij}C)(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})(C^{\dagger}P^{ij^{\prime}}C)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}}\left(\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}P^{ij}_{E}\left(\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}P^{ij}_{A}C)(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})(C^{\dagger}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}C)\right)P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}\neq P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}}\left(\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}P^{ij}_{E}\left(\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}P^{ij}_{A}C)(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})(C^{\dagger}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}C)\right)P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}}\left(\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}P^{ij}_{E}\left(\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}P^{ij}_{A}C)(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})(C^{\dagger}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}C)\right)P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)

(
A.5
.1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}=\mathbb{I}_{A}}\left(\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}P^{ij}_{E}\left(\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}P^{ij}_{A}C)(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})(C^{\dagger}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}C)\right)P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}\neq\mathbb{I}_{A}}\left(\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}P^{ij}_{E}\left(\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{\dagger}P^{ij}_{A}C)(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})(C^{\dagger}P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}C)\right)P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}=\mathbb{I}_{A}}\left(\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}P^{ij}_{E}(\psi_{\hat{A}AE})P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j,j^{\prime}:P^{ij}_{A}=P^{ij^{\prime}}_{A}\neq\mathbb{I}_{A}}\left(\alpha_{ij}\alpha^{*}_{ij^{\prime}}P^{ij}_{E}\left(\frac{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|(\psi_{\hat{A}E}\otimes U_{A})-\psi_{\hat{A}EA}}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}\right)P^{ij^{\prime}}_{E}\right)

(
A.5
.2)

\displaystyle\approx_{\frac{2}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}}\sum_{i}M^{\mathbb{I}_{A}}_{i}\psi_{A\hat{A}E}(M^{\mathbb{I}_{A}}_{i})^{\dagger}+\sum_{i}\sum_{Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\land Q\neq\mathbb{I}_{A}}M^{Q}_{i}(U_{A}\otimes\psi_{\hat{A}E})(M^{Q}_{i})^{\dagger}.

The approximation in the result follows from the fact that

\left\|\frac{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|(U_{A}\otimes\psi_{\hat{A}E})-\psi_{A\hat{A}E}}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}-(U_{A}\otimes\psi_{\hat{A}E})\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{2}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}.

and the monotonicity of trace distance
2.1
, since the operators
\{M_{i}^{Q}\}_{i,Q}
define a CPTP set of Krauss operators. Finally, we let
\{M^{\mathbb{I}}_{i}\}_{i,\mathbb{I}}
define the CP map
\Phi_{1}
and
\{M^{Q}_{i}\}_{i,Q\neq\mathbb{I}}
define
\Phi_{2}
.

∎

Appendix B
Non-Malleable Codes against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}

In a recent work,
[
BGJR23
]
introduced the notion of a non-malleable code for quantum messages, and presented constructions in the split-state model with entangled adversaries
\mathsf{LO}^{2}_{*}
. Their constructions were based on quantum secure classical non-malleable codes and families of unitary 2-designs, and had inverse-polynomial rate. In this section, we revisit
[
BGJR23
]
’s construction of 2-split-state quantum non-malleable codes under the assumption that the adversaries are unentangled, that is, against
\mathsf{LO}
channels. We show that in this model, simply an
augmented
classical non-malleable code (instead of quantum secure) suffices to achieve security with much better parameters. Our main result here is a 2-split non-malleable code for quantum messages with
constant
rate:

Theorem B.1
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a quantum non-malleable code with efficient encoding and decoding against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}
, with message length
k
, block length
2n
, rate
k/(2n)=\Omega(1)
and error
2^{-\Omega(k)}
.

We leverage this
2
-split construction to instantiate our tamper-detection reduction in
Theorem

3.1
.
To prove
Theorem

B.1
, we first show the average-case non-malleability against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}
which is stated in the following
Theorem

B.2
. We next make use of average-case to worst-case reduction as stated in
Theorem

B.3
to conclude the desired main result of this section, i.e.
Theorem

B.1
.

Theorem B.2
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an average-case quantum non-malleable code with efficient encoding and decoding against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}
, with message length
k
, block length
2n
, rate
k/(2n)=\Omega(1)
and error
2^{-\Omega(k)}
.

Theorem B.3

(Worst-case to average-case reduction,
[
BGJR23
, Lemma
8
]
)
.

Let
t\geq 2.
Any
average-case quantum non-malleable code
against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
for messages of length
b
and error
\varepsilon
is also a
worst-case quantum non-malleable code
against
\mathsf{LO}^{t}
for messages of length
b
with error
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq 2^{b}\cdot\varepsilon
.

We next proceed to provide the construction of average-case quantum non-malleable code as stated in
Theorem

B.2
and analyze the construction. Before that we refer the reader to
Appendix

A
to background on Clifford operators, and dedicate next subsection to state basic fact on the transpose method.

B.0.1
The transpose method

The transpose method (see, e.g.,
[
Ozo16
]
) is one of the most important
tools for manipulating maximally entangled states. Roughly speaking, the transpose method corresponds to the statement that some local action on one half of the maximally entangled state is equivalent to performing the transpose of the same action on the other half of that state. We now state this formally.

Fact B.1

(Transpose method)
.

Let
\rho_{A\hat{A}}
be the canonical purification of
\rho_{A}=U_{A}
. For any
M\in{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
it holds that

(M\otimes\mathbb{I}_{\hat{A}})\rho_{A\hat{A}}(M^{\dagger}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{\hat{A}})=(\mathbb{I}_{A}\otimes M^{T})\rho_{A\hat{A}}(\mathbb{I}_{A}\otimes(M^{T})^{\dagger}).

B.1
Code Construction

Our goal is to provide a construction and prove the average-case non-malleability of quantum non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}
. However, to achieve constant rate, we need a really careful choice of components: we require a
2
-split classical non-malleable code of constant rate and inverse exponential error, and a family of
2
designs with key-size linear in the number of qubits.

The design of 2-split-state NMCs with constant rate and inverse exponential error has been an outstanding open problem for many years, however, recently
[
Li23b
]
presented remarkable constructions of non-malleable extractors and codes with near-optimal parameters.

Theorem B.4

(
[
Li23b
]
)
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an augmented classical non-malleable code with efficient encoding and decoding against
2
-split-state tampering, which has message length
k
, block length
2n
, rate
k/(2n)=\Omega(1)
and error
2^{-\Omega(k)}
.

B.1.1
Ingredients and Overview

We combine:

1.

An augmented classical non-malleable code in the 2-split-state model,
(\mathsf{Enc}^{\mathsf{NM}},\mathsf{Dec}^{\mathsf{NM}})
from
Theorem

B.4
.

2.

A family of unitary 2-designs, and in particular the subgroup
\mathcal{SC}
of the Clifford group guaranteed in
A.1
.

Consider the following quantum non-malleable code defined by encrypting the quantum message into a randomly chosen Clifford in the subgroup, and non-malleably protecting the randomness into
\mathsf{Enc}^{\mathsf{NM}}
:

\mathsf{Enc}(\sigma_{M})=\mathbb{E}_{r}\left(\mathsf{Enc}^{\mathsf{NM}}(r)_{YX}\otimes(C_{r}\sigma_{M}C_{r}^{\dagger})_{Z}\right).

(154)

Registers
(Y,XZ)
form
2
-split state codewords. After
unentangled
adversarial tampering
(U,V)
, the receiver first measures the classical registers obtaining
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
, decodes them obtaining a key
R^{\prime}=\mathsf{Dec}^{\mathsf{NM}}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
, and attempts to decrypt the quantum message using
C_{R^{\prime}}
. We refer the reader to
Figure

13
for the quantum non-malleable code along with the tampering process. We remark that if the classical non-malleable code has perfect correctness, then the correctness of the quantum code is trivial. It remains to show security.

We prove that the construction above defines an
average-case
quantum non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}
, where the message
\sigma_{M}=U_{M}
is a maximally mixed state.

Theorem B.5
.

If
(\mathsf{Enc}^{\mathsf{NM}},\mathsf{Dec}^{\mathsf{NM}})
is an
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
-secure augmented 2-split-state classical non-malleable code, and
\mathcal{SC}
is the subgroup of the Clifford group guaranteed by
A.1
, then
(\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
above is an average-case
\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{2-|M|}
-secure quantum non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}
(or simply unentangled adversaries).

B.1.2
Analysis

At a high level, the outline of our proof of security follows that of
[
BGJR23
]
. To prove this theorem, we begin with
B.6
,
B.7
and the “transpose method” (see
B.1
) to roughly show that the classical code remains secure in the presence of unentangled adversaries. We defer the proof of Claims to the next subsection, and use them to prove the theorem.

Our goal is to show that
\eta_{M^{\prime}\hat{M}}\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+2^{2-|M|}}p_{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}})\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}}\otimes U_{\hat{M}}
, where
p_{\mathcal{A}},\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}}
depend only on
\mathcal{A}
. We first note that the final states in both the
Figure

13
and
Figure

14
are same.

We now consider the split-state tampering experiment after applying the transpose method and delaying the application of the corresponding Clifford operator (see
Figure

14
). After the adversarial tampering, the receiver measures the classical registers, and the state collapses into a mixture where the quantum message only depends on the right share
(x,x^{\prime})
. Let
\theta_{2}
be the resulting state:

Claim B.6
.

\theta_{2}=\sum_{x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}_{2}(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\cdot\outerproduct{x', y'}{x', y'}\otimes(\theta_{2}^{x,x^{\prime}})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}

(155)

where
\mathbb{P}_{2}(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\mathbb{P}(x,y)\cdot\mathbb{P}(x^{\prime}|x)\cdot\mathbb{P}(y^{\prime}|y)
is the joint distribution over split-state shares before and after tampering, and for each
x,x^{\prime}
,
(\theta_{2}^{x,x^{\prime}})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}
is some bipartite density matrix (independent of
y,y^{\prime}
).

After the receiver attempts to decode the classical non-malleable code, they produce a mixture over original keys
r
and received keys
r^{\prime}
, together with a quantum state dependent only on the right share
x,x^{\prime}
:

Claim B.7
.

\theta_{3}=\sum_{r,r^{\prime},x,x^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}_{3}(r,r^{\prime},x,x^{\prime})\cdot\outerproduct{r}{r}\otimes\outerproduct{r'}{r'}\otimes(\theta_{2}^{x,x^{\prime}})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}

(156)

Moreover,
\mathbb{P}_{3}
is
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}
close to a convex combination
q
, satisfying

q(r,r^{\prime},x,x^{\prime})=\mathbb{P}(r)\cdot\big[p_{\mathsf{same}}\cdot\delta_{r=r^{\prime}}\cdot q^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\cdot q^{\prime\prime}(r^{\prime},x,x^{\prime})\big]

(157)

for some
p_{\mathsf{same}}\in[0,1]
, and distributions
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}
which depend only on adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

Since
\mathbb{P}(r)
is uniform to begin with, this implies

\theta_{3}\approx_{\varepsilon_{NM}}p_{\mathsf{same}}\cdot U_{R}U_{R^{\prime}=R}\otimes(\theta^{1}_{3})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}+(1-p_{\mathsf{same}})\cdot U_{R}\otimes(\theta^{2}_{3})_{R^{\prime}Z^{\prime}\hat{M}},

(158)

where
(\theta^{1}_{3})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}=\sum_{(x,x^{\prime})}q^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime})(\theta_{2}^{x,x^{\prime}})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}
and
(\theta^{2}_{3})_{R^{\prime}Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}=\sum_{(r^{\prime},x,x^{\prime})}q^{\prime\prime}(r^{\prime},x,x^{\prime})(\outerproduct{r'}{r'}\otimes(\theta_{2}^{x,x^{\prime}})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}})
. Intuitively, this shows that either
R=R^{\prime}
and the quantum component is uncorrelated from
R
, or,
R
is uniform and uncorrelated from
R^{\prime}
and the quantum state. Together with the additional proof techniques from
[
BGJR23
]
, we complete the proof as stated below.

Proof.

[of
Theorem

B.5
]

Let
D_{\hat{R}\hat{M}}
on registers
(\hat{R},\hat{M})
be the application of the controlled Clifford gates as shown in
Figure

14
(Similarly
D_{{R^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}}
on registers
(R^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
). We consider
D_{\hat{R}\hat{M}}
on registers
(\hat{R},\hat{M})
and
D_{{R^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}}
on registers
R^{\prime},Z^{\prime}
, on the approximate state to
\theta_{3}
. First, if the key is received, we expect to recover the quantum message if there is no tampering on it. From
A.5
(the Clifford Twirl),
2.6
(approximate sampling from
\mathcal{SC}
) and using techniques from
[
BGJR23
]
, there exists
p_{\mathsf{EPR}}\in[0,1]
and
\delta\leq 2\cdot 2^{-|M|}
, such that the reduced density matrix on
M^{\prime},\hat{M}
satisfies:

\Tr_{R^{\prime},\hat{R}}\bigg[\bigg(D_{\hat{R}\hat{M}}\otimes D_{R^{\prime}{Z^{\prime}}}\bigg)\circ\bigg(U_{R}U_{R^{\prime}=R}\otimes(\theta^{1}_{3})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}\bigg)\bigg]\approx_{\delta}p_{\mathsf{EPR}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}+(1-p_{\mathsf{EPR}})U_{{M}}\otimes U_{\hat{M}}.

(159)

If the key is not received, we expect the controlled Clifford to decouple the quantum message. Note that,

\displaystyle\Tr_{R^{\prime},\hat{R}}\bigg[\bigg(D_{\hat{R}\hat{M}}\otimes D_{{R}^{\prime}Z^{\prime}}\bigg)\circ\bigg(U_{R}\otimes(\theta^{2}_{3})_{R^{\prime}Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}\bigg)\bigg]\approx_{\delta^{\prime}}\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}}\otimes U_{\hat{M}}

(160)

by
2.5
(Cliffords are 1-designs) and again
2.6
(approximate sampling from
\mathcal{SC}
), for
\delta^{\prime}\leq 2\cdot 2^{-|M|}
and some choice of
\gamma_{M^{\prime}}
that depends only on adversary. Put together, if
p_{\mathcal{A}}=p_{\mathsf{EPR}}\cdot p_{\mathsf{same}}
, we have from
Equation

158
and data processing:

\eta_{M^{\prime}\hat{M}}\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{NM}}+\delta+\delta^{\prime}}p_{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{M\hat{M}}+(1-p_{\mathcal{A}})\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}}\otimes U_{\hat{M}}

(161)

again for some (unentangled) choice of
\gamma^{\mathcal{A}}_{M^{\prime}}
. This is a convex combination of the original message
\sigma_{M}
and a fixed state, as intended.
∎

B.1.3
Deferred Proofs

B.7
is a consequence of the augmented classical non-malleable code in the 2-split-state model,
(\mathsf{Enc}^{\mathsf{NM}},\mathsf{Dec}^{\mathsf{NM}})
. We now proceed to provide the proof of
B.6
.

Proof.

[of
B.6
] The state before tampering is given by

\theta=\sum_{x,y}\mathbb{P}(x,y)\cdot\outerproduct{x, y}{x, y}\otimes\sigma_{M\hat{M}}

(162)

Now, the state
\theta_{2}
after the tampering,

\theta_{2}=\sum_{x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}(x,y)\cdot\mathbb{P}(y^{\prime}|y)\cdot\mathbb{P}(x^{\prime}|x)\cdot\outerproduct{x', y'}{x', y'}\otimes(\theta_{2}^{x,x^{\prime}})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}

(163)

where
\mathbb{P}(y^{\prime}|y)=\Tr[(\outerproduct{y'}{y'})V\big(\outerproduct{y}{y}\big)]
,
\mathbb{P}(x^{\prime}|x)=\Tr[(\outerproduct{x'}{x'}\otimes\mathbb{I})U\big(\outerproduct{x}{x}\otimes\sigma\big)]
, and the post-measurement state (on registers
Z^{\prime}\hat{M}
) is given by:

(\theta_{2}^{x,x^{\prime}})_{Z^{\prime}\hat{M}}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}(x^{\prime}|x)}\cdot\Tr_{(Z^{\prime}\hat{M})^{C}}\bigg[(\outerproduct{x'}{x'}\otimes\mathbb{I})V\bigg(\outerproduct{x}{x}\otimes\sigma_{M\hat{M}}\bigg)(\outerproduct{x'}{x'}\otimes\mathbb{I})\bigg].

(164)

∎

B.2
[
BGJR23
]
’s 2-Split-State NMCs are Augmented

In opening up
[
BGJR23
]
’s construction, we also make the observation that it is an augmented non-malleable code, under
Definition

2.15
. We dedicate
Section

B.2
to note the necessary changes in the proof of
[
BGJR23
]
to conclude the augmented property.

Claim B.8
.

The
2
-split-state quantum non-malleable code against
\mathsf{LO}^{2}_{*}
by
[
BGJR23
]
is a quantum augmented non-malleable code with inverse polynomial rate and error
\varepsilon\leq 2^{-n^{c}}
for some tiny constant
c>0
, where
n
is the length of the codeword.

For simplicity, we simply state the modifications required to their proof. The key insight lies in the fact that they use an augmented quantum secure non-malleable randomness encoder (NMRE), based on a quantum secure non-malleable extractor. In this manner, the data-processing inequalities present in the proofs remain true in the presence of side entanglement (
W_{2}
, in their proof) held by the adversary. The only major modification that we need is to the
Lemma

B.9
(which is Lemma
8
in
[
BGJR23
]
proof) which is now
Lemma

B.10
to note the augmented property.

Lemma B.9

(Lemma
8
in
[
BGJR23
]
)
.

Let
\ket{\psi}_{\hat{A}A}
be the canonical purification of
\psi_{A}=U_{A}
,
\rho_{\hat{A}A}
be any state, and
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be the subgroup of Clifford group as defined in
A.1
.
Define
\Pi=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
.
Then, we have that

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\rho_{\hat{A}A}((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)\\
=\tr(\Pi\rho)\psi+(1-\tr(\Pi\rho))\frac{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|(U_{\hat{A}}\otimes U_{{A}})-\psi_{A\hat{A}}}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}\\
\approx_{\frac{2}{4^{|A|}}}\tr(\Pi\rho)\psi+(1-\tr(\Pi\rho))(U_{\hat{A}}\otimes U_{A}).

(165)

We need the following modified version of the above lemma.

Lemma B.10
.

Let
\ket{\psi}_{\hat{A}A}
be the canonical purification of
\psi_{A}=U_{A}
,
\rho_{\hat{A}AE}
be any state, and
\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be the subgroup of Clifford group as defined in
A.1
. Define

\Pi=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\quad;\quad\gamma^{0}_{E}=\tr_{\hat{A}A}\left(\frac{\Pi\rho\Pi}{\tr(\Pi\rho)}\right)\quad;\quad\gamma^{1}_{E}=\tr_{\hat{A}A}\left(\frac{\bar{\Pi}\rho\bar{\Pi}}{1-\tr(\Pi\rho)}\right).

Then, we have that

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\rho_{\hat{A}AE}((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)\\
=\tr(\Pi\rho)(\psi\otimes\gamma^{0}_{E})+(1-\tr(\Pi\rho))\left(\left(\frac{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|(U_{\hat{A}}\otimes U_{{A}})-\psi_{A\hat{A}}}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}\right)\otimes\gamma^{1}_{E}\right)\\
\approx_{\frac{2}{4^{|A|}}}\tr(\Pi\rho)\psi+(1-\tr(\Pi\rho))(U_{\hat{A}}\otimes U_{A}\otimes\gamma^{1}_{E}).

(166)

Proof.

The proof proceeds along the lines of the proof of
Lemma

B.9
. We provide the proof here for completeness.

Let
\ket{\phi}_{\hat{A}AE}
be an eigenvector of
\rho_{\hat{A}AE}
. Consider the decomposition

\ket{\phi}_{\hat{A}AE}=\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}\alpha_{P}(\mathbb{I}\otimes P)\ket{\psi}_{\hat{A}A}\ket{\phi^P}_{E},

where
\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}|\alpha_{P}|^{2}=1
.
Define

\tau(P,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}(\mathbb{I}\otimes P)\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}(\mathbb{I}\otimes Q^{\dagger}).

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\outerproduct{\phi}{\phi}((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\left(\sum_{P,Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}\alpha_{P}\alpha^{*}_{Q}\tau(P,Q)\otimes\ket{\phi^P}\bra{\phi^Q}\right)((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P,Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}\alpha_{P}\alpha^{*}_{Q}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\tau(P,Q)((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)\otimes\ket{\phi^P}\bra{\phi^Q}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P,Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\land(P\neq Q)}\alpha_{P}\alpha^{*}_{Q}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\tau(P,Q)((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)\otimes\ket{\phi^P}\bra{\phi^Q}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{P,Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\land(P=Q=\mathbb{I}_{A})}\alpha_{P}\alpha^{*}_{Q}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\tau(P,Q)((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)\otimes\ket{\phi^P}\bra{\phi^Q}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{P,Q\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\land(P=Q\neq\mathbb{I}_{A})}\alpha_{P}\alpha^{*}_{Q}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\tau(P,Q)((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)\otimes\ket{\phi^P}\bra{\phi^Q}\right)

\displaystyle=|\alpha_{\mathbb{I}_{A}}|^{2}\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\otimes\ket{\phi^{\id}}\bra{\phi^{\id}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\setminus\mathbb{I}_{A}}|\alpha_{P}|^{2}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}\sum_{C\in\mathcal{SC}(\mathcal{H}_{A})}(C^{T}\otimes C^{\dagger})\tau(P,P)((C^{T})^{\dagger}\otimes C)\otimes\ket{\phi^{P}}\bra{\phi^{P}}\right)

\displaystyle=|\alpha_{\mathbb{I}_{A}}|^{2}\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\otimes\ket{\phi^{\id}}\bra{\phi^{\id}}+(1-|\alpha_{\mathbb{I}_{A}}|^{2})\left(\frac{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|(U_{A}\otimes U_{\hat{A}})-\psi_{A\hat{A}}}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}\otimes\left(\frac{(\sum_{P\in{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\setminus\mathbb{I}_{A}}|\alpha_{P}|^{2}\ket{\phi^{P}}\bra{\phi^{P}})}{(1-|\alpha_{\mathbb{I}_{A}}|^{2})}\right)\right).

The last equality follows from
A.5
and the transpose method. Now, the first equality in
Equation

166
from the lemma statement follows by observing that
\rho_{\hat{A}AE}
is a convex combination of its eigenvectors, and the approximation in
Equation

166
follows from
2.3
by observing that

\left\|\frac{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|(U_{A}\otimes U_{\hat{A}})-\psi_{A\hat{A}}}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|-1}-(U_{A}\otimes U_{\hat{A}})\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{2}{|{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{H}_{A})|}.

∎

Appendix C
Secret Sharing Schemes Resilient to Joint Quantum Leakage

In this section, we show that simple modifications to a recent construction of leakage resilient secret sharing schemes by
[
CKOS22
]
can be made secure against quantum leakage, even when the leakage adversaries are allowed to jointly leak a quantum state from an unauthorized subset (of size
k
) to another (of size
<t
). We refer to this leakage model as
\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{n,t}
. The main result of this section

Theorem C.1
.

For every
k<t\leq p<l,\mu\in\mathbb{N}
there exists an
(p,t,0,0)
threshold secret sharing scheme on messages of
l
bits and shares of size
l+\mu+o(l,\mu)
bits, which is perfectly private and
p\cdot 2^{-\tilde{\Omega}(\sqrt[3]{\frac{l+\mu}{p}})}
leakage resilient against the
k
local
\mu
qubit leakage family
\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{p,t}
.

We organize the rest of this section as follows. In
Section

C.1
, we present the relevant background on quantum secure extractors, and recall the relevant secret sharing definitions. In
Section

C.2
, we present the code construction, and in
Section

C.3
its proof of security. Finally, we instantiate our construction using specific secret sharing schemes and extractors in
Section

C.3.1
.

C.1
Preliminaries

C.1.1
Leakage Resilient Secret Sharing

We refer the reader to
Section

2.5.2
for a more comprehensive background on secret sharing.

Definition C.1

(Leakage-Resilient Secret Sharing)
.

Let
(\mathsf{Share},\mathsf{Rec})
be a secret sharing scheme with randomized sharing function
\mathsf{Share}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\{\{0,1\}^{l^{\prime}}\}^{p}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a family of leakage channels. Then
\mathsf{Share}
is said to be
(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon_{lr})
leakage-resilient if, for every channel
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
,

\forall m_{0},m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}:\text{ }\Lambda(\mathsf{Share}(m_{0}))\approx_{\varepsilon_{lr}}\Lambda(\mathsf{Share}(m_{1}))

(167)

Definition C.2

(Quantum
k
local leakage model)
.

For any integer sizes
p,t,k
and leakage length (in qubits)
\mu
, we define the
(p,t,k,\mu)
-local leakage model to be the collection of channels specified by

\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{p,t}=\bigg\{(T,K,\Lambda):T,K\subset[p],|T|<t,|K|\leq k,\text{ and }\Lambda:\{0,1\}^{l^{\prime}\cdot|K|}\rightarrow{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})\bigg\}

(168)

Where
\log\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\mu})=\mu
. A leakage query
(T,K,\Lambda)\in\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{p,t}
on a secret
m
is the density matrix:

(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}(m)_{T\cup K})

(169)

In their constructions
[
CKOS22
]
leverage secret sharing schemes augmented to satisfy a “local uniformity” property, where individual shares given out by the Share function are statistically close to the uniform distribution over the share space. To extend their construction to
k
local tampering, we require
k
wise independence:

Definition C.3
.

A randomized sharing function
\mathsf{Share}:\{0,1\}^{l}\rightarrow\{\{0,1\}^{l^{\prime}}\}^{p}
is
\varepsilon_{u}
-approximately
k
wise independent if for every message
m\in\{0,1\}^{l}
and subset
S\subset[p]
of size
k
:

\mathsf{Share}(m)_{S}\approx_{\varepsilon_{u}}U_{l^{\prime}}^{\otimes k}

(170)

We note that
(p,t)
Shamir Secret Sharing
[
Sha79
]
is exactly
(t-1)
wise independent.

C.1.2
Quantum Min Entropy and Randomness Extractors

Definition C.4

(Quantum conditional min-entropy)
.

Let
X,Y
be registers with state space
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}

and joint state
\rho
. We define the conditional min-entropy of
X
given
Y
as

H_{\infty}(X|Y)_{\rho}=-\log\min_{\sigma\in\mathcal{Y}}\{\min_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\lambda\cdot\mathbb{I}\otimes\sigma\geq\rho\}.

(171)

When
\rho
is a cq-state,
H_{\infty}(X|Y)_{\rho}
has an operational meaning in terms of the optimal guessing probability for
X
given
Y
. We remark that product states
\rho=\tau_{X}\otimes\sigma_{Y}
have conditional min entropy
H_{\infty}(X|Y)_{\rho}=H_{\infty}(X)_{\rho}=-\log\lambda_{max}(\tau)
equal to the log of the largest eigenvalue of
\tau_{X}
. When
\rho
is separable, it satisfies a Chain rule:

Lemma C.2

(Separable chain rule for quantum min-entropy
[
DD07
]
, Lemma 7)
.

Let
A,B,C
be registers with some joint, separable state
\rho=\sum_{i}\tau^{AB}_{i}\otimes\sigma^{C}_{i}
. Then,

H_{\infty}(A|B,C)_{\rho}\geq H_{\infty}(A|B)_{\rho}-\log|C|

(172)

Definition C.5

(Quantum-proof seeded extractor
[
DPVR12
]
)
.

A function
\mathsf{Ext}:\{0,1\}^{\eta}\times\{0,1\}^{d}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{l}
is said to be a
(\eta,\tau,d,l,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}})
-strong quantum-proof seeded extractor if for any cq-state
\rho\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{Y}
of the registers
X,Y
with
H_{\infty}(X|Y)_{\rho}\geq\tau
, we have

\mathsf{Ext}(X,S),Y,S\approx_{\varepsilon}U_{l},Y,S\text{ where }S\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{d}

(173)

Morover, if
\mathsf{Ext}(\cdot,s)
is a linear function for all
s\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, then
\mathsf{Ext}
is called a linear seeded extractor.

Lemma C.3

(
[
Tre01
,
DPVR12
]
)
.

There is an explicit
(\eta,\tau,d,l,\varepsilon)
-strong quantum-proof linear seeded extractor with
d=O(log^{3}(\eta/\varepsilon)
and
l=\tau-O(d)
.

We require the extractor to support efficient
pre-image sampling
. Given a seed
s
and some
y\in\{0,1\}^{l}
, the inverting function
\mathsf{IExt}
needs to sample an element uniformly from the set
\mathsf{Ext}(\cdot,s)^{-1}(y)={w:\mathsf{Ext}(w;s)=y}
.
[
CKOS22
]
showed that linear extractors always admit such sampling:

Lemma C.4

(
[
CKOS22
]
)
.

For every efficient linear extractor
\mathsf{Ext}
, there exists an efficient randomized function
\mathsf{IExt}:\{0,1\}^{l}\times\{0,1\}^{d}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\eta}\cup{\bot}
(termed inverter) such that

1.

U_{\eta},U_{d},\mathsf{Ext}(U_{\eta};U_{d})\equiv\mathsf{IExt}(\mathsf{Ext}(U_{\eta};U_{d}),U_{d}),U_{d},\mathsf{Ext}(U_{\eta};U_{d})

2.

For each
(s,y)\in\{0,1\}^{d}\times\{0,1\}^{l}
:

(a)

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{IExt}(y,s)=\bot]=1
, if and only if there exists no
w\in\{0,1\}^{\eta}
such that
\mathsf{Ext}(w;s)=y
.

(b)

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{Ext}(\mathsf{IExt}(y,s),s)=y]=1
, if there exists some
w\in\{0,1\}^{\eta}
such that
\mathsf{Ext}(w;s)=y

C.2
Code Construction

Our code construction uses essentially the same ingredients as
[
CKOS22
]
, with small modifications to the locality of their privacy parameters and to their compiler.

1.

(\mathsf{MShare},\mathsf{MRec})
, an
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},0)
threshold secret sharing scheme which is
(k,\varepsilon_{u})
-locally uniform over the message space
\{0,1\}^{l}
and with share size
l^{\prime}
.

2.

(\mathsf{SdShare},\mathsf{SdRec})
, an
(p,k+1,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime},0)
threshold secret sharing scheme over the message space
\{0,1\}^{d}
and share size
d^{\prime}
.

3.

\mathsf{Ext}
, a quantum-proof
(\eta,\tau\leq\eta-\mu,d,l^{\prime},\varepsilon_{ext})
-strong linear extractor. Let
\mathsf{IExt}
be the inverter function corresponding to
\mathsf{Ext}
given by
Lemma

C.4
.

Share
To share a message
m
, we begin by encoding it into
(M_{1},\cdots,M_{p})\leftarrow\mathsf{MShare}(m)
. Then, for each party
i\in[p]
, we sample a random seed
R_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, and then use
\mathsf{IExt}
to get the source
W_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R_{i})
. If any of the
W_{i}=\bot
rejects, output each of the share to be
(\bot,M_{i})
. Else, concatenate the randomness
R=(R_{1},\cdots,R_{p})
and share it using
\mathsf{SdShare}(R)
to get
(S_{1},\cdots,S_{p})
, and finally set the
i
-th share to be
(W_{i},S_{i})
.

Rec
Assuming the encoding doesn’t reject, an authorized party
T\subset[p]
of size
\geq t>k
begins by recovering the randomness
R=(R_{1},\cdots,R_{p})
using
\mathsf{SdRec}
on any
k+1
(honest) shares of
T
. Then, they recover the message shares
M_{T}=\{M_{i}:i\in T\}
by running the extractor on
(W_{i},R_{i})
for each
i\in T
. Finally, using
\mathsf{MRec}
on
M_{T}
it decodes the message
m
.

Theorem C.5
.

(\mathsf{Share},\mathsf{Rec})
defines a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}},0)
secret sharing scheme, which is
\leq 2(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime})+2p\cdot(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}+\varepsilon_{u})
leakage resilient against
\mathcal{F}_{k,\mu}^{p,t}
.

C.3
Analysis

The correctness and privacy of the scheme are inherited from that of
\mathsf{MShare},\mathsf{MRec}
. We analyze its rate in the next subsection
Section

C.3.1
, and dedicate the rest of this subsection to a proof of security.

Fix a leakage channel
(T,K,\Lambda)
and a message
m
. We assume
|T|\leq t-1
and
|K|\leq k
are both unauthorized subsets. We proceed in a sequence of hybrids, where within the encoding map
\mathsf{Share}
we replace the shares of
K
,
W_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R_{i})
, by a uniformly random source
W_{i}\leftarrow U
:

\mathsf{Share}^{0}(m)
: To share a message
m
, we simply encode it into
\mathsf{Share}(m)
.

\mathsf{Share}^{1}(m)
: To share a message
m
, we begin by encoding it into
(M_{1},\cdots,M_{p})\leftarrow\mathsf{MShare}(m)
. For each
i\in[n]
, sample a random seed
R_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
and use
\mathsf{IExt}
to get the source
W_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R_{i})
. Then, concatenate the randomness
R=(R_{1},\cdots,R_{p})
and share it using
\mathsf{SdShare}(R)
to get
(S_{1},\cdots,S_{p})
, and finally set the
i
-th share to be
(W_{i},S_{i})
.

\mathsf{Share}^{2}(m)
: To share a message
m
, we begin by encoding it into
(M_{1},\cdots,M_{p})\leftarrow\mathsf{MShare}(m)
. For each
i\in K
, sample a random seed
R_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
and source
W_{i}
. For each
i\in[p]\setminus K
, sample a random seed
R_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
and use
\mathsf{IExt}
to get the source
W_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R_{i})
. Then, concatenate the randomness
R=(R_{1},\cdots,R_{p})
and share it using
\mathsf{SdShare}(R)
to get
(S_{1},\cdots,S_{p})
, and finally set the
i
-th share to be
(W_{i},S_{i})
.

Note that
\mathsf{Share}^{0}
differs from
\mathsf{Share}^{1}
in that it conditions on the extractor pre-image sampling succeeding.
[
CKOS22
]
begin by proving that it succeeds with high probability, and thus
\mathsf{Share}(m)\approx\mathsf{Share}^{1}(m)
.

Claim C.6

(
[
CKOS22
]
)
.

For any message
m
,
\mathsf{Share}(m)=((\bot,M_{1}),\cdots,(\bot,M_{p}))
with probability
\leq p(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}+\varepsilon_{u})
.

Moreover, note that
\mathsf{Share}^{2}(m)
is completely independent of the shares
M_{i}:i\in K
, and thus the reduced density matrix
\mathsf{Share}^{2}_{T\cup K}
only depends on the shares of
M_{i}:i\in T
- where
|T|\leq t-1
. By the privacy of
\mathsf{MShare}
,

Claim C.7

(
[
CKOS22
]
)
.

For any pair of messages
m,m^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{Share}^{2}_{T\cup K}(m)\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}}\mathsf{Share}^{2}_{T\cup K}(m^{\prime})
.

It remains to argue that
\mathsf{Share}^{1}(m)
and
\mathsf{Share}^{2}(m)
are indistinguishable, given the shares in the unauthorized subset
T
and the leakage
L
.

Claim C.8
.

For any message
m
,

(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}^{1}_{T\cup K}(m))\approx_{\delta}(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}_{T\cup K}^{2}(m))

(174)

where
\delta\leq 2(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{u}+k\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}})
.

By the triangle inequality and the claims above, we conclude that for all
m,m^{\prime}
:

(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}_{T\cup K}(m))\approx_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}_{T\cup K}(m^{\prime}))

(175)

For some
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta+2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}+2\cdot p(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}+\varepsilon_{u})\leq 2(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime})+2p\cdot(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}+\varepsilon_{u})
, which is exactly
Theorem

C.5
. Now we prove
C.8
:

Proof.

[of
C.8
] Fix a message
m
. Consider the quantum-classical mixed state comprised of the classical shares
M_{K}=\{M_{i}:i\in K\}
of
\mathsf{MShare}
, the seed
R
and its shares
S_{K}=\{S_{i}:i\in K\}
, and the quantum leakage register
L
. Our goal will be to show that this cq density matrix is nearly independent of the “source” register in the shares of
K
. That is, if we denote as
W_{i},i\in K
as uniformly random sources on
\eta
bits, then it suffices to show that for some
\delta
,

\Lambda\big(\{S_{i},\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R)\}_{i\in K}\big),R,S_{K},M_{K}\approx_{\delta}\Lambda\big(\{S_{i},W_{i}\}_{i\in K}\big),R,S_{K},M_{K}.

(176)

This is since there is a CPTP map
\mathcal{N}^{m}
(dependent on the message) which given
R,S_{K},M_{K}
and the leakage
L
produces

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{m}(\Lambda\big(\{S_{i},\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R)\}_{i\in K}\big),R,S_{K},M_{K})=(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}^{1}_{T\cup K}(m))\text{ and }

(177)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{m}(\Lambda\big(\{S_{i},W_{i}\}_{i\in K}\big),R,S_{K},M_{K})=(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}^{2}_{T\cup K}(m)).

(178)

Note that
\mathcal{N}^{m}
simply samples shares
M_{T},S_{T}
consistent with
m,R
and
M_{K},S_{K}
.
22
22

22

This is also known as “consistent resampling”
[
CKOS22
]
Thereby by monotonicity of trace distance we obtain the desired
(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}^{1}_{T\cup K}(m))\approx_{\delta}(\mathbb{I}_{T}\otimes\Lambda_{K})(\mathsf{Share}_{T\cup K}^{2}(m))
.

It remains to show
Equation

176
. We begin by replacing the shares in
K
by fixed shares independent of
R
,
\hat{S}_{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{SdShare}(0^{d})_{K}
using the privacy of
\mathsf{SdShare}
, and leveraging the
\varepsilon_{u}
approximate
k
-wise independence of the shares
M_{K}
to replace them by the uniform distribution:

\displaystyle\Lambda\big(\{S_{i},\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R)\}_{i\in K}\big),R,S_{K},M_{K}\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime}}\Lambda\big(\{\hat{S}_{i},\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R)\}_{i\in K}\big),R,\hat{S}_{K},M_{K},\text{ and }

(179)

\displaystyle\Lambda\big(\{\hat{S}_{i},\mathsf{IExt}(M_{i},R)\}_{i\in K}\big),R,\hat{S}_{K},M_{K}\approx_{\varepsilon_{u}}\Lambda\big(\{\hat{S}_{i},\mathsf{IExt}(U_{l^{\prime}}^{i},R_{i})\}_{i\in K}\big),R,\hat{S}_{K},(U_{l^{\prime}})^{\otimes k}

(180)

Recall
W_{1},\cdots,W_{K}
are uniformly random
\eta
bit sources. By
Definition

C.5
, since
\mathsf{Ext}
is a strong linear seeded extractor,
\mathsf{Ext}(W_{i},R_{i})\approx_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}}U_{l^{\prime}}
, and moreover by

(b) from
[

]
we have
W_{i}=\mathsf{IExt}(\mathsf{Ext}(W_{i},R_{i}),R_{i})
. Thus,

\Lambda\big(\{\hat{S}_{i},\mathsf{IExt}(U_{l^{\prime}}^{i},R_{i})\}_{i\in K}\big),R,\hat{S}_{K},(U_{l^{\prime}})^{\otimes k}\approx_{k\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}}\Lambda\big(\{\hat{S}_{i},W_{i}\}_{i\in K}),R,\hat{S}_{K},\{\mathsf{Ext}(W_{i},R_{i})\}_{i\in K}

(181)

We now replace each
\mathsf{Ext}(W_{j},R_{j})
by
U_{l}
in a sequence of hybrids, evoking
quantum-proof
extractor security. This is the main modification to the proof of
[
CKOS22
]
: Fix
0\leq j\leq k
, and define the collection of classical registers

Z_{j}=R_{[n]\setminus\{j\}},\hat{S}_{K},(U_{l})^{\otimes(j-1)},\{\mathsf{Ext}(W_{i},R_{i})\}_{j<i\leq k}

Z_{j}

. If

(183)

Which implies through the triangle inequality,

\Lambda\big(\{\hat{S}_{i},W_{i}\}_{i\in K}),R,\hat{S}_{K},\{\mathsf{Ext}(W_{i},R_{i})\}_{i\in K}\approx_{k\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}}\Lambda\big(\{\hat{S}_{i},W_{i}\}_{i\in K}),R,\hat{S}_{K},U^{\otimes k}.

(185)

That is,
\delta\leq 2(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{u}+k\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}})
.

C.3.1
Parameters

We combine

1.

(\mathsf{MShare},\mathsf{MRec})
is a
(p,t,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}=0,\varepsilon_{c}=0)
-Shamir secret sharing scheme for
l
bit messages and
l
bit shares, which is perfectly
t-1
wise independent.

2.

We set
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}=2^{-\lambda^{-1/3}}
, and let
\mathsf{Ext}
be the
(\eta=l+\mu+O(d),\tau=l+O(d),d,l,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}})
quantum proof strong linear extractor guaranteed by
Lemma

C.3
, where
d=O(\log^{3}\frac{\eta}{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{Ext}}})=O(\lambda+\log^{3}(l+\mu))
.

3.

(\mathsf{SdShare},\mathsf{SdRec})
is a
(p,k+1,\varepsilon_{\mathsf{priv}}^{\prime}=0,\varepsilon^{\prime}_{c}=0)
-Shamir secret sharing scheme for
p\cdot d
bit messages and
p\cdot d
bit shares.

The resulting share size of
\mathsf{Share}
to handle
\mu
qubits of leakage is
p\cdot d+\eta=l+\mu+O(p\cdot\lambda+p\cdot\log^{3}(l+\mu))
, which is
l+\mu+o(l,\mu)
whenever
\lambda=o(\frac{l+\mu}{p})
and
p=O(\frac{l+\mu}{\log^{3}(l+\mu)})
.
